Evals should be grounded in real user problems, not abstract quality criteria. Someone has to talk to users, read support tickets, and understand what "failure" means in context. That's PM work.

Hamel Husain (top-grossing AI eval course on Maven, previously at GitHub):
"You're doing it wrong if you aren't looking at lots of data." And: "Remove ALL friction from looking at data."
The PM needs to be deeply embedded in the eval data — reading outputs, spotting patterns, understanding failure modes firsthand. Not waiting for a summary dashboard from engineering.

The convergence is striking. A CPO, a tool company, a model provider, and an independent practitioner — from different organizations, with different incentives — all saying the same thing: the person who understands the product's purpose must define how the product's quality is measured.

When competitors agree on something, pay attention. They disagree on everything else. But on "the PM should own evals"? Unanimous.

Why Engineering Agrees (When You Explain It)

You might worry that claiming eval ownership will create friction with engineering. In practice, the opposite happens. Most engineers are relieved when the PM takes ownership of eval rubrics and test case curation.

Engineers know they're not the right people to judge AI output quality. When an engineer writes an eval rubric, they have to make product judgment calls they're not confident about: "Is this tone formal enough? Is this level of detail right for the user?" These are uncomfortable questions for someone whose expertise is system design, not user empathy.

When you show up and say "I'll own the rubrics and test cases, you own the infrastructure," you're not creating a conflict. You're resolving one.

A Typical Conversation
PM: "I want to own the eval rubrics and test cases. I'll define what we're testing and what scores we need. Can you build me a runner that executes the suite and shows me results?"

Engineer: "Yes. Thank god. I've been making up scoring criteria and I know they're wrong. Can you also tell me which dimensions matter most?"

PM: "Absolutely. Accuracy is our top priority, then tone, then completeness. Safety is a hard gate — any failure is a block."

Engineer: "Perfect. I can have an automated runner in the CI pipeline by next week if you give me the test cases in a JSON file."

This is the ideal working relationship. Product judgment + engineering execution.

The Feedback Loop: The Heartbeat of AI Products

Evals don't exist in isolation. They're the center of a continuous improvement cycle — a feedback loop that runs for the entire life of the product. If the loop stops, the product stops improving. If the loop runs fast, the product improves fast.

Ship feature
→
Users interact
→
Collect outputs
→
Identify failures
→
Add to eval suite
→
Improve prompt/model
→
Run evals to verify
→
Ship again

Six of the eight steps involve product decisions. Engineering owns infrastructure and implementation. The PM owns judgment and direction. The eval suite bridges the two.

The faster this loop runs, the faster your product improves. A team that runs this loop weekly will improve 4x faster than a team that runs it monthly. A team that runs it daily — possible with automated evals — will outpace competitors who run it manually on a sprint cadence.

The Loop Speed Advantage
Team A runs the improvement loop monthly: 12 prompt improvements per year, each verified by evals.

Team B runs it weekly: 52 per year.

Even if each individual improvement is small (a 1-2% gain), the compounding effect is dramatic. 52 small improvements, verified and not regressing, will demolish 12 improvements. This is the quiet competitive advantage of eval-driven AI products.
Real Example — How This Works at Duolingo
Duolingo runs this loop weekly for their AI language tutor:

  1. Ship: Deploy a prompt change to make grammar explanations clearer for intermediate Spanish learners.
  2. Users interact: Millions of students use the AI tutor. The system logs every AI explanation and every student response.
  3. Collect: Automated pipeline stores AI responses alongside student behavior signals: did they ask a follow-up? Did they get the next practice question right?
  4. Identify failures: The PM reviews 50 cases where students asked confused follow-up questions. Pattern: the AI uses terms like "subjunctive mood" that intermediate learners haven't been taught yet.
  5. Add to eval suite: PM creates 15 test cases with a new rubric criterion: "Uses no grammar terminology without first explaining it in simple language."
  6. Improve prompt: Add to system prompt: "When explaining grammar to intermediate learners, never use terms like 'subjunctive' or 'gerund' without first explaining them in simple language."
  7. Run evals: All 15 new test cases pass. All 200 existing cases still pass. Clarity score: 3.2 → 4.1.
  8. Ship. Follow-up question rate drops 23%.

Every step that involved a decision required product context. The engineer built the pipeline. The PM made the decisions that actually moved quality.

Evals vs. Vibes: What the Difference Looks Like

Same product, same team, same model. The only difference is whether the PM owns a systematic eval process:

Vibes-Driven (No Eval Ownership)

Monday: PM tries 5 test prompts. "Looks good to me."

Tuesday: Ships prompt change based on gut feeling.

Wednesday: User complains about wrong answers. PM: "Weird, it worked when I tested it."

Thursday: 3 more complaints. PM asks engineering: "Did something change?" Engineering: "No."

Friday: CEO asks if AI is improving. PM: "We think so?"

Next week: More complaints. Different problem. Nobody connects it to Tuesday's change. The regression lives in production for 6 weeks.

Eval-Driven (PM Owns Evals)

Monday: PM reviews 30 recent AI outputs. Identifies 4 with tone issues. Adds to eval suite.

Tuesday: PM modifies prompt. Runs eval suite (150 test cases, 4 dimensions). Accuracy: +3%. Tone: +8%. No regression.

Wednesday: Ships with confidence. Dashboard shows: "v2.3.1 — accuracy 87%, tone 82%, completeness 91%, safety 100%."

Thursday: User complaint comes in. PM checks: it's a known edge case, already in the eval suite as a "fail." Prioritized for next sprint.

Friday: CEO asks if AI is improving. PM shows trendline: "Accuracy up from 72% to 87% over 8 weeks. Here's the chart."

Same team. Same product. Same model. Completely different outcomes. The difference is a PM who owns a spreadsheet with 150 test cases and runs it on every change. That's not a $100K eval platform. It's one PM, one spreadsheet, and the discipline to run it consistently.

The Eval Maturity Gap: Your Opportunity

Let's revisit those survey numbers from the introduction:

The Numbers Tell A Story
57% of organizations have AI agents in production.
Only 37% run systematic evaluations on those agents.

That means roughly 35% of all organizations are shipping AI products with no systematic quality measurement. Among the 37% who DO run evals, most run them ad-hoc, evaluate only the final output, and have no regression testing. The number with a mature, comprehensive eval practice is probably under 10%.

Source: LangChain "State of AI Agents" survey, 2025. 1,300+ respondents.

This gap exists for a predictable reason: nobody owns it. Engineering thinks it's a PM problem. The PM thinks it's an engineering problem. And so it doesn't get done.

Here's the maturity landscape:

Level 0 — No Evals
Flying Blind
No systematic quality measurement. AI quality assessed by "trying a few prompts" before shipping. This is where ~35% of companies with AI in production currently sit.
Where most teams start
Level 1 — Manual Evals
Spreadsheet Era
PM has a spreadsheet with 10-50 test cases. Runs them manually before deployments. Basic quality dimensions defined. Catches some regressions.
1 day to achieve from Level 0
Level 2 — Automated Evals
CI/CD Integration
Eval suite runs automatically on every prompt change. 50-200 test cases. LLM-as-judge for fuzzy criteria. Results visible in a dashboard. Regression detection is reliable.
2-4 weeks from Level 0 with engineering support
Level 3 — Production Monitoring
Live Quality Signal
Quality is monitored on live production traffic. Real user interactions sampled and scored. User feedback correlated with eval scores. Failures automatically routed to the eval suite.
Top 5% of AI product teams
Level 4 — Closed Loop
Self-Improving
Evals drive product decisions automatically. Low scores trigger prompt experiments. A/B tests run on production traffic. The eval suite itself is evaluated and improved.
Top 1% of AI product teams

The biggest ROI is the jump from Level 0 to Level 1. That's a single week of work (the playbook later in this chapter) and it transforms your relationship with AI quality from "I have no idea" to "I have data."

The PM who steps into this gap creates disproportionate leverage. You become the person who can answer the CEO's question: "Is the AI getting better or worse?" You become the person who turns vague user complaints into specific, measurable quality improvements. That transformation is visible, tangible, and rare enough to be career-defining.

The Career Argument
The AI PM talent pool is thin. Many PMs can write a PRD and run a sprint. Very few can build and maintain an eval suite, interpret the results, and use them to drive product decisions systematically. That skill gap is a differentiation opportunity.

When you interview for an AI PM role and say: "I built an eval suite of 200 test cases across 4 quality dimensions. I improved our accuracy score from 72% to 89% while maintaining tone quality scores. User-reported AI errors dropped 40% over 3 months. I can show you the trend lines." — you're not competing with other PMs. You're in a category of one.

Chapter 26 covers the career angle in detail.

What PM Ownership Actually Looks Like

"PM owns evals" doesn't mean the PM personally writes every test case, builds the eval runner, and runs every suite. Here's the realistic division of labor:

ActivityOwnerWhy This Person
Define quality dimensions and weights PM Product strategy decision — what the product values
Write rubrics and scoring criteria PM Defines what "good" means for users of this product
Curate test cases from user failures PM Requires understanding user needs and failure impact
Build eval runner and automation Engineering Infrastructure, CI/CD integration, tooling
Build data collection and logging Engineering Pipelines, storage, instrumentation
Interpret eval results and decide action PM Product judgment — is this good enough to ship?
Prioritize which failures to fix PM Product prioritization — what matters most to users/business
Iterate on prompts based on results PM + Eng Collaborative — PM sets direction, eng implements
Maintain and expand test case library PM Ongoing quality ownership as product and users evolve

The pattern: the PM owns everything that requires judgment about what matters for the product. Engineering owns everything that requires building reliable systems. The "requirements" aren't a static PRD — they're a living eval suite that evolves continuously, expressed as automated tests rather than bullet points.

What Happens When the PM Doesn't Own Evals

The engineering team, being responsible and thorough, builds an eval system. It's reliable, fast, and integrated into CI/CD. They test for the things engineers naturally test for:

  • Format compliance: Does the JSON parse? Are the expected fields present?
  • Latency: Does the response come back within 3 seconds at P95?
  • Basic correctness: Does the response mention the entities from the query?
  • Length bounds: Is the response between 50 and 500 tokens?
  • Consistency: Does the same input produce roughly the same structure each time?

All valid things to test. The evals all pass. The team ships with confidence. Within the first month, user complaints start rolling in — and they don't map to any eval dimension:

User ComplaintEval Suite CoverageReal Problem
"The answers feel robotic and cold." Not tested Tone is wrong for the brand.
"Responses are too long for simple questions." Partially tested Max length is checked (500 tokens), but appropriate length isn't.
"The AI hedges on everything." Not tested Calibration — the AI says "it depends" to questions with clear answers.
"Every answer sounds the same." Not tested Specificity — the AI gives generic boilerplate instead of tailored responses.
"The AI recommended a competitor!" Not tested Brand safety. Engineers don't think about competitive positioning.

The engineering evals all pass. The product is failing. This is technically correct and product-wrong.

The Measurement Trap
Goodhart's Law: "When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure."

When engineering defines the metrics, the product optimizes for engineering metrics. Latency improves. Error rates drop. Format compliance reaches 100%. The dashboard turns green. Meanwhile, users are getting responses that are fast, well-formatted, and completely unhelpful. The metrics are improving. The product is getting worse.

This is the measurement trap. Choosing what to measure is the most consequential product decision you'll make in AI, because the product will relentlessly optimize for whatever you measure.

The Objection You'll Hear (and How to Handle It)

The pushback you'll almost certainly encounter: "But the PM doesn't know how to code evals."

First, modern tooling has largely eliminated the coding requirement. Platforms like Braintrust, Promptfoo, and LangSmith have GUIs where you type in test cases, define rubrics in plain English, and click Run.

Second, the hard part was never the code. Running a Python script is easy. The hard part is knowing what to test, how to score it, and what the results mean for the product. That's the PM's unique contribution.

Third, the PM who can code evals is even more powerful. The combination of product judgment AND technical execution is extremely rare. But it's a nice-to-have, not a prerequisite.

A Common Trap: The PM Who Over-Indexes on Infrastructure
Some PMs hear "evals are your job" and respond by diving deep into tooling. They spend weeks evaluating eval platforms, building custom pipelines, and creating dashboards. By the time the infrastructure is perfect, they've spent a month and still haven't looked at actual AI outputs.

Your job is not to build eval infrastructure — that's engineering's job. Your job is to:
  1. Define what "good" looks like (write the rubrics)
  2. Curate the test cases (from real user failures and edge cases)
  3. Interpret the results (and make product decisions based on them)
  4. Continuously expand the suite (as you learn more about how the product fails)
If you're spending more than 20% of your eval time on infrastructure, you're doing the wrong work. A spreadsheet with 30 carefully curated test cases and thoughtful rubrics beats a $50K eval platform with 5 hastily written test cases every time.

Your First Week: The Comprehensive Playbook

If you're reading this and thinking "evals at my company are currently owned by nobody, or by engineering, and I want to change that" — here's your day-by-day plan. It is not theoretical advice. It is a literal schedule. Follow it.

But first, a principle. Before you build any evaluation infrastructure — rubrics, grading pipelines, automated tests — you need observation. Deep, unstructured, deliberate observation of how the agent actually behaves with real users on real inputs. Without this, you will build evals that measure the wrong things.

Resist the Urge to Fix
During this week, you will see problems you immediately know how to fix. A better prompt here, a guardrail there. Write them down, but do not act on them yet. Premature fixes address symptoms, not root causes. You are gathering evidence. The fixes come after you have the full picture.

Days 1–2: Read 30 Production Traces End-to-End

Block four hours each day. No meetings during this time. Tell your team you are "doing eval research" and will be unavailable.

A trace is the complete record of what happened during one agent execution: every LLM call, every tool invocation, every response. It might be a log file, a JSON message array, a visualization in LangSmith or Braintrust, or raw stdout. If your system does not produce traces, making them available is your first engineering request. You cannot evaluate what you cannot observe.

How to select your 30 traces. Do not just grab the 30 most recent. Curate a sample that covers the full range of behavior:

  • 10 "normal" traces: Typical inputs, typical outcomes. Select at random from your most common use case.
  • 10 "varied" traces: Different input types, different user intents, different complexity levels.
  • 5 "failure" traces: The user abandoned, regenerated, or complained. Most informative because they show failure modes directly.
  • 5 "excellent" traces: Especially good output. You need to understand excellence to define it in a rubric later.

How to read each trace. Follow the execution path:

User input → First tool call → Tool result → Agent reasoning → Next tool call → ... → Final output

At each node, ask three questions:

  1. Was this step reasonable? Given what the agent knew at this point, does the choice make sense?
  2. Did quality drop here? Is the output of this step worse than the input deserved?
  3. Would a domain expert do this differently?

What to look for:

Signal What It Looks Like in the Trace What It Tells You
Quality drops Step N's output is clearly worse than step N-1's input warranted This step has a quality problem worth evaluating
User abandonment User disconnects, navigates away, or regenerates mid-stream Too slow, too wrong, or too confusing at this point
Wasted work Agent calls tools whose results are never used Inefficiency that increases cost and latency without improving quality
Error cascades An early mistake propagates through multiple downstream steps Validates cascade hypotheses from your prioritization
Surprising successes Unexpected path, excellent results "Grade outcomes, not paths" — this path should not be penalized

How to take notes. Freeform. Do not use a template. Do not categorize yet. Just write down every observation as it comes to you. This is what Husain calls "open coding" — a technique from grounded theory research where you observe without predetermined categories and let patterns emerge from the data.

Sample Notes from Day 1 (Creative Campaign Agent)
Trace 1 (Nike competitor, sportswear): Research solid. Hooks good but #4 (FOMO) feels generic. Images OK but all look like stock photos.
Trace 2 (Small candle brand): Research grabbed wrong company — found a candle manufacturer, not the artisan brand. Everything downstream wrong. User abandoned after seeing hooks.
Trace 3 (SaaS product): Research OK. Hooks are all about features, none about outcomes. Images are abstract gradients — no clear connection to the product.
Trace 4 (Luxury skincare): Research was thorough. Hooks excellent. But images look cheap — wrong aesthetic for luxury brand. User regenerated images 3x.
Trace 5 (Restaurant chain): Good overall! One hook (#2, story hook) referenced a founding story that does not exist. Hallucination.

Five traces, five different observations. Already you can see patterns forming: research accuracy is critical, image aesthetic matching is recurring, hallucination is a risk. After 30 traces, these observations become your failure taxonomy.

Why 30? The number comes from qualitative research methodology. Domain experts typically reach theoretical saturation — the point where new traces stop revealing new failure modes — between 20 and 50 traces. Thirty is the sweet spot. If traces 25-30 feel like repetitions of patterns you already noted, you have enough. If you're still discovering new failure types at trace 30, keep going.

As you read, make a gut judgment on each trace: good, fine, or bad. Note the bad ones and write one sentence about why. By the end of Day 2, start looking at your "bad" pile and grouping failures into 3-5 categories: "too generic," "wrong tone," "hallucinated a fact," "too long," "didn't answer the actual question." These categories are your quality dimensions. Name them.

Set up a simple trace viewer. If reading traces means SSHing into a server and parsing JSON files, you will do it once and never again. If your agent logs to a tracing platform (LangSmith, Braintrust, Arize Phoenix), use their UI. If it logs to files, write a simple script that formats the last N traces readably. If you have no logging at all, that is your first engineering request.

Day 3: Shadow 10 Real User Sessions

Traces tell you what happened. Sessions tell you how it felt.

A trace might show the agent completed successfully in 4 minutes. But if you watch the user, you see them fidgeting, switching tabs, checking their phone. The trace said "success." The user said "too slow."

How to shadow. Choose one method:

  • Session recordings (FullStory, Hotjar, LogRocket): Watch 10 recordings. Best for async observation.
  • Live shadowing: Sit next to a user and watch. Ask them to think aloud. Best for understanding expectations.
  • Dogfooding: Use the agent yourself as a real user. Best when you have no recordings yet. Not as good as watching real users, but infinitely better than not observing.

For each session, record:

What You Observed Why It Matters
What the user expected to happen Defines "good" from the user's perspective
What actually happened Defines the current state of quality
The gap between expected and actual This gap is where your evals will live.
Moments of delight (user says "wow," leans in, shares with someone) What to protect. Do not regress on what works.
Moments of frustration (user sighs, regenerates, abandons) What to fix. Highest-priority eval targets.
The Gap Is the Eval
The gap between what the user expected and what the agent delivered is precisely what your evals should measure. If the user expected on-brand luxury images and got stock-photo-looking images, your eval should measure "brand aesthetic alignment." If the user expected the agent to remember their previous feedback, your eval should measure "context retention across follow-ups." The gap defines the eval. Every time.

Common expectation gaps that appear across AI products:

User Expectation Common Agent Reality The Gap (Potential Eval)
"It remembers what I said earlier" Context window fills up; earlier instructions get lost Context retention over long conversations
"It understands my brand / my business" Surface-level research; misses nuance of positioning Depth and accuracy of brand understanding
"It should know when it does not know" Confident answers even when uncertain; hallucinations Calibration — does confidence match correctness?
"It should improve when I give feedback" Follow-ups sometimes make things worse or ignore feedback Follow-up quality — did the revision address specific feedback?
"It should be consistent" Same input produces wildly different quality across runs Output variance — standard deviation of quality scores

Shadowing reveals which of these gaps matter most for your specific users. Maybe your users don't care about speed. Maybe consistency matters more than peak quality. Only observation tells you.

Day 4: Talk to the Engineering Team and Write Your First Test Cases

You now have 30 traces of observations and 10 sessions of user insights. The engineers fill in the third piece: what the agent was supposed to do, and where it is known to fall short.

Schedule 30-60 minutes. Ask these questions in this order:

  1. "What are the known quality problems?" — Engineers always know. They have a mental list. This gives them permission to share it.
  2. "What is the most common user complaint?" — Cross-reference with your trace and session observations.
  3. "Where does the agent struggle most?" — The difficult cases, unusual inputs, adversarial users.
  4. "What would you fix if you had a week?" — Reveals engineering priorities. Are they aligned with yours?
  5. "Have you tried to measure quality before? What happened?" — Reveals past eval attempts and why they failed.
  6. "Are there parts of the system that nobody monitors?" — The most dangerous failures are the ones nobody is watching.

Frame the conversation around their frustrations, not your evaluation agenda. "What annoys you about the agent's behavior?" gets better answers than "What are the quality problems I should be measuring?"

Map their answers to your architecture. For each problem identified, find the decision point where that problem originates. You now have a blueprint annotated with three types of evidence — your trace observations, your session observations, and engineering's institutional knowledge.

Example: Annotated Blueprint (Creative Campaign Agent)
[STEP 1] Research the brand
  [ENG] "Sometimes grabs wrong company when name is ambiguous"
  [TRACE] Trace 2: Wrong company. Trace 14: Shallow research on niche brand.

[STEP 2] Write 6 ad hooks
  [ENG] "Hooks can sound generic — not enough brand differentiation"
  [TRACE] Trace 3: Feature-focused, no outcomes. Trace 5: Hallucinated founding story.

[STEP 3] Art direction
  [ENG] "Hard to match brand aesthetic without explicit style guide"

[STEP 4] Image generation
  [ENG] "Would fix image-text alignment if had a week"
  [TRACE] Trace 1: Stock photo look. Trace 4: Wrong aesthetic for luxury.
Triangulation Is the Point
Any one source can mislead you. Traces can make a rare problem look common if your sample was unlucky. User sessions over-index on vocal users. Engineers have blind spots about problems they've normalized. But when all three sources point to the same problem, you have high-confidence signal. Start there.

Then write your first 10 test cases. In the afternoon, pick the 10 worst outputs from your trace review. For each one, write: (a) the input, (b) what the AI actually produced, (c) what it should have produced, and (d) which quality dimension(s) it failed on. Put it in a spreadsheet. You now have your first eval dataset.

Day 5: Score Your Test Cases, Write the State of Quality, Claim Ownership

Today you synthesize the week into two concrete artifacts and make your move.

Morning: score your test cases. Write a 2-sentence definition for each of your quality dimensions. Score your 10 test cases on each dimension using pass/fail (or a 1-5 scale — either works to start). Here's what a first-week snapshot might look like:

  Your First Eval Snapshot (Day 5)
  =================================

  Quality Dimensions Found:
    1. Accuracy    — Is the information factually correct?
    2. Specificity — Is the answer specific to the user's question,
                     or could it answer any question? (biggest pattern)
    3. Tone        — Does it match our brand voice?

  Test Cases: 10
    +----+-----------+-----------+-------+-----------------------------+
    | #  | Accuracy  | Specific. | Tone  | Notes                       |
    +----+-----------+-----------+-------+-----------------------------+
    |  1 | PASS     | FAIL     | PASS  | Generic -- could be any brand|
    |  2 | FAIL     | PASS     | PASS  | Wrong founding year          |
    |  3 | PASS     | FAIL     | FAIL  | Generic + too formal         |
    |  4 | PASS     | PASS     | PASS  | Good example -- use as golden|
    |  5 | PASS     | FAIL     | PASS  | Boilerplate response         |
    | ...|           |           |       |                             |
    +----+-----------+-----------+-------+-----------------------------+

  Current Scores:
    Accuracy:    80% pass (8/10)
    Specificity: 50% pass (5/10)  ← biggest problem
    Tone:        70% pass (7/10)

Midday: write the State of Quality document. One page. This document crystallizes your understanding, creates alignment with the team, and sets the baseline you'll measure improvements against.

STATE OF QUALITY
Product: [Name]
Date: [Today]
Author: [You]
Based on: 30 traces, 10 user sessions, engineering interviews

TOP 3 JOBS
1. [Job name] — Estimated quality: [1-5 gut feel]
2. [Job name] — Estimated quality: [1-5 gut feel]
3. [Job name] — Estimated quality: [1-5 gut feel]

TOP 5 OBSERVED FAILURES
1. [Description] — Seen in [X] of 30 traces — Severity: [high/med/low]
2. [Description] — Seen in [X] of 30 traces — Severity: [high/med/low]
3. [Description] — Seen in [X] of 30 traces — Severity: [high/med/low]
4. [Description] — Seen in [X] of 30 traces — Severity: [high/med/low]
5. [Description] — Seen in [X] of 30 traces — Severity: [high/med/low]

BIGGEST RISK
[One sentence: What is the worst thing that could happen to a user?]

RECOMMENDED FIRST EVAL TARGET
[The job + specific failure mode you will build your first eval around]

OPEN QUESTIONS
- [Things you could not determine this week]
- [Areas where you need more data]
Example — Creative Campaign Agent State of Quality
Product: Creative Campaign Agent
Date: March 31, 2026
Based on: 30 traces, 10 user sessions, 2 engineer interviews

TOP 3 JOBS
1. Research brand accurately — Quality: 2.5/5 (high variance; great on well-known brands, poor on niche/ambiguous brands)
2. Write compelling hooks — Quality: 3.5/5 (usually solid, but tend toward generic when research is weak)
3. Generate on-brand images — Quality: 2/5 (frequent aesthetic mismatch, stock-photo feel for premium brands)

TOP 5 OBSERVED FAILURES
1. Wrong company researched (ambiguous brand name) — 4/30 traces — Severity: high
2. Image aesthetic does not match brand positioning — 9/30 traces — Severity: medium
3. Hooks sound generic / not differentiated from competitors — 7/30 traces — Severity: medium
4. Hallucinated facts in hooks (founding story, statistics) — 3/30 traces — Severity: high
5. Follow-up ignores previous context — 2/10 follow-up traces — Severity: medium

BIGGEST RISK: Agent confidently produces a full campaign about the wrong company, user does not notice until they show it to their client.

RECOMMENDED FIRST EVAL: Research accuracy — specifically, "did the agent identify the correct company and its actual products/positioning?"

OPEN QUESTIONS:
• How well does research handle brands with no English web presence?
• Is image quality limited by the image model, or by the prompts we generate?
• Do users actually care about hook variety, or would 6 versions of their favorite hook type be better?

The gut-feel scores will feel wrong. That discomfort is normal. The point is not precision — it's direction. You're saying "research quality is somewhere in the mediocre range." Real eval scores in Phase 2 will replace these estimates.

Send the State of Quality to: the engineering team (they'll validate or correct your observations), your manager (they need the baseline before you propose investments), and any PM or designer who touches the product. This creates alignment before you build anything. When you come back with a proposal to build evals, nobody will be surprised by the problems you're solving.

What Happens When You Skip Alignment
A PM spends three weeks building an eval suite for "response factual accuracy." They present the results. The engineering lead says: "We already know accuracy is a problem. We needed you to figure out whether the tone is right — that is what users keep escalating about." Three weeks wasted, not because the eval was bad, but because it measured the wrong thing. The State of Quality document prevents this.

Afternoon: claim the ownership. In your team standup or sprint planning, say something like: "I've started building our eval suite. I reviewed 30 recent traces and 10 user sessions, and found these patterns: [list your dimensions]. Here are our worst 10 cases. I'm going to expand this to 30 test cases next week and start running them on every prompt change. I'll need engineering support to automate the runner once we're past 50 cases."

Show the eval snapshot and State of Quality to your engineering lead and your manager. You now have credibility, data, and a conversation starter.

The Week at a Glance

Day Activity Time Output
1 Read 15 production traces, take freeform notes 4 hrs 15 annotated traces with observations
2 Read 15 more traces, group failures into quality dimensions 4 hrs 30 annotated traces, 3-5 named quality dimensions
3 Shadow 10 user sessions, note expectation gaps 3–5 hrs 10 session notes with expected vs. actual
4 Interview engineers, map to blueprint, write 10 test cases 3–4 hrs Annotated blueprint, first eval spreadsheet
5 Score test cases, write State of Quality, claim ownership 3–4 hrs Eval snapshot, 1-page quality baseline, team alignment

Total time: about 17-21 hours across the week. This is not extra work on top of your job. This is the job. Understanding your product's quality is the core responsibility of an AI PM.

What If You Don't Have Production Data Yet?

If your agent is pre-launch, adapt the playbook:

  • Days 1-2: Generate 30 traces yourself using synthetic but realistic inputs. Cover the range you expect in production. If you're building a creative campaign agent, use 10 well-known brands, 10 small/niche brands, and 10 edge cases (brands with ambiguous names, non-English brands, brands with minimal web presence).
  • Day 3: Run a beta session with 5-10 internal users or beta testers. Give them real tasks, not toy examples. "Create an ad campaign for your favorite local restaurant" is better than "Create an ad campaign for Nike" because it tests inputs the team hasn't optimized for.
  • Days 4-5: Same as above, but label your quality scores as "pre-launch estimates."

The pre-launch version is less informative (synthetic traces lack the diversity of real user behavior) but infinitely better than skipping it entirely.

Do Not Skip This Week
PMs who skip observation and jump straight to building eval infrastructure measure the wrong things with expensive precision. They build beautiful dashboards that track metrics nobody cares about. They write rubrics that miss the actual failure modes.

How do you know if you skipped this step? You find yourself asking: "What should I measure?" If you have to ask, you have not observed enough. After 30 traces and 10 sessions, the answer will be obvious. The data tells you what to measure. You just have to look at it.

There is a second anti-pattern worth naming: the dashboard trap. This is when a PM inherits an existing eval dashboard and starts optimizing whatever metrics are already being tracked, without questioning whether those are the right metrics. The dashboard says "average response time: 2.3s" and "tool call success rate: 94%," so the PM works on those numbers. But maybe response time doesn't matter to users, and the 6% tool call failure rate is concentrated on the most important use case. Inherited metrics are hypotheses, not truths. Validate them against your observation week before accepting them.

The PM's Eval Cadence

Once you own evals, what does the ongoing practice look like? About 3-4 hours per week — roughly 10% of your time:

  Weekly Eval Cadence (3-4 hours/week)
  ====================================

  Monday    Review outputs (1 hour)
            Read 30-50 recent AI outputs. Flag the bad ones.
            Note patterns. This is your most important hour.

  Tuesday   Update suite (30 min)
            Add 2-3 new test cases from Monday's review.
            Write or refine rubric criteria if needed.

  Wed-Thu   Run evals on any prompt changes (30 min per change)
            Every prompt change gets a full suite run.
            Review results. Ship or revert.

  Friday    Weekly quality snapshot (30 min)
            Run the full suite on current production prompt.
            Log the scores. Track the trendline.
            Share the snapshot with the team.

  Monthly
  ------------------------------------
  Deep review (2 hours): Read 200+ outputs. Look for new
  failure patterns. Challenge existing rubric weights.
  Are you still measuring the right things?

This cadence seems modest. That's intentional. The biggest risk with eval ownership isn't underinvesting — it's overcomplicating. A simple, consistent weekly rhythm beats an elaborate system that you abandon after two weeks.

The cadence scales naturally. In month 1, you're reviewing outputs manually and adding test cases to a spreadsheet. By month 3, automation makes sense. By month 6, evals run in CI/CD, and your Monday morning review focuses on production outputs the automated system flagged.

The Compound Effect
If you add 3 test cases per week, in one year you'll have 156 test cases. Each one represents a specific failure mode you've caught and permanently prevented. Over time, your eval suite becomes a comprehensive map of everything that can go wrong with your AI product — and the automated assurance that none of those things are going wrong right now.

This compound effect is why starting early matters. A PM who started six months ago has 75+ test cases, deep pattern recognition, and a quality trendline to show leadership. Start today.

Getting Buy-In: Speaking to Different Audiences

When you present your eval plan, different stakeholders care about different things:

Framing by Audience
To your CEO/CPO:
"Right now, we can't answer 'is the AI getting better or worse?' I'm building a measurement system that will give us a quality score we can track over time. Within two weeks, I'll have trendlines for accuracy, tone, and user-relevant quality metrics."

To your engineering lead:
"I'll own the rubrics and test case curation. You own the infrastructure. I need a runner that takes a JSON file of test cases, sends each one through the model, and returns the outputs. Can you build that in a sprint?"

To your design/UX lead:
"I'm documenting the specific ways our AI output fails users. Some of it is accuracy, which is model-side. But some of it is presentation. I want your input on the 'taste' and 'presentation' dimensions of the eval rubric."

To your team in sprint planning:
"I've reviewed 30 recent traces and 10 user sessions. Here are the top 3 failure patterns and their frequency. I've added 10 test cases for each pattern. Here's our baseline score. I'm proposing we dedicate 20% of this sprint to the #1 failure pattern."

In each case, you're saying the same thing: "I'm building an eval system." But framing it in terms of what each person cares about. Note what you're NOT saying: "We need to buy an eval platform" (don't lead with tooling). "Engineering needs to build a testing framework" (don't make it sound like their problem). You're saying: "I've started. Here's what I found. Here's what I need from you." Lead with results, not requests.

What "Great" Looks Like: Three Companies

Example 1 — Early-Stage SaaS (AI Customer Support, 15 Employees)
PM's eval setup: One Google Sheet with 85 test cases. Three quality dimensions: accuracy, tone, completeness. The PM updates weekly by adding 3-5 cases from user complaints.

Process: Before any prompt change, the PM runs all 85 inputs through the model using a simple Python script. The PM manually reviews outputs against the rubric. Takes about 90 minutes. If accuracy stays above 85% and no safety failures, the change ships.

Results after 3 months: Accuracy: "nobody knows" → measurable 88%. User-reported AI errors dropped 35%. PM can show investors a quality trendline.

Total ongoing investment: ~3 hours/week of PM time.
Example 2 — Growth-Stage Startup (AI Content Generation, 80 Employees)
PM's eval setup: Braintrust account with 350 test cases across 6 quality dimensions. LLM-as-judge scores each output 1-5 per dimension. Runs automatically in CI/CD on every prompt-changing PR.

Process: Monday mornings: review 50 real production outputs. Sprint planning includes "eval improvement" as a standing item. Weekly quality snapshot to leadership.

Results after 6 months: Quality scores improved across every dimension. Time to ship a prompt change: 3 days → 2 hours. Company uses eval scores in marketing: "91% accuracy on independent evaluation."

Total ongoing investment: ~5 hours/week of PM time + engineering maintains CI/CD integration.
Example 3 — Enterprise Product (AI Legal Assistant, 500 Employees)
PM's eval setup: Custom platform with 1,200+ test cases. Multiple rubrics for different use cases. Human reviewers (legal domain experts) grade a sample of production outputs weekly. LLM-as-judge handles automated scoring.

Process: Every model version, prompt change, and knowledge base update triggers a full suite run. Weekly quality reviews with PM, engineering, and domain experts. Monthly reports to compliance and the board.

Results: Never shipped a regression that affected users. 18 months of quality trendlines showing continuous improvement. Eval system is a competitive moat for risk-averse enterprise customers.

Total ongoing investment: ~10 hours/week of PM time + 0.5 FTE eval ops + quarterly domain expert reviews.

Three scales. Three investment levels. Same pattern: the PM owns the definition of quality, the test case curation, the rubric design, and the interpretation of results. Engineering owns the infrastructure.

How Evals Connect to Every Other PM Artifact

PM ArtifactHow Evals Connect
PRD / Product Spec Your eval rubric IS the spec, expressed as automated tests. When stakeholders ask "what does 'good' look like?" — show them the rubric.
Sprint Planning Eval results drive the backlog. "Tone score dropped 5 points this week" is a sprint item.
Stakeholder Reviews Replace "the AI seems better" with "accuracy improved from 78% to 86% this quarter, zero safety failures."
Model Migration Run your eval suite on both models. "The new model scores 3 points higher on accuracy but 2 points lower on conciseness."
Competitive Analysis Run your eval suite on a competitor's product. Now you know your moat.
User Research User complaints map to eval failures. "Users say the AI is too verbose" maps to a low conciseness score.

Evals aren't extra work on top of your PM work. They ARE your PM work, expressed in a format that's automated, measurable, and actionable.

Common Anti-Patterns (What NOT to Do)

Anti-Pattern 1: "We'll Add Evals Later"
The team ships the AI feature first, plans to add evals "once things stabilize." Things never stabilize. Evals stay on the roadmap forever. The AI degrades silently for months.

The fix: Build 10 test cases before you ship. Not after. Not in the next sprint. Before.
Anti-Pattern 2: "Our Users Are Our Eval"
The team relies on user feedback to identify quality problems. This is like using customers as your QA department. By the time they complain, they've already had a bad experience and considered switching.

The fix: Catch failures before users do. That's the entire point.
Anti-Pattern 3: "We Have Unit Tests, So We Have Evals"
The engineering team has unit tests for API status codes, JSON parsing, and model name. These are infrastructure tests, not quality evals. Passing all unit tests while shipping terrible AI outputs is the norm.

The fix: Distinguish infrastructure tests (engineering owns) from quality evals (PM owns). You need both.
Anti-Pattern 4: "We Do Manual QA Before Every Release"
The PM manually tests 10-20 prompts before each deployment. Three fatal flaws: not repeatable (different things each time), no regression detection, doesn't scale.

The fix: Turn manual test prompts into automated test cases. Run ALL of them on every change.
Anti-Pattern 5: "Our Eval Suite Is Set and Forget"
A PM builds 50 test cases, integrates them into CI/CD, and moves on. Six months later, the product has 3 new features and the failure modes have evolved. The suite is testing stale patterns.

The fix: The eval suite is a living document. Add 2-5 new test cases every week. Remove irrelevant ones. Update rubrics as the product strategy evolves.

The Paradox of Eval Investment

The teams that most need evals are the teams that feel they least have time for evals.

If your product has quality problems, you're firefighting: user complaints, incident reports, rollbacks, manual reviews. When someone suggests "let's build an eval suite," the reaction is "we don't have time — we're too busy fighting fires." But the fires exist because there are no evals.

  The Eval Investment Paradox
  ===========================

  Without evals (vicious cycle):
  Ship blind → bad changes → fires → fight fires → no time
  → ship blind again → more fires → ...

  With evals (virtuous cycle):
  Invest one week → catch bad changes before shipping → fewer fires
  → time saved → invest in more test cases → even fewer fires
  → even more time saved → better product → ...

The initial investment breaks the vicious cycle. The ROI is visible within the first two weeks.

The Bottom Line
Evals are the PM's job because the eval rubric is the product spec. The dimensions you choose, the weights you assign, the quality bar you set, the test cases you curate — these decisions determine what your AI product optimizes for, every day, on every output, for every user. That's product strategy. It's yours.

The industry agrees. The logic is clear. The opportunity is enormous. The question is whether you're going to step into the gap or wait for someone else to.

A Quick Self-Assessment

After reading this chapter, you should be able to answer these questions confidently:

  • Why is "the PM who defines the eval rubric defines the product" true? (Because rubric weights encode product strategy.)
  • What's the difference between infrastructure tests and quality evals? (Infrastructure = system is running. Quality = system is producing good output.)
  • What are the 5 common anti-patterns in eval ownership? (Later, set-and-forget, users-as-eval, unit-tests-as-evals, manual-QA-only.)
  • What does your first week look like? (Days 1-2: read 30 traces. Day 3: shadow 10 sessions. Day 4: interview engineers + write 10 test cases. Day 5: score, write State of Quality, claim ownership.)
  • How do you frame eval ownership to different stakeholders? (CEO: trendlines. Eng: interface spec. Design: UX dimensions. Team: sprint priorities.)

If you can answer these, you've internalized the chapter. What matters is that you've developed the conviction: this is my job, and I know how to start.

What's Coming Next

Phase 1 of 5

Map Your Agent

Answering Q1: What is the agent supposed to DO?

Chapters 04 – 06  •  Before you can evaluate anything, you need to understand what you are evaluating.

Chapter 04Decompose Your Agent’s Architecture

Here is a first principle that applies to every system ever built, from a toaster to a spacecraft to an AI agent:

First Principle
Any system that takes input and produces output has decision points. Find those decision points. Each one is where things can go right or wrong. Each one is an evaluation surface.

A toaster has one decision point: how long to heat. A car has thousands. An AI agent? It depends on the agent. But every single one of those decision points is a place where quality can succeed or fail. Your job as a PM is to find them all, map them, and then decide which ones to measure.

This chapter is about building that map.

What Is a Decision Point?

A decision point is any moment where the agent makes a choice. Not a deterministic computation — a choice. A place where, given the same input, the agent might do something different on the next run. A place where it exercises judgment.

Here are the categories of decisions that agents make:

Decision Type What the Agent Is Choosing Example
Tool selection Which tool to call, or whether to call one at all Should I search the web, read a file, or already have enough context?
Parameter construction What arguments to pass to the selected tool What search query should I use? Which file path?
Information prioritization What to keep, what to discard, what to emphasize The brand has 12 product lines — which 3 matter for this campaign?
Content generation What to write, in what tone, with what structure Write a curiosity-based ad hook for Gen Z vs. a stat-based hook for CFOs
Routing Which path to take through the workflow Is this a simple question or does it need deep research?
Stopping When to stop iterating and declare "done" Is this image prompt specific enough, or should I revise it again?
Error recovery What to do when something fails Image generation returned an error — retry with different parameters, or skip?

Every one of these is an evaluation surface. Every one of these is where your product quality is determined.

Definition
Evaluation surface — A point in the agent’s execution where quality can be measured. Each decision point creates at least one evaluation surface. Some create several (you can evaluate both the choice and the outcome of the choice).

How to Find Decision Points: The 10-Trace Method

You cannot reason your way to a complete list of decision points from first principles alone. The agent will surprise you. It will make choices you did not design and skip steps you thought were mandatory. The only reliable method is observation.

Here is the method:

  1. Run the agent 10 times on representative inputs — 5 typical cases, 3 edge cases, 2 failure-prone cases.
  2. For each run, capture the full trace: every LLM call, every tool invocation, every intermediate output, every final output.
  3. Read each trace end-to-end. Do not skim. Read every token.
  4. Mark every place where the agent makes a choice. Use the decision types table above as your checklist. Every tool call is a choice. Every piece of generated content is a choice. Every "I will now do X" is a choice.
  5. Compare the 10 traces. Where do they diverge? Those divergence points are your highest-variance decision points — the places where the agent is most unpredictable.
Common Mistake
Do not skip step 5. PMs often trace one run, nod along, and call it done. The power of 10 traces is in the comparison. One trace tells you what the agent did. Ten traces tell you what the agent might do.

After this exercise you will have a list of 10 to 50 decision points, depending on the complexity of your agent. That list is the raw material for everything that follows in this guide.

What This Looks Like in Practice

Say you are tracing a customer support bot. Here is what your notes might look like for one trace:

Trace 3 — User asks about refund for damaged item
• DECISION: Agent classified as “product quality” not “returns/refunds” — wrong route
• DECISION: Searched knowledge base for “damaged product policy” (reasonable query)
• DECISION: Retrieved 3 articles, chose the one about manufacturer warranties (wrong choice — user wants return policy)
• DECISION: Drafted response about warranty claims instead of refund process
• DECISION: Did not escalate to human even though answer was off-topic
Result: User asked again. Agent corrected on second attempt. 3 minutes wasted.

Five decision points in one trace. The first one (wrong classification) cascaded into all the others. Now multiply this by 10 traces and you start seeing which decision points fail consistently versus which ones were just unlucky on this run.

The Divergence Method

Step 5 — comparing traces against each other — deserves special attention. When you line up 10 traces for similar inputs, you will see some decision points where the agent always does the same thing, and others where it does something different every time.

The points of high divergence are your most important evaluation surfaces. They represent the decisions where the agent is most uncertain, most variable, and therefore most likely to produce inconsistent quality. A decision point with zero divergence (agent always does the same thing) either works well or fails consistently — either way, it is predictable. A decision point with high divergence is where the user experience becomes a roulette wheel.

In practice, the highest-divergence decision points tend to be information prioritization and content generation decisions. Tool selection and parameter construction are often more consistent because they are more constrained by the available tools. But what the agent does with the tool results — what it emphasizes, what it ignores, how it frames the output — that is where variance lives.

The Five Building Blocks of Agent Architecture

When you trace your 10 runs, you will notice that the decision points cluster into recognizable patterns. Anthropic, in their “Building Effective Agents” guide, identified five composable workflow patterns that appear across virtually all agent systems. These are not a framework you impose on your agent. They are patterns you will discover in your traces.

Think of them as architectural building blocks. Most agents combine two or more of these patterns. Understanding each one tells you what kind of evaluation that part of the system needs.

Why Patterns Matter for Evaluation
You do not need to memorize these patterns as academic taxonomy. They matter for a practical reason: each pattern has a different failure mode, and therefore needs a different evaluation approach. A prompt chain fails through cascade (bad input pollutes all downstream steps). A router fails through misclassification (the input goes down the wrong path). An evaluator-optimizer fails through miscalibration (the critic approves bad work or rejects good work). Knowing the pattern tells you what kind of failure to look for.

Pattern 1: Prompt Chaining

What it looks like: The agent does step A, takes the output, feeds it into step B, takes that output, feeds it into step C. A sequential pipeline where each link depends on the one before it.

Research the brand → Write ad hooks → Generate image prompts → Create images

What to evaluate: Each step’s output quality on its own, and how errors propagate across the chain. The defining characteristic of prompt chaining is that a mistake at step A poisons steps B, C, and D. If the research gets the brand wrong, every hook will be wrong, every image prompt will be wrong, and every image will be wrong.

Grading approach: Gate checks between steps. After each step, evaluate the output before feeding it to the next step. This lets you catch errors early instead of discovering them only at the final output.

Real Product Example — Creative Campaign Agent
An ad campaign generator researches a brand, then writes 6 hooks, then generates art direction, then creates 6 images. If the research step misidentifies the target audience (say, describing a luxury skincare brand as a budget discount retailer), the hooks will target the wrong buyer, the image prompts will describe the wrong aesthetic, and the final images will look like they belong on a completely different brand’s website. The entire downstream chain is contaminated. Gate-checking the research output alone could prevent all of those downstream failures.

Pattern 2: Routing

What it looks like: The agent receives an input, classifies it, and sends it down one of several paths. The classification is the decision. Once the path is chosen, a different handler takes over.

User query arrives → Classify: billing / technical / general? → Route to specialist

What to evaluate: Classification accuracy. Did the router send the input down the right path? This is often the simplest decision point to evaluate because you can compare the chosen route against a ground truth label.

Grading approach: Deterministic check against expected route. Build a labeled dataset of inputs with their correct routes, run the router, measure accuracy. This is one of the few agent decisions you can evaluate with a simple yes/no check.

Real Product Example — Customer Support Bot
Intercom’s AI bot classifies incoming tickets into categories: billing, technical, account access, feature request, complaint. A billing question routed to the technical team gets a confusing answer. A complaint routed to the automated FAQ handler gets a tone-deaf response. Misrouting is measurable, binary, and directly impacts user satisfaction. Many support bots achieve 90%+ routing accuracy — but that remaining 10% generates most of the negative CSAT scores.

Pattern 3: Parallelization

What it looks like: The agent splits the work into multiple branches that run simultaneously, then aggregates the results. This is the “fan out, fan in” pattern.

Task → Branch A
Task → Branch B → Aggregate
Task → Branch C

What to evaluate: Two separate things. First, evaluate each branch independently — did Branch A produce good results? Did Branch B? Second, evaluate the aggregation — did the agent combine the branch outputs effectively? A common failure mode is that all branches produce excellent results individually, but the aggregation step drops important information or introduces contradictions.

Grading approach: Score each branch independently, then score the synthesis. The aggregation score is often the bottleneck — individual branches can be high-quality, but the merge step introduces a new decision point: what to keep, what to discard, how to reconcile conflicts.

Real Product Example — Perplexity Search
When you ask Perplexity a complex question, it searches multiple sources simultaneously. Each search returns a different perspective, different facts, different levels of reliability. The hard part is not the individual searches. The hard part is the synthesis — weaving five search results into one coherent, accurate, well-cited answer. One branch might say “revenue was $4.2B” while another says “revenue was $4.8B” (different fiscal years). The agent must decide which to use, or explain the discrepancy. That aggregation decision is the evaluation surface.

Pattern 4: Orchestrator-Workers

What it looks like: A central “orchestrator” agent decomposes a task into subtasks, delegates each subtask to a specialist “worker” agent, and then assembles the results. Unlike parallelization (where the branches are pre-defined), the orchestrator decides what the subtasks should be.

Orchestrator: decompose task → Worker 1: research
Orchestrator: decompose task → Worker 2: write copy → Orchestrator: assemble
Orchestrator: decompose task → Worker 3: generate images

What to evaluate: Three layers. (1) Did the orchestrator decompose the task well? (2) Did each worker produce good output for its subtask? (3) Did the orchestrator assemble the worker outputs into a coherent final product? Each layer is an independent evaluation surface.

Grading approach: Evaluate the orchestrator’s plan, each worker’s output, and the final assembly. Anthropic’s own multi-agent research system (Claude Opus lead agent with Claude Sonnet sub-agents) used this exact pattern and evaluated it with an LLM judge scoring five criteria: factual accuracy, citation accuracy, completeness, source quality, and tool efficiency.

Real Product Example — Devin (Cognition AI)
Devin, the AI software engineer, uses an orchestrator pattern. A planning agent reads the task (“add dark mode support to the settings page”), decomposes it into subtasks (find relevant files, understand the theme system, modify components, update styles, write tests), and dispatches specialized workers for each. The orchestrator must understand enough about code architecture to create a good plan. A bad plan — missing a subtask, or decomposing at the wrong granularity — causes everything downstream to fail.

Pattern 5: Evaluator-Optimizer

What it looks like: A generator agent produces output. An evaluator agent (the “critic”) reviews it and provides feedback. The generator revises. This loop repeats until the evaluator is satisfied or a maximum number of iterations is reached.

Generate draft → Evaluate: good enough? → Revise draft ↺

What to evaluate: Evaluator accuracy (does it correctly identify problems?), convergence behavior (does quality actually improve with each iteration?), and final quality (is the output after N iterations better than the first draft?). A sneaky failure mode: the evaluator might be too lenient (approves bad work) or too strict (never approves, hitting the iteration cap).

Grading approach: Track the improvement curve across iterations. Plot quality scores at each iteration. If the curve is flat (no improvement) or oscillates (getting better then worse), the evaluator-optimizer loop is broken.

Real Product Example — Cursor (Code Generation)
When Cursor generates a complex code change, it often includes an internal review step: generate the diff, check for syntax errors and logical inconsistencies, revise. The evaluator catches things like “you deleted a function that’s called elsewhere” or “you imported a module that doesn’t exist.” When the evaluator is well-calibrated, you get cleaner first-try code. When it is poorly calibrated, you get either sloppy code (lenient evaluator) or endless “thinking...” loops (strict evaluator).

Pattern Evaluation Summary

Here is the complete map of what to evaluate and how, for each pattern:

Pattern What to Evaluate Grading Approach
Prompt Chaining Each step’s output quality; how errors cascade downstream Gate checks between steps. Catch mistakes before they propagate.
Routing Classification accuracy — did the input go down the right path? Deterministic check against labeled ground truth.
Parallelization Each branch independently; quality of the aggregation Score branches independently, then score the synthesis.
Orchestrator-Workers Task decomposition; each worker’s output; final assembly Evaluate plan + workers + assembly as three separate layers.
Evaluator-Optimizer Evaluator accuracy; convergence over iterations; final quality Track improvement curve. Quality should increase monotonically.
Key Insight
Most production agents are not one pattern. They are combinations. A creative campaign agent uses prompt chaining (research feeds hooks feeds images) with an orchestrator managing the overall flow. A customer support bot uses routing followed by prompt chaining within each route. When you trace your 10 runs, you will see the patterns layered on top of each other. Label each section of your trace with the pattern it belongs to. That labeling tells you which evaluation approach applies to that section.

Drawing Your Agent’s Blueprint

Now take your 10 traces, your list of decision points, and your pattern labels, and draw the agent’s architecture as a visual diagram. This is your blueprint — the artifact you will reference for the rest of this guide.

Use four shape types:

Shape Meaning Example
Rectangle A processing step (LLM call, computation) “Generate 6 ad hooks”
Diamond A decision point (agent makes a choice) “Is the research complete enough?”
Rounded rectangle A tool invocation (external call) “Call web search API” or “Call image generation API”
Arrow Data flow (output of one step becomes input of next) Research report → Hook generation

The diamonds are your evaluation surfaces. Every diamond on this diagram is a place where you could write an eval. Not every diamond needs an eval right now — but you should be able to point to every diamond and say “I know this decision exists, and I have a plan for whether and when to evaluate it.”

Example Blueprint: An Ad Campaign Agent

Let’s trace a real agent to make this concrete. Here is an AI-powered ad campaign generator that takes a brand URL and produces 6 creative ads with images:

// The agent's architecture, traced from 10 production runs:

[INPUT] User provides: brand URL + optional brief
    |
    v
[STEP 1] Research the brand // prompt chaining begins
    DECISION: What to search for? // tool parameter construction
    DECISION: Which search results matter? // information prioritization
    DECISION: Is research sufficient? // stopping decision
    |
    v
[STEP 2] Write 6 ad hooks (stat, story, FOMO, curiosity, callout, contrast)
    DECISION: What angle for each hook type? // content generation
    DECISION: Which research facts to use? // information prioritization
    DECISION: Tone and voice for this brand? // content generation
    |
    v
[STEP 3] Generate art direction for each hook
    DECISION: Which visual style? // choosing from 14 art styles
    DECISION: What composition and color palette? // content generation
    |
    v
[STEP 4] Generate 6 images via image API
    DECISION: Image prompt wording? // tool parameter construction
    DECISION: Retry on failure? // error recovery
    |
    v
[OUTPUT] 6 ad creatives with hooks + images

Count the diamonds: 10 decision points across 4 steps. Each one is an evaluation surface. Some are high-stakes (getting the brand research wrong poisons everything downstream), and some are lower-stakes (the exact image prompt wording can be regenerated cheaply). We will prioritize them in the next chapter.

But first, notice the pattern: this is prompt chaining (each step feeds the next) with decision points embedded at every step. That tells us the primary evaluation strategy: gate checks between steps, with special attention to error propagation from Step 1 (research) through the entire chain.

Identifying Combination Patterns

Real agents rarely use a single pattern in isolation. Here is how to spot combinations in your traces:

If You See This in Your Trace... It Is This Pattern Combined With
Step 1 output feeds directly into Step 2 Prompt Chaining Often contains routing or parallelization within individual steps
An “if/then” branch based on input classification Routing The branches themselves are usually prompt chains
Multiple tool calls happening in the same turn for the same purpose Parallelization Results feed into a chaining step for synthesis
A central agent creating sub-tasks and dispatching them Orchestrator-Workers Workers may use any other pattern internally
The agent revising its own output multiple times before returning it Evaluator-Optimizer Each revision cycle is itself a prompt chain

For the ad campaign agent above, the architecture is primarily prompt chaining with an implicit evaluator-optimizer inside Step 1 (the agent decides whether research is “sufficient” and may search again). Steps 2-4 may also include internal evaluation loops if the agent reviews its own hooks or prompts before moving forward. Labeling these combination patterns in your blueprint helps you assign the right evaluation strategy to each section.

The Most Important Insight: Grade Outcomes, Not Paths

Now here is a trap that catches almost every new AI PM.

You have your beautiful blueprint. You see the decision points. Your instinct says: “Let me write evals that check whether the agent followed the correct sequence of steps.” Tool A was called before Tool B. The search query contained the brand name. The hooks were generated in the right order.

Do not do this.

The Path Trap
Anthropic’s “Demystifying Evals” guide says it directly: “Grade outcomes, not paths.” There is a common instinct to check that agents followed very specific steps — a particular sequence of tool calls in the right order. This approach is too rigid and penalizes valid alternative solutions. The agent might take a completely different path to an equally good (or better) result. Evaluating the path instead of the outcome will cause you to fail good outputs and miss bad ones.

Think about it this way. Imagine you hired a human researcher to investigate a brand. You do not care whether they checked the company’s LinkedIn page before or after their website. You do not care whether they read the About page or the Press page first. You care about the research report they hand you. Is it accurate? Is it complete? Does it identify the right target audience?

The same principle applies to agents. Your blueprint shows you where to evaluate. But what you evaluate at each point is the quality of the output, not the specific path the agent took to produce it.

Here is a concrete example of why this matters:

Path-Based Eval (Fragile)

“Check that the agent called web_search exactly 3 times, then called read_page on the brand’s About page, then called read_page on their Products page.”

Fails when the agent finds all the information in a single comprehensive search result. Fails when the brand’s About page does not exist. Fails when the agent uses a different but equally valid research strategy.

Outcome-Based Eval (Robust)

“Check that the research output correctly identifies the brand’s target audience, top 3 products, competitive positioning, and brand voice.”

Passes regardless of how the agent found the information. Rewards creative research strategies. Only fails when the actual output is incomplete or wrong.

The blueprint is the map of evaluation surfaces. The outcomes at each surface are what you actually grade.

Do This Now
Draw YOUR agent’s blueprint.
  1. Run 10 traces on representative inputs (5 typical, 3 edge, 2 failure-prone).
  2. Read each trace end-to-end. Mark every decision point.
  3. Label each section with the Anthropic pattern it matches (chaining, routing, parallelization, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer).
  4. Draw the diagram: rectangles for steps, diamonds for decisions, rounded rectangles for tools, arrows for data flow.
  5. Count your diamonds. That number is the size of your evaluation architecture.

Keep this blueprint. You will use it in every chapter that follows.

You have a blueprint. But a blueprint shows you what the agent does. It does not tell you what the user needs. The difference between the system and the jobs is where evaluation starts. An agent can follow its blueprint perfectly and still fail the user — because the blueprint serves the system, not the person. Let’s close that gap.

Chapter 05Identify the Agent’s Jobs and Prioritize

The architecture map from Chapter 04 describes the system. It tells you what the agent does, step by step, decision by decision. But users do not think in terms of decision points and prompt chains. Users think in terms of outcomes. They want the agent to do something for them. The job map tells you what quality means to the user.

This distinction — between what the system does and what the user gets — is where evaluation actually starts. If you only evaluate the system’s internal steps, you can get perfect scores on every step and still have an agent that users hate. The job map closes that gap.

Steps vs. Jobs: The Crucial Distinction

A step is something the agent does internally. A job is something the user gets. Steps are visible in traces. Jobs are visible in user satisfaction.

Steps (System Perspective)

Call web search API

Parse HTML from 7 pages

Extract key brand attributes

Generate research summary

Job (User Perspective)

“Understand my brand well enough to write ads for it.”

Steps are components of the machine. Jobs are promises to the user. You evaluate steps to find bugs. You evaluate jobs to find product failures. Both matter, but the job map tells you which steps matter most.

Real Example
A code assistant team measured “tool call accuracy” (which tool was selected for a given query) and improved it from 82% to 91%. User satisfaction did not budge. Why? Because the job — produce correct, working code — was still succeeding even when the wrong tool was called. The agent would call read_file instead of search_codebase, get a slightly different path to the answer, and still produce the right output. The step metric was noisy. The job metric was what mattered.
Definition
Job map — The complete list of promises your agent makes to users, prioritized by frequency, severity, and impact. This map becomes your evaluation roadmap: you build evals for the highest-priority jobs first.

How to Find Jobs: Four Sources

Source 1: Read 30 Production Traces

You already read 10 traces in Chapter 04 to find decision points. Now go back to those traces — and 20 more — with a different lens. This time you are not looking for decision points. You are answering a single question: What did the user ASK?

Strip away all the internal steps. Ignore the tool calls, the intermediate outputs, the routing decisions. Look only at the input (what the user wanted) and the output (what the user got). The gap between those two is the job.

Source 2: Shadow 10 User Sessions

Watch real users interact with your agent. Not recordings — live sessions where you can see their face, hear their sighs, watch them retype the same prompt three different ways. You are looking for the gap between what they expected and what they actually got. Every gap is a job the agent failed to deliver.

Source 3: Talk to the Engineering Team

Engineers know the hidden capabilities and the impossible jobs. They will tell you “the agent can actually do X, but nobody knows about it” (a job that exists but is not surfaced) and “users keep asking for Y, but the architecture cannot support it” (a job that users expect but cannot be delivered). Both of these shape your job map.

Source 4: Read Support Tickets

Users complain about failed promises. Support tickets are the unfiltered voice of job failure. Every ticket is a user saying “I expected the agent to do X, and it did not.” That expectation is a job.

Do Not Skip Source 4
Support tickets are uncomfortable reading. They are emotional, sometimes unfair, often poorly written. But they show you what matters enough to a user that they took the time to complain about it. That signal is invaluable. A job that generates support tickets is, by definition, high-severity.

Example Job Maps for Three Products

Creative Campaign Agent:

# Job What the User Expects
1 Research my brand Understand what my company does, who we sell to, and how we position ourselves
2 Write compelling hooks Give me 6 ad angles that would make my target audience stop scrolling
3 Art-direct the images Choose visual styles and compositions that match my brand aesthetic
4 Generate the images Produce polished images I can actually use in ad campaigns
5 Handle follow-ups Let me refine the output without starting over from scratch

Customer Support Bot:

# Job What the User Expects
1 Classify my issue Understand what kind of problem I have without making me explain it twice
2 Find the right policy Know the rules that apply to my specific situation
3 Draft a response Give me a clear, accurate answer I can act on
4 Escalate when needed Know when you are out of your depth and hand me to a human
5 Not make it worse Do not give me wrong information that causes me to take the wrong action

Code Assistant:

# Job What the User Expects
1 Understand my intent Know what I am trying to build from a brief, possibly ambiguous description
2 Search the codebase Find the relevant files and understand the existing patterns
3 Generate correct code Write code that works, follows the project’s conventions, and does what I asked
4 Explain what changed Help me understand the changes so I can review them confidently
5 Not break existing code Do not introduce regressions, delete things I need, or ignore tests

Notice that each product has roughly 5 jobs. This is typical. If you have more than 7 or 8, you are probably listing steps disguised as jobs. If you have fewer than 3, you are probably grouping too aggressively.

The Litmus Test: Is This a Step or a Job?

Use two tests to distinguish steps from jobs:

Test 1: Can you explain it to a user without mentioning internal workings?

  • “Parse the JSON response from the API” — Step. Users don’t know or care about JSON.
  • “Find the refund policy that applies to your situation” — Job. Users understand this immediately.
  • “Embed the query into a vector and search the index” — Step. Technical implementation detail.
  • “Search the codebase for relevant files” — Job. Users want this and would notice if it failed.

Test 2: Would the user file a support ticket if this failed?

If the JSON parser breaks, the user sees a generic error message and retries. They do not file a ticket about JSON parsing. If the agent finds the wrong refund policy and gives incorrect advice, the user absolutely files a ticket. The second test catches the things that matter to users.

The Relationship Between Jobs and Steps
Every job is implemented by steps. But the relationship is not one-to-one. One job may require dozens of steps. Multiple steps may serve a single job. And some steps exist only for internal bookkeeping and do not map to any user-facing job at all. Your architecture blueprint (Chapter 04) maps the steps. Your job map (this chapter) maps the promises. Evaluation connects the two: you measure jobs to know if the product is good, and you measure steps to diagnose why a job is failing.

The Prioritization Matrix: Which Jobs to Evaluate First

You cannot evaluate all jobs with equal rigor on day one. You need a priority order. Use three dimensions:

Dimension What It Measures Scale
Frequency How often does this job get triggered? 1 (rare) to 5 (every session)
Failure Severity How bad is it when this job fails? 1 (minor annoyance) to 5 (catastrophic, unrecoverable)
User Impact How visible is this job to the user? 1 (invisible internal step) to 5 (the primary deliverable)
Priority Score Formula
Priority = Frequency × Severity × User Impact
Range: 1 to 125. Score each dimension 1–5, then multiply. Why multiply instead of add? Because a job that is frequent AND severe AND visible is exponentially more important than one that is high on only one dimension. Addition would underweight the combination effect.

Example: Prioritizing the Creative Campaign Agent’s Jobs

Job Frequency Severity Impact Score
Research my brand 5 5 5 125
Write compelling hooks 5 4 5 100
Art-direct the images 5 3 5 75
Generate the images 5 3 5 75
Handle follow-ups 3 3 4 36

Research accuracy comes first because of the cascade effect (see Chapter 01). If the research is wrong, hooks are wrong, art direction is wrong, images are wrong. Everything downstream is contaminated. Image generation scores only severity 3 (not 5) because a bad image is recoverable — the user can regenerate. Bad research is not recoverable without starting over.

Applying This to the Customer Support Bot

Job Frequency Severity Impact Score
Not make it worse 5 5 5 125
Find the right policy 5 5 4 100
Draft a response 5 3 5 75
Classify my issue 5 4 3 60
Escalate when needed 2 5 3 30

Different product, different risk profile. “Not make it worse” tops the list because wrong information from a support bot can cause real financial harm (a user takes the wrong action based on bad advice). Escalation scores lower on frequency because most queries can be handled automatically — but when escalation is needed and does not happen, the severity is high.

The Cascade Principle
In prompt-chaining agents, jobs earlier in the chain always have higher severity — early failures cascade downstream (see Chapter 01). Start evals with the first major step.
Do This Now
Build your job priority list.
  1. List every promise your agent makes to users (aim for 5–7 jobs).
  2. Use the litmus test: can you explain it without mentioning internals? Would users file a ticket if it failed?
  3. Score each job on Frequency (1–5), Severity (1–5), and User Impact (1–5).
  4. Multiply to get the Priority Score. Sort descending.
  5. Circle the top 3. These are where you will build evals first.

Write this down. It is your evaluation roadmap for the next 4 weeks.

Phase 2Observe Failures

You've mapped your agent and spent a week observing. You have gut feelings about what's wrong. Now we turn those gut feelings into a structured failure taxonomy — the single most valuable artifact in your entire eval practice.

Phase 1: Map the Agent
→
Phase 2: Observe Failures
→
Phase 3: Define Quality

Phase 4: Build Evals
→
Phase 5: Ship & Iterate

Chapter 07Error Analysis — The Foundation of Everything

Here is the first principle from which everything else in this guide flows:
