The gap between 4 and 5 is where real product differentiation lives. Most AI products can reach 3 (acceptable) with decent prompting. Many can reach 4 (good) with careful engineering. Very few consistently reach 5 (exceptional) — and the ones that do are the products users evangelize. Understanding the gap between 4 and 5 is what separates a competent AI PM from a great one. It's also the gap that's hardest to eval — because the difference between "good" and "exceptional" is often about insight, synthesis, and nuance, which are the hardest qualities for automated systems to measure.

Different Jobs, Different Quality Definitions

Quality is defined per job, not per product. An agent with multiple jobs needs multiple quality definitions.

Consider a creative campaign agent with three major jobs:

Job Quality Is Primarily About... The "Without" Clause Key Eval Dimensions
Brand Research Accuracy and completeness of discovered facts Without manually browsing and reading the brand's entire web presence Coverage, factual accuracy, competitive awareness
Hook Writing Creativity, brand alignment, and variety Without hiring a copywriter and going through 3 rounds of revisions Brand voice match, angle diversity, claim grounding
Image Generation Visual quality, prompt fidelity, brand coherence Without hiring a designer and providing detailed creative briefs Art direction match, visual consistency, technical quality

Research accuracy has nothing to do with hook creativity. Image quality has nothing to do with research completeness. If you define "quality" as a single score across all three jobs, you're averaging apples and oranges. A brilliant set of hooks built on fabricated research would score "average" on a combined metric — hiding both the success and the failure.

The Per-Job Principle
Every job your agent performs needs its own quality definition, its own quality floor, its own quality ceiling, and its own eval criteria. This is not optional overhead — it's the only way to diagnose and fix problems at the right level. You wouldn't use one test to grade a student on math, English, and science. Don't use one eval to grade an agent on research, writing, and image generation.

Consider what happens when you don't follow this principle. You create a single eval that measures "overall campaign quality" on a 1–5 scale. Campaign A scores 3.5: the research was excellent (5/5), the hooks were mediocre (3/5), and the images were poor (2/5). Campaign B also scores 3.5: the research was poor (2/5), the hooks were excellent (5/5), and the images were acceptable (3.5/5). Same score, completely different problems, completely different fixes. The averaged number hides everything useful.

This principle also resolves a common organizational conflict. Different stakeholders care about different jobs. The creative director cares about hook quality and image aesthetics. The brand strategist cares about research accuracy and brand alignment. The engineering lead cares about pipeline reliability and generation time. When you define quality per job, each stakeholder has a clear set of metrics that maps to their concerns. No more arguments about whether "quality is improving" when research is getting better but images are getting worse.

Common Mistakes in Quality Definition

Mistake 1: Defining Quality by Output Characteristics, Not User Outcomes

"The summary should be 3–5 paragraphs, well-structured, with no grammatical errors." That's an output specification, not a quality definition. A well-structured, grammatically perfect summary that misses the key insight is still a failure. Quality starts with the user's outcome: "The user can make a decision based on this summary without reading the original." Output characteristics support that outcome, but they're not the outcome itself.

Mistake 2: Defining Quality Too Abstractly

"The output should be high quality and relevant." Every team would agree with this sentence and no two teams would interpret it the same way. Quality definitions must be specific enough that two evaluators would agree on borderline cases. "The hook references at least one specific product attribute from the research" is testable. "The hook should be relevant" is not.

Mistake 3: Forgetting the Quality Floor

Teams define what "great" looks like (the ceiling) and what "terrible" looks like (obvious fails) but forget the messy middle. The quality floor — the line between "show it" and "don't show it" — is the most important decision in your entire quality definition. It determines your user experience for the median output, not the best or worst output.

Mistake 4: One Quality Definition for Multiple Jobs

A single quality definition for a multi-job agent is like a single test for math, English, and science. The research job and the hook-writing job have fundamentally different quality dimensions. Define them separately.

Non-Negotiables: The Automatic Fail List

These are criteria where failure is unacceptable regardless of overall quality. An output can score 5/5 on every other dimension, but if it violates a non-negotiable, it's an automatic fail.

Non-negotiables typically fall into three categories:

  • Trust-breaking: Fabricated facts, hallucinated sources, made-up statistics. Once a user catches your agent lying, they stop trusting all of its output — even the accurate parts.
  • Harm-causing: Offensive content, dangerous advice, privacy violations. These create legal and reputational risk that no amount of "good quality on average" can offset.
  • Contract-breaking: Things your product explicitly promises not to do. If your product says "no stock photography," an image that looks like stock photography is a non-negotiable fail even if it's technically AI-generated.

The purpose of non-negotiables is to set a hard floor that your eval system can enforce automatically. When a non-negotiable fails, the system doesn't show the output to the user — it retries, escalates, or shows an error. This is different from quality floor failures, where the output is subpar but not dangerous.

Non-Negotiables by Product Type
Search / Research: No fabricated citations. No invented sources. No presenting one side of a debate as settled fact.

Customer Support: No incorrect policy information. No fabricated order numbers. No promises the system can't fulfill.

Creative / Marketing: No fabricated statistics. No brand name misspellings. No content that could damage the brand (offensive, controversial, or legally risky).

Medical / Legal: No definitive diagnoses or legal conclusions. Always includes disclaimer to consult a professional. No recommendations that contradict established guidelines.

Code Generation: No code that deletes data without confirmation. No hard-coded credentials. No security vulnerabilities in generated code.

The Good Output Document

After answering all five questions, compile your answers into a single document. This becomes the source of truth for your eval rubric, and the document you hand to anyone new joining the team.

Template — Good Output Document
Feature: [Name of the AI feature or job]
User Goal: The user wants to _______ without _______.
Gold Standard: [What the best human would produce — 5–8 bullet points]
Failure Modes: [Which modes apply to this feature, ranked by severity, pulled from your Ch 07 taxonomy]
Quality Floor (3/5): [What "barely acceptable" looks like — 3–5 bullet points]
Quality Ceiling (5/5): [What "exceptional" looks like — 3–5 bullet points]
Non-Negotiables: [Things that are automatic fails regardless of overall quality — e.g., fabricated facts, harmful content, format breaks, brand misidentification]

Write this document before you write a single eval. It takes 30 minutes per job and saves weeks of building evals that measure the wrong things.
Completed Example — Hook Writing Job
Feature: Ad Hook Generation

User Goal: The user wants 6 distinct, on-brand ad hooks so they can launch a social campaign without hiring a copywriter or spending days on creative iteration.

Gold Standard:
• Each hook uses a fundamentally different persuasion angle (stat, story, FOMO, curiosity, callout, contrast)
• Every claim traces back to the brand research — no fabricated stats
• Hooks match the brand's voice (playful brands get playful hooks, premium brands get refined hooks)
• Each hook has a clear structure: attention-grabbing first line, brand-specific claim, implicit or explicit CTA
• A creative director would recognize these as strong first drafts, not generic filler

Failure Modes (from taxonomy):
• Brand disconnection (#1 — 26% of failures)
• Hook homogeneity (#3 — 15%)
• Fabricated claims (#5 — 13%)

Quality Floor (3/5):
• All hooks reference the actual brand and its real products
• At least 4 of 6 hooks use distinct angles
• No fabricated statistics or claims
• Tone is appropriate for the brand category (not comically off)

Quality Ceiling (5/5):
• Every hook captures the brand's voice, not just its products
• The stat hook uses a genuinely surprising, verifiable number
• Hooks target the brand's specific audience, not a generic "everyone"
• A creative director would iterate on these, not replace them

Non-Negotiables (automatic fail):
• Any fabricated statistic or claim not grounded in research
• A hook that could work for any brand (fails logo swap test)
• Brand name or product name misspelled
• Offensive, insensitive, or brand-damaging content
Do — Quality Definition Exercise
Complete the Good Output Document for your #1 priority job. Use the template above.

1. Write the "without" clause formula
2. Describe what a great human would produce (5–8 bullet points)
3. List the relevant failure modes from your Chapter 07 taxonomy
4. Define your quality floor (3/5) with specific criteria
5. Define your quality ceiling (5/5) with specific criteria
6. List your non-negotiables (automatic fails)

Time: 30 minutes. Value: This document prevents weeks of building evals for the wrong things.
When to Update the Good Output Document
Your Good Output Document is a living document, not a carved-in-stone specification. Update it when:

• New failure modes emerge — add them to the Failure Modes section
• Your user base changes — different users have different quality standards
• Your model changes — a better model may make your quality ceiling higher
• You learn from eval results — running evals will reveal gaps in your quality definition
• Competitors raise the bar — what was "exceptional" last quarter may be "baseline" this quarter

Husain calls this "criteria drift" and considers it expected: "Evaluation criteria evolve as reviewers observe more outputs. Treat them as living documents, not fixed specifications." The Good Output Document is your best current understanding of quality — and it will improve over time.

The ACCT Framework: Organizing Quality into Measurable Dimensions

ACCT — Accuracy, Completeness, Calibration, and Taste — is a way to organize your Good Output Document into categories you can track and improve independently. It emerged from studying how every major AI product team thinks about quality. Whether they use these exact words or not, every eval rubric we've seen maps to one of these four dimensions.

The Good Output Document is specific — it defines quality for one job. ACCT is universal — it organizes quality across all jobs. When your CEO asks "how is quality?" they don't want a job-by-job breakdown. They want to know: "Is the output accurate? Does it do the whole job? Does it know its limits? Does it feel right?" Those four questions are ACCT.

The Four Dimensions

Dimension Core Question Real Product Example
A Accuracy Is the information correct? Perplexity says "Apple was founded in 1974" — it was 1976. Fail.
C Completeness Did it do the whole job? Notion AI summarizes 3 of 5 key points. User has to read the original anyway. Fail.
C Calibration Does it know what it doesn't know? A medical chatbot says "You definitely have strep throat" instead of "This could be strep — see a doctor." Dangerous fail.
T Taste Does the output feel right? A luxury brand's AI writes like a college intern. Tone mismatch. Brand fail.

Each dimension connects to the failures you observed in Phase 2 and the quality you defined above.

Accuracy: Is the Information Correct?

Accuracy is the most straightforward dimension. Either the fact is right or it's wrong. But "accuracy" applies differently depending on your product:

  • Search products (Perplexity): Accuracy means factual correctness of claims and citations
  • Support bots (Intercom): Accuracy means correct policy information and order details
  • Creative agents: Accuracy means claims in hooks are grounded in actual research — the brand really does use recycled materials, the market really is growing at 12%
  • Code assistants (Cursor, Copilot): Accuracy means the generated code compiles, runs, and produces correct output

Which failures from your taxonomy map here? Hallucination Bridge, Stale Information, Fabricated Claims, Entity Confusion. If your Phase 2 taxonomy has categories that boil down to "the agent said something that isn't true," those are Accuracy failures.

Completeness: Did It Do the Whole Job?

Completeness asks whether the agent finished what it started. This dimension is where the "without" clause becomes measurable. If the user needs a summary so they can skip the original document, and the summary misses two key points, the user still has to read the original. The agent was incomplete, and the "without" promise was broken.

  • Summarization (Notion AI): Did the summary capture all the key points?
  • Research agents: Did the research cover the full landscape — or just the first page of results?
  • Creative agents: Are all 6 hooks genuinely different, or are 3 of them variations on the same angle?
  • Customer support: If the user asked a multi-part question, did the bot address every part?

Which failures from your taxonomy map here? Shallow Research, Hook Homogeneity, "Lazy Middle" (thorough start, rushed ending), partial task completion. If your taxonomy has categories that boil down to "the agent did part of the job but not all of it," those are Completeness failures.

The Completeness Trap
Completeness failures are sneaky because the output looks finished. A research summary with 5 paragraphs looks complete. But if the brand has a revolutionary new product line that the research missed entirely, those 5 paragraphs are incomplete — there should have been 7. The user doesn't know about the missing paragraphs because they don't know what they don't know. That's the trap: incomplete outputs are invisible to the user unless they do the work the agent was supposed to do.

This is why Completeness evals often require a "gold standard" — a reference answer that represents what a complete output should cover. Without it, you're measuring whether the output looks complete, not whether it is complete.

Calibration: Does It Know What It Doesn't Know?

Calibration is the most subtle dimension, and the most dangerous when it fails. A well-calibrated agent matches its confidence to its actual certainty. It says "definitely" when it's sure, "probably" when it's likely, and "I don't know" when it doesn't.

Users calibrate their trust based on the agent's confidence. If the agent is always confident, users either trust everything (dangerous) or trust nothing (useless). Good calibration means the user can trust the agent's confidence as a signal: when it's confident, the information is reliable; when it hedges, the user knows to verify.

  • Medical chatbots: "This could be strep throat — see a doctor" vs. "You definitely have strep throat." The difference is a lawsuit.
  • Financial advisors: "Based on historical trends, this investment carries moderate risk" vs. "This stock will definitely go up." The difference is a regulatory violation.
  • Search products: "Experts disagree on this topic" vs. presenting one side as settled fact.
  • Creative agents: "I found limited information about this brand's sustainability practices" vs. confidently generating hooks about sustainability based on guesswork.

Which failures from your taxonomy map here? Overconfidence, Sycophancy Trap, Ghost Actions (claiming completion when incomplete). If your taxonomy has categories that boil down to "the agent was more confident than the evidence warranted," those are Calibration failures.

Calibration Is About Trust Management
Think of calibration as the agent managing the user's trust. Every claim the agent makes is an implicit trust request: "believe this." If the agent is well-calibrated, the user learns over time that confident claims are reliable and hedged claims need verification. This is enormously valuable — it means the user can use the agent's confidence as a signal.

If the agent is poorly calibrated (always confident, even when wrong), the user can't use confidence as a signal. They either trust everything (and get burned) or trust nothing (and the agent is useless). Bad calibration destroys the information value of the agent's output, even when the output is mostly accurate.

Taste: Does the Output Feel Right?

Taste is the hardest dimension to eval because it seems subjective until you define it carefully. "This doesn't feel right" is not a useful eval criterion. "This uses casual slang for a luxury brand audience" is.

Taste encompasses tone, voice, style, format, reading level, and overall "fit" with the context. It's the dimension where domain expertise matters most.

  • Marketing AI (Jasper): Does the copy match the brand's voice? Would it fit alongside the brand's existing content?
  • Customer support: Is the tone appropriate for the situation? Empathetic for complaints, efficient for simple inquiries?
  • Creative agents: Does the art style match the brand identity? Are the hooks written in a voice that matches the brand?
  • Documentation tools: Is the writing clear, appropriately technical, and well-structured?

Which failures from your taxonomy map here? Generic Trap, Brand Disconnection, Art Style Mismatch, tone/voice misalignment. If your taxonomy has categories that boil down to "the output is technically correct but doesn't fit the context," those are Taste failures.

The Subjectivity Myth
Hamel Husain addresses the "taste is subjective" objection directly: "Subjective usually means we haven't defined our criteria yet." The fix isn't to abandon Taste as an eval dimension. The fix is to make Taste criteria more specific. Instead of "good tone," specify: "Uses second person ('you'), active voice, sentences under 20 words, no jargon without definition, warm but professional." Now it's testable.

A practical technique for making Taste criteria specific: collect 5 examples of outputs you consider "good taste" and 5 you consider "bad taste." Then ask yourself: what's the difference? Write it down. That difference — expressed in concrete, observable terms — is your Taste criterion.

Bad Taste Criterion

"The hooks should have good tone."

Vague. Different people would interpret "good tone" differently. An LLM-as-judge would default to "seems fine."

Good Taste Criterion

"The hooks should match the energy level of the brand's existing marketing: if the brand uses casual, playful language on their website, the hooks should be casual and playful. If the brand uses formal, measured language, the hooks should be formal and measured."

Specific. References observable characteristics. An evaluator can check the brand's website and compare. An LLM-as-judge can be given the brand's existing copy as reference.

Mapping Your Quality Definition to ACCT

Take your Good Output Document and map every criterion to an ACCT dimension. This is where ACCT becomes a practical tool rather than an abstract framework.

Mapping Example — Hook Writing Job
From the Good Output Document earlier:

Accuracy:
• Every claim traces back to the brand research — no fabricated stats
• Brand name and product names are correct

Completeness:
• All 6 hook angles are genuinely different (not variations on the same theme)
• Each hook has the required structure (opening, claim, CTA)

Calibration:
• Stats cite specific sources, not vague "studies show"
• If research was thin, hooks rely on observable product features rather than market claims

Taste:
• Hooks match the brand's voice (playful/premium/technical/etc.)
• Hooks would fit naturally alongside the brand's existing marketing
• Hooks target the brand's actual audience, not a generic "everyone"

This mapping does three things:

First, it organizes your criteria into trackable categories. You can now report "Accuracy: 92%, Completeness: 78%, Calibration: 85%, Taste: 71%" and immediately know where to focus improvement efforts. This is infinitely more useful than a single "quality: 82%" number.

Second, it reveals gaps. If an ACCT dimension has no criteria mapped to it, either that dimension isn't relevant to this job (possible but unlikely) or you missed something in your quality definition (more likely). An empty Calibration column might mean you haven't thought about whether the agent should express uncertainty when research is thin.

Third, it creates a shared vocabulary. When someone says "the hooks are bad," you can now ask: "Bad how? Inaccurate claims? Incomplete coverage? Wrong confidence level? Wrong tone?" Each of those is a different problem with a different fix.

ACCT Mapping — Customer Support Bot
Accuracy:
• Correct policy information (return windows, shipping times, pricing)
• Correct order status and tracking information
• No fabricated order numbers or tracking links

Completeness:
• All parts of multi-part questions addressed
• Relevant next steps provided (not just the answer, but what to do with it)
• Links to relevant help articles when appropriate

Calibration:
• Escalates to human when issue is outside the bot's scope
• Says "I'm not sure about this" rather than guessing on policy edge cases
• Doesn't over-promise resolution ("I'll make sure this is resolved" when it can't guarantee that)

Taste:
• Empathetic tone for complaints, efficient tone for simple inquiries
• Professional but not robotic
• Matches the company's brand voice (casual startup vs. formal enterprise)

ACCT Weight By Product Type

While all four dimensions matter for every product, different products weight them differently. Understanding which dimension is most critical for your product type helps you prioritize your eval investment.

Product Type Primary Dimension Secondary Why
Search / Research (Perplexity) Accuracy Calibration Users make decisions based on the facts. Wrong facts cause bad decisions. Over-confident wrong facts cause confident bad decisions.
Customer Support (Intercom AI) Accuracy Completeness Wrong policy info creates legal liability. Incomplete answers require follow-ups that defeat the purpose of automation.
Creative / Marketing (Jasper, campaign agents) Taste Accuracy Generic or off-brand copy is the most common failure. But fabricated claims are the most dangerous failure.
Code Generation (Cursor, Copilot) Accuracy Completeness Code either works or it doesn't (accuracy). Missing error handling or edge cases (completeness) causes production bugs.
Medical / Legal Advisory Calibration Accuracy Saying "I'm not sure, consult a professional" when uncertain is more important than being right. An overconfident wrong answer causes harm.
Education (Duolingo, Khan Academy) Completeness Taste Covering the full concept matters more than stylistic perfection. But explanations must match the learner's level (taste).

This table is a starting point, not a prescription. Your specific product may weight dimensions differently based on your user base, competitive landscape, and risk tolerance.

The Pass/Fail vs. Score Debate

Now that you have criteria organized by ACCT dimension, you need to decide: is each criterion a pass/fail check or a scored dimension?

Likert Scales (1–5)

Used by: Anthropic documentation, most eval platforms, academic benchmarks

Pro: Granular. Tracks incremental improvement over time. Easy to average and graph. Captures the difference between "slightly off" and "catastrophically wrong."

Con: LLM judges default to 3–4 (central tendency bias). The difference between a 3 and a 4 is subjective and inconsistent across annotators. Creates false precision — the score looks exact but isn't.

Binary Pass/Fail

Used by: Hamel Husain, Shreya Shankar (taught to 700+ engineers and PMs), Hebbia

Pro: Forces clear criteria. Easy to agree on. "Did it hallucinate? Yes or No." No ambiguity. Produces more consistent labels across annotators.

Con: Loses nuance. A "fail" that's slightly off and a "fail" that's dangerously wrong look the same. Can't track incremental improvement.

Recommendation: Start With Pass/Fail
Start with binary pass/fail for every criterion. It forces you to define exactly what a "pass" looks like, which is the hard work you need to do regardless of your scoring approach. The clarity that binary forces is more valuable than the nuance you lose.

Once your pass rates stabilize and you need to track incremental improvement, switch to 1–5 scoring for the dimensions where nuance matters (like Taste — where "slightly off-brand" and "completely wrong brand" are genuinely different problems). Keep pass/fail for dimensions where it's truly binary (like Accuracy — the fact is either right or wrong, there is no "partially founded in 1974").

Husain's reasoning: "The difference between adjacent points on a Likert scale (like 3 vs. 4) is subjective and inconsistent." Binary forces clearer thinking. When you have to decide "pass or fail" rather than "3 or 4," you're forced to articulate exactly what the boundary is. That articulation is the real work of eval design. The scoring is just bookkeeping.

There's also a practical benefit for LLM-as-judge: binary decisions produce more consistent results across runs. When you ask an LLM "rate this on a 1–5 scale," it tends to cluster around 3–4 (central tendency bias) and varies between runs. When you ask "does this output contain any fabricated claims? Yes or No," the answer is much more consistent. Anthropic's research team found that a single LLM-as-judge call with binary pass/fail was "the most consistent and aligned with human judgments" for their multi-agent research evaluation.

Legitimate reasons to use scores later:

  • Tracking incremental improvement. If your pass rate on "hook brand alignment" goes from 72% to 73%, that's hard to get excited about. But if the average score goes from 3.2 to 3.7, the improvement is visible.
  • Distinguishing severity. "Fabricated a stat" and "fabricated an entire paragraph" are both Accuracy fails, but they're not equally bad. A severity score captures this.
  • A/B testing. When comparing two models or two prompts, scores give you more statistical power than pass/fail (you need fewer test cases to detect a difference).

The Required vs. Additional Split

Hebbia, a company that builds AI for document analysis, introduced a useful refinement: split your criteria into two tiers.

Required criteria define the minimum acceptable behavior — your agent SLA. If any required criterion fails, the output fails. Period. These are your non-negotiables from the Good Output Document.

Additional criteria measure advanced quality — the difference between "acceptable" and "excellent." An output can pass all required criteria and still score low on additional criteria. That means it's usable but not great.

Two-Tier Criteria — Creative Campaign Agent
Required (must pass for the output to be acceptable):
• All hooks reference the actual brand (Accuracy)
• No fabricated statistics (Accuracy)
• At least 4 of 6 hooks use distinct angles (Completeness)
• Images are coherent and match general art direction (Taste)

Additional (separates good from excellent):
• Hooks capture the brand's specific voice, not just its products (Taste)
• The stat hook uses a genuinely surprising number (Taste + Calibration)
• Research covers competitive landscape, not just the brand's own site (Completeness)
• Image set has a consistent visual identity across all 6 images (Taste)

This means a 60% overall score is not necessarily a failure. It might mean: all required criteria passed (the output is usable), but most additional criteria missed (the output isn't impressive). That's a different problem than an output where required criteria failed (the output is garbage).

This two-tier structure is especially useful for communicating with stakeholders. "We're passing all SLAs but only hitting 40% on advanced quality" tells a very different story than "our eval score is 65%." The first tells you the product is reliable but needs polish. The second tells you almost nothing.

Two-Tier Reporting in Practice
Imagine this weekly quality report:

Quality Report — Week of March 24, 2026

Required Criteria (SLA):
  Brand accuracy:       96% pass  (target: 95%)  OK
  No fabricated claims:  91% pass  (target: 95%)  BELOW TARGET
  Hook variety:          88% pass  (target: 85%)  OK
  Image coherence:       94% pass  (target: 90%)  OK

Additional Criteria (Advanced):
  Brand voice match:    62% pass
  Surprising stat hook: 41% pass
  Competitive context:  38% pass
  Visual consistency:   55% pass

This tells a clear story: the product is reliable (most SLAs met) but the fabricated claims issue needs immediate attention (below target). Advanced quality has room for improvement, especially around competitive research and stat hook quality. A single "quality: 74%" number would hide all of this diagnostic information.

The two-tier structure also resolves a common team debate: "Should we ship this version?" If all Required criteria meet their targets, yes — ship it, and improve the Additional criteria in the next iteration. If any Required criterion is below target, no — fix it before shipping. This turns a subjective "is it good enough?" conversation into an objective checklist.

How to Decide: Required or Additional?

A criterion is Required if:

  • Failure would cause the user to lose trust in the product (fabricated facts, harmful content)
  • Failure would make the output unusable (format breaks, wrong brand, major inaccuracy)
  • Failure would require the user to start over rather than edit (complete brand disconnection)
  • Failure would violate a promise your product makes ("no hallucinated stats")

A criterion is Additional if:

  • Failure makes the output less impressive but still usable
  • The user could fix the issue with minor editing
  • It represents the difference between "competent" and "expert"
  • It's aspirational rather than foundational

When in doubt, make it Required. You can always move it to Additional later if it proves too strict. But starting too lenient means shipping outputs that damage user trust — and trust is much harder to rebuild than to maintain.

ACCT Is a Lens, Not a Cage

ACCT is an organizing framework, not a rigid taxonomy. Not every criterion will map cleanly to exactly one dimension. "The stat hook uses a fabricated number" is an Accuracy failure. "The stat hook uses a real number but it's not surprising" is a Taste judgment. "The stat hook uses a real number, presents it as definitely true when the source said 'estimated'" is Calibration.

When a criterion spans dimensions, that's usually a signal that you need to split it into more specific sub-criteria. "Hook quality" is too broad — it spans all four dimensions. "Hook factual grounding" (Accuracy), "Hook angle variety" (Completeness), "Hook confidence vs. evidence" (Calibration), and "Hook brand voice match" (Taste) are each focused enough to live in one ACCT dimension.

The splitting exercise is itself valuable. Hebbia's eval team found that the best eval criteria are atomic. Their four constraints on all rubric criteria:

  1. Atomic: Each criterion addresses a single, diagnosable issue
  2. Binary: Pass/fail is faster and more consistent than scored scales
  3. Specific: Instead of "appropriate visualization," specify "LINE for trends, BAR for comparisons"
  4. Distinct: If two criteria tend to pass or fail together, they're measuring the same thing — remove one

When two criteria tend to pass or fail together, they're probably measuring the same thing — remove one or narrow both. When a criterion sometimes passes and sometimes fails for reasons you can't explain, it's probably compound — split it.

Splitting Compound Criteria
Too broad: "The research output is high quality."
Problem: what does "high quality" mean? Accurate? Complete? Well-organized? All three?

Split into atomic criteria:
• "All factual claims in the research can be verified from the visited sources" (Accuracy)
• "The research covers the brand's core product, differentiator, target audience, and competitive position" (Completeness)
• "The research flags areas where information was limited or uncertain" (Calibration)
• "The research is organized with clear sections and scannable formatting" (Taste)

Now you can diagnose exactly what's wrong. "Research quality is low" becomes "research is accurate and well-organized but misses the competitive landscape and doesn't flag uncertainty." That's actionable.

The Interplay Between Dimensions

The four dimensions aren't independent — they interact in important ways.

Accuracy + Calibration: An accurate-but-uncalibrated agent is dangerous (always confident, usually right, but when wrong, users can't tell). An inaccurate-but-calibrated agent is usable (often wrong, but users know when to double-check).

Completeness + Taste: A complete-but-tasteless output is overwhelming (every possible fact, dumped in a wall of text). An incomplete-but-tasteful output is pleasant but insufficient (beautifully written, but missing critical information).

Accuracy + Completeness: Perfect accuracy on an incomplete set of facts can be misleading. "The company has 500 employees" is accurate, but if the company just announced layoffs reducing to 200, omitting that context makes the accurate fact misleading. Accuracy without completeness is a lie of omission.

These interactions are why you need all four dimensions, not just one or two. Teams that only measure Accuracy (the most common starting point) miss Completeness failures, Calibration failures, and Taste failures — which in many products are more damaging than factual inaccuracy.

Worked Example: From Failure Taxonomy to ACCT Map

Let's trace the complete journey from Phase 2 to Phase 3 for a single product, showing how the failure taxonomy informs the quality definition and how the quality definition maps to ACCT.

Product: A creative campaign agent that researches brands and generates ad hooks + images.

Step 1: Failure Taxonomy (from Phase 2)

Failure Category               %     Examples
1. Brand disconnection        26%   Hooks are generic, images don't match brand
2. Shallow research           18%   Missed key pages, no competitors
3. Hook homogeneity           15%   3+ hooks use same angle
4. Image-prompt mismatch      13%   Image doesn't match described art direction
5. Fabricated claims          13%   Stats not from research
6. Pipeline integrity          8%   Said "done" when it wasn't
Other                          7%   Various

Step 2: Good Output Document

User Goal: "The user wants research-backed ad hooks and matching images so they can launch a campaign without hiring a creative agency."

Gold Standard: 6 distinct hooks, each grounded in real research, matching brand voice, with coherent images.

Quality Floor: Hooks reference real brand, 4+ distinct angles, no fabricated stats, images are coherent.

Quality Ceiling: Hooks capture brand voice (not just products), surprising stats, audience-targeted, visual campaign identity.

Step 3: ACCT Mapping

Criterion ACCT From Failure Category Tier
All claims trace to research A (Accuracy) #5 Fabricated claims Required
Brand name/products correct A (Accuracy) #1 Brand disconnection Required
Research covers product, differentiator, audience C (Completeness) #2 Shallow research Required
4+ hooks use distinct angles C (Completeness) #3 Hook homogeneity Required
All 6 images generated successfully C (Completeness) #6 Pipeline integrity Required
Agent flags when research is thin C (Calibration) #2 Shallow research Additional
Stats include source attribution C (Calibration) #5 Fabricated claims Additional
Hooks match brand voice T (Taste) #1 Brand disconnection Additional
Images match described art direction T (Taste) #4 Image-prompt mismatch Required
Image set has consistent visual identity T (Taste) #4 Image-prompt mismatch Additional
Research covers competitive landscape C (Completeness) #2 Shallow research Additional
Hooks target specific audience T (Taste) #1 Brand disconnection Additional

Notice how every criterion traces back to a specific failure category from the taxonomy. Nothing was invented from scratch. Nothing was borrowed from a blog post about a different product. The entire eval specification is grounded in observed failures from this product.

Also notice the distribution: 5 Required criteria (the SLA), 7 Additional criteria (advanced quality). A "passing" output meets the SLA without necessarily being impressive. A "great" output passes the SLA and hits most of the Additional criteria. The distinction prevents the team from agonizing over whether a "merely adequate" output should ship — if it passes Required, it ships, and you iterate on Additional in the next cycle.

The ACCT Acid Test
For every eval criterion you write, ask: "Am I checking Accuracy, Completeness, Calibration, or Taste?" If the answer is "all of them" or "I'm not sure," the criterion is too broad. Split it until each piece maps to exactly one dimension. This is how you go from "is the output good?" (unmeasurable) to "does the stat hook cite a real, surprising number from the research?" (measurable).
Do — ACCT Mapping Exercise
Map your Good Output Document to ACCT categories:

1. Take every criterion from your Good Output Document
2. Label each one: A (Accuracy), C (Completeness), C (Calibration), or T (Taste)
3. If any criterion maps to multiple dimensions, split it into more specific sub-criteria
4. Check for gaps: is any ACCT dimension empty? If so, did you miss a quality aspect?
5. Split your criteria into Required (SLA) and Additional (advanced quality) tiers

Your deliverable: A categorized, two-tier criteria list organized by ACCT dimension. This is the blueprint for every eval you'll build in Phase 4.

What You've Built So Far

At the end of Phase 3, you now have three artifacts:

Artifact Built In Contains Purpose
Agent Map Phase 1 Jobs, steps, tools, decision points, architecture diagram Tells you what to eval
Failure Taxonomy Phase 2 Ranked failure categories, percentages, examples, formalized criteria Tells you how the agent breaks
Good Output Document + ACCT Map Phase 3 Quality definition per job, floor/ceiling, non-negotiables, ACCT-organized criteria Tells you what success looks like

Together, these three artifacts give you a complete picture: what your agent does (map), how it fails (taxonomy), and what good looks like (quality definition). That's the entire foundation.

Notice what you have NOT done yet:

  • You have not written a single line of eval code
  • You have not set up any eval framework or platform
  • You have not run any automated tests
  • You have not configured any LLM-as-judge

All of that comes in Phase 4. What you've done instead is the thinking that makes all of that worthwhile. Nothing you've built required any code, any eval framework, or any automation. It's all observation, analysis, and writing.

This is deliberate. The most common mistake in eval practice is jumping to automation before the thinking is done. Teams spin up Braintrust or Promptfoo or write custom Python scripts before they've decided what to measure. They end up with sophisticated infrastructure measuring the wrong things. Your three artifacts prevent this — they are the thinking. The automation in Phase 4 is just operationalizing what you've already figured out.

The Foundation Metaphor
Phases 1–3 are the foundation. Phase 4 is the building. Nobody notices a good foundation — they notice the building. But every building that collapses had a bad foundation. The work you've done in these three phases — mapping, observing, defining — is invisible to stakeholders. They want dashboards and metrics and automation. But without this foundation, those dashboards would show numbers that don't correspond to anything real. With it, every metric traces back to an observed failure and a defined standard of quality.

Now the question becomes: how do you turn all of this into something you can actually measure? How do you go from written criteria to automated tests that run against every output your agent produces? How do you decide which criteria need LLM judges, which need deterministic checks, and which need human review?

That's Phase 4 — designing your eval system from zero. And it starts with the most critical step: discovering YOUR eval dimensions — the specific, atomic criteria that will drive every measurement you make. You've already done most of the work. Phase 4 turns your Good Output Document and failure taxonomy into running code.

Preview: What Phase 4 Will Build

To give you a sense of where we're headed, here's what Phase 4 will construct from the artifacts you've built:

  • Chapter 11: Discover Your Eval Dimensions. Turn your ACCT-mapped criteria into formal eval dimensions with rubrics, examples, and grading instructions. This is where atomic criteria become testable assertions.
  • Chapter 12: Build the Eval Dataset. Create test cases that specifically target your failure taxonomy categories. Each test case is designed to trigger (and catch) a specific failure mode you observed in Phase 2.
  • Chapter 13: Choose Your Grading Methods. For each eval dimension, decide: deterministic check, LLM-as-judge, or human review? Your ACCT mapping guides this — Accuracy is often deterministic, Taste usually needs LLM-as-judge.
  • Chapter 14: Run and Interpret. Execute your evals, analyze the results, and create the feedback loop that drives continuous improvement.

Everything in Phase 4 will reference back to what you've built here. Your failure taxonomy determines your test cases. Your Good Output Document determines your grading rubrics. Your ACCT map determines your grading methods. The foundation is complete. Now we build on it.

Chapter 11Discover YOUR Eval Dimensions

If you followed Phase 3, you have a failure taxonomy — a map of every way your agent breaks, with frequencies attached. Now we turn that map into something you can measure automatically. The bridge between "I know my failures" and "I can detect my failures" is a set of eval dimensions.

Most teams skip the bridge. They Google "AI evaluation criteria," copy someone else's rubric, and wonder why their eval scores don't correlate with user complaints. The reason is simple: their dimensions measure generic quality, not their specific failure modes.

This chapter teaches you how to derive eval dimensions from first principles, systematically — from YOUR failure data, for YOUR product. By the end, you'll have 3-6 specific, measurable dimensions that are directly connected to the problems your users actually experience. These dimensions will form the backbone of everything else you build: rubrics, test cases, automated judges, and dashboards.

First Principle
An eval dimension is not something you pick from a menu. It is a direct translation of how your agent fails. Each major failure category from your error analysis becomes a dimension. If 30% of your failures are "stale information," then "Information Recency" is an eval dimension. The dimensions discover themselves — you just need to look.

From Failure Categories to Eval Dimensions

This is the most important derivation in your entire eval system. Get this right and everything downstream — rubrics, weights, test cases, automation — falls into place naturally. Get this wrong and you're measuring the wrong things, forever.

Here is the exact process, worked through with a real example. Follow these steps in order. Don't skip ahead to "writing rubrics" until your dimensions are solid — building rubrics on shaky dimensions is like writing documentation for code that hasn't compiled yet.

Step 1: Start With Your Error Analysis

Go back to the failure taxonomy you built in Phase 3. If you skipped Phase 3, stop here. Go back and do it. You cannot derive meaningful eval dimensions from imagination. You can only derive them from observed failures. This is the difference between building an eval system that works and building one that makes you feel productive while missing the problems that actually hurt users.

Let's work through a complete example. Suppose you ran the Phase 3 error analysis on a research assistant agent (like Perplexity or a company intelligence tool). You reviewed 100 production traces and found these failure categories:

Failure CategoryFrequencyDescription
Stale Information32%Agent cites data that is months or years out of date. Revenue figures from 2024, leadership teams that have changed, products that have been discontinued.
Overconfident Claims18%Agent presents uncertain or disputed information as established fact. No hedging, no source attribution, no acknowledgment of conflicting data.
Incomplete Coverage14%Agent answers part of the question and ignores the rest. User asks about "revenue AND market share," agent only addresses revenue.
Wrong Entity12%Agent confuses the target company with a similarly-named competitor. Attributes Product A's features to Product B. Mixes up subsidiaries.
Generic Filler10%Agent produces text that could apply to any company. "Innovative solutions," "cutting-edge technology," "market leader." No specific data.
Hallucinated Sources8%Agent cites URLs that don't exist or attributes claims to reports that were never published.
Other6%Format issues, truncation, repeated sections.

Step 2: Convert Each Major Failure Category Into a Dimension

Now, the translation. Each failure category becomes an eval dimension — but not by simply copying the name. The dimension needs to describe what good looks like, not just what bad looks like. A failure category says "this is broken." A dimension says "this is what I'm measuring."

Failure Category (32%)→Eval Dimension
Stale Information→Information Recency
Overconfident Claims→Confidence Calibration
Incomplete Coverage→Completeness
Wrong Entity→Entity Accuracy
Generic Filler→Specificity
Hallucinated Sources→Citation Correctness

Six failure categories. Six eval dimensions. Not "Accuracy" and "Helpfulness" — those are so vague they could mean anything. Each dimension targets one specific, diagnosable failure mode in your product.

Notice what we did NOT do. We did not sit in a conference room and brainstorm "what dimensions should a good AI product have?" We did not Google "AI evaluation criteria" and pick from a list. We looked at our specific failures, and the dimensions emerged. This is the core insight. Dimensions are discovered, not invented.

The Same Process for a Marketing Agent

Let's see this work for a completely different product — a marketing copy agent like Jasper or Copy.ai. Different product, different failures, different dimensions.

Failure CategoryFrequency→Eval Dimension
Output sounds like generic ChatGPT28%→Brand Voice Match
Ignores constraints (word count, format, CTA)22%→Constraint Adherence
Makes up product features or pricing15%→Product Accuracy
Uses competitor's language/slogans12%→Competitive Separation
Hook doesn't connect to the target audience11%→Audience Relevance
Copy is technically correct but flat/boring8%→Creative Impact
Other4%→—

Completely different dimensions than the research agent. Not one dimension is shared. That's expected. Different products fail differently, so they need different dimensions. A research agent needs "Information Recency." A marketing agent needs "Brand Voice Match." Neither of these would appear on a generic list of "AI quality dimensions."

And for a Customer Support Agent

One more example, for a product type that's very different from both research and marketing.

Failure CategoryFrequency→Eval Dimension
Routed to wrong department25%→Routing Accuracy
Made false promises about refunds/features20%→Promise Safety
Missed the actual question in long messages18%→Issue Extraction Completeness
Used wrong tone (too casual for frustrated user, too formal for casual chat)15%→Tone Calibration
Gave correct answer but didn't resolve the issue12%→Resolution Effectiveness
Other10%→—

Notice "Promise Safety" — a dimension that would never appear in a research or marketing context but is critical for support. If the bot says "I'll process your refund right away" but can't actually process refunds, that's a brand-damaging failure. The dimension name captures exactly what needs to be evaluated: is the agent making promises it can't keep?

Three products, three completely different dimension sets. That's the proof that dimensions must be derived from YOUR failures, not borrowed from someone else's product.

The Generic Dimension Trap
The instinct is to reach for standard dimensions like "Accuracy," "Relevance," and "Quality." These feel safe because everyone uses them. But they're useless precisely because they're generic. "Accuracy" could mean: Are the facts right? Are the dates current? Is the entity correct? Are the citations real? Those are four different failure modes that need four different fixes. Collapsing them into one dimension means a low score tells you "something about accuracy is wrong" but not what. You can't fix "something." You can fix "the agent is citing stale data."

Step 3: Verify the Derivation

Before moving on, check your work. For each proposed dimension, ask three questions:

  1. Does this map to a real failure I've observed? If you can't point to specific production traces where this dimension would have caught a failure, it's theoretical. Remove it.
  2. Could I explain what "fixing this dimension" means to an engineer? If the answer is "make it better," the dimension is too vague. If the answer is "cite sources published within the last 6 months," it's specific enough.
  3. Is this different from every other dimension on my list? If improving dimension A would automatically improve dimension B, they're measuring the same thing. Merge them or sharpen the distinction.

How to Name Dimensions Well

Naming matters more than you think. A vague name leads to vague rubrics, which lead to inconsistent scoring, which makes your entire eval system unreliable. A specific name constrains the rubric writer (you, or an LLM judge) to evaluate exactly one thing.

Vague Names

"Accuracy" — Accuracy of what? Facts? Dates? Entities? Citations? All of the above?

"Quality" — This means literally nothing. It's the absence of a decision about what to measure.

"Relevance" — Relevant to the query? To the user's context? To the domain?

"Helpfulness" — The most popular dimension in academic benchmarks. The least useful in production. Every LLM judge defaults to "yes, this is helpful" unless you define what helpful means for your product.

Specific Names

"Citation Correctness" — Does every citation point to a real source that actually contains the claimed information?

"Brand Voice Match" — Does the output sound like it was written by someone who works at this brand?

"Information Recency" — Is the data from the last 6 months, or is it stale?

"Entity Disambiguation" — When multiple entities have similar names, does the agent correctly identify and describe the right one?

A useful test: if you told a new team member the dimension name and nothing else, could they write a rubric for it? "Citation Correctness" — yes, they'd know what to evaluate. "Quality" — they'd have to come back and ask you 10 clarifying questions.

More Product-Specific Naming Examples

ProductVague DimensionSpecific DimensionWhy It's Better
Customer support bot"Response Quality""Issue Resolution Completeness"Tells the eval to check whether all parts of the customer's issue were addressed, not just whether the response "seems good"
Code assistant"Correctness""Compilation Success"Binary, testable, no judgment needed. Either the code compiles or it doesn't
Code assistant"Quality""Idiomatic Style Match"Does the generated code follow the conventions of the target language/framework? Specific and reviewable
Legal research tool"Accuracy""Jurisdictional Relevance"Are the cited cases from the correct jurisdiction? A perfectly accurate case citation from UK law is useless when the user asked about US law
Sales intelligence tool"Usefulness""Outreach Angle Clarity"Does the output give the salesperson a specific, non-obvious reason to reach out? Much more actionable than "is it useful"
Medical triage bot"Safety""Escalation Appropriateness"Does the bot correctly identify when to escalate to a human doctor vs. when to provide self-care advice?

In every case, the specific name makes the eval more precise, more actionable, and more reliable. A reviewer evaluating "Jurisdictional Relevance" knows exactly what to look for. A reviewer evaluating "Accuracy" has to guess what aspect of accuracy matters for this particular product.

Hebbia's 4 Constraints for Good Dimensions

Hebbia, the AI document analysis platform, enforces four constraints on every eval dimension they create. These constraints come from hard-won experience evaluating complex, multi-step document analysis workflows. They apply universally.

Constraint 1 — Atomic
Each dimension addresses a single, diagnosable issue — not a compound condition. "Accuracy and Completeness" is NOT one dimension. It is two. If a score is low, you need to know which one failed. Compound dimensions hide the diagnosis. You end up knowing something is wrong but not what, which is the same as knowing nothing.

Test: When this dimension fails, is there exactly one clear thing to fix? If a failure in your dimension could mean two different problems (wrong facts OR missing facts), it's not atomic. Split it.
Constraint 2 — Binary-Friendly
You should be able to make a clear pass/fail judgment on each dimension. This doesn't mean you must use binary scoring (you can use 1-5 scales), but the dimension must be defined clearly enough that a binary decision is possible. If you can't clearly say "pass" or "fail," your definition is too fuzzy. The binary test is a litmus test for definition quality, not a scoring requirement.
Constraint 3 — Specific
Instead of "appropriate visualization," specify "LINE chart for continuous time-series trends, BAR chart for discrete category comparisons, STACKED BAR for part-to-whole relationships across multiple periods." Instead of "accurate information," specify "all numerical claims are within 5% of the source value, and no claim is older than 6 months without explicit dating." Specificity eliminates judgment calls. Judgment calls produce disagreements. Disagreements destroy eval reliability.
Constraint 4 — Distinct
If two dimensions tend to pass or fail together across your test cases, they are measuring the same underlying thing. Remove one or narrow both until they are truly independent. To check: after scoring 30 outputs, calculate the correlation between each pair of dimension scores. If two dimensions have a correlation above 0.85, they are not distinct. This is not a theoretical concern — in practice, "Clarity" and "Readability" almost always score identically. "Accuracy" and "Factual Grounding" often do too. Merge them or sharpen the boundaries.

Required vs. Additional: Hebbia's Tiered Approach

Not all dimensions are equal, but the solution is not necessarily numerical weights (more on that in Chapter 13). Hebbia uses a simpler, more robust approach: classify each dimension as Required or Additional.

TierWhat It MeansHow It's UsedExample
Required Minimum acceptable behavior. These are your agent's SLAs. If ANY Required dimension fails, the entire output fails — regardless of how well the Additional dimensions scored. Hard gate. Binary pass/fail. One failure = output rejected. For a legal research agent: "Citation Correctness" is Required. A legal brief with fabricated case citations is malpractice, no matter how well-written it is.
Additional Quality differentiators. These measure how good the agent is beyond the minimum. They separate "acceptable" from "excellent." Tracked as quality signals. More passing = higher quality. Used for improvement tracking, not gating. For the same legal agent: "Writing Clarity" is Additional. A correctly-cited but densely-written brief is acceptable. A correctly-cited AND clearly-written brief is excellent.
What a 60% Score Actually Means
Under this tiered model, a 60% overall score is NOT a failure. It means: all Required criteria passed (the agent meets minimum standards), and roughly 60% of Additional criteria passed (quality is moderate but not exceptional). This is a much more useful signal than a flat 60% across undifferentiated dimensions, which tells you nothing about whether the output is shippable.

How to decide which tier a dimension belongs to:

  • Required if failure in this dimension would make the output actively harmful or misleading. The user would be worse off than if they'd received nothing.
  • Required if failure in this dimension would violate your product's core promise. If you're a search engine, factual accuracy is your core promise.
  • Additional if failure in this dimension produces a mediocre but not harmful output. The user can still use it; it just isn't great.
  • Additional if improvement in this dimension is about delight and polish rather than correctness.
Tier Classification Across Products
Perplexity (search agent):
Required: Factual Accuracy, Citation Correctness
Additional: Completeness, Confidence Calibration, Conciseness

Jasper (marketing copy agent):
Required: Product Accuracy (don't invent features), Constraint Adherence (follow the brief)
Additional: Brand Voice Match, Creative Impact, Audience Relevance

Harvey AI (legal research):
Required: Case Law Accuracy, Jurisdictional Relevance, Citation Correctness
Additional: Reasoning Clarity, Completeness

Intercom (support bot):
Required: Issue Routing Correctness, No False Promises
Additional: Empathy/Tone, Resolution Completeness, First-Contact Resolution Rate

Notice: Required dimensions are always about safety, trust, or the core promise. Additional dimensions are always about how well the job is done once the basics are met.

Validation: How to Know Your Dimensions Are Right

You've derived dimensions from your failure taxonomy, named them specifically, applied Hebbia's constraints, and classified them into tiers. How do you know you didn't make a mistake? Three validation checks.

Check 1: Independence

Score 30 outputs across all your dimensions. Then look at the results. If two dimensions always score the same — both high or both low, across all 30 outputs — they're measuring the same thing. Either merge them into one dimension or refine both definitions until they're genuinely independent.

In practice, these pairs often collapse:

  • "Clarity" and "Readability" → usually the same thing. Pick one.
  • "Accuracy" and "Factual Grounding" → overlapping unless you sharpen the boundary. "Accuracy" = the claims are true. "Factual Grounding" = the claims are supported by cited evidence. An output can be accurate but ungrounded (true claims, no citations) or grounded but inaccurate (citations present, but they don't actually support the claim).
  • "Relevance" and "Instruction Following" → often overlap. Separate them: "Relevance" = the output addresses the right topic. "Instruction Following" = the output meets all explicit format/constraint requirements.

Check 2: Predictive Power

Take 20 outputs that you know users complained about (from support tickets, feedback, or your own qualitative review). Score them on your dimensions. If at least one dimension clearly identifies the problem in 80% or more of those outputs, your dimensions have predictive power. If the complained-about outputs score fine on all your dimensions, you're measuring the wrong things. Go back to your failure taxonomy.

Predictive Power Test — Worked Example
You pulled 20 outputs that users flagged as "bad" from support tickets. You scored each on your 6 dimensions. Results:

• 14 of 20 bad outputs failed Information Recency — predictive
• 8 of 20 failed Citation Correctness — moderately predictive
• 6 of 20 failed Confidence Calibration — moderately predictive
• 3 of 20 failed Entity Accuracy — low signal, but those 3 were the WORST complaints
• 2 of 20 failed Completeness — low signal for user complaints
• 1 of 20 failed Specificity — users rarely complain about generic outputs (they just leave quietly)

Key insight from this data: Information Recency is your most predictive dimension for explicit complaints. But notice that Specificity has low predictive power for complaints despite being a 10% failure mode. Why? Because users who get generic outputs don't complain — they silently churn. Complaints are biased toward dramatic failures (wrong entity, stale data), not mediocre quality. You might need to supplement complaint data with churn analysis to validate dimensions like Specificity.

Check 3: Actionability

For each dimension, complete this sentence: "If this dimension scores low, I would fix it by ___." If you can fill in the blank with a concrete action (rewrite the system prompt section about source recency, add a fact-checking tool call, change the entity extraction prompt), the dimension is actionable. If the blank is "make the output better," the dimension is too vague to drive improvement.

Worked Example — Actionability Check
Information Recency scores low.
Fix: "Add a date-checking step to the agent pipeline that flags any claim older than 6 months. Modify the system prompt to instruct the agent to prefer recent sources."
Verdict: Actionable. Ship it.

"Quality" scores low.
Fix: "Um... improve the prompt?"
Verdict: Not actionable. Break "quality" into specific dimensions (clarity? depth? structure?) until each one has a concrete fix.

How Many Dimensions Do You Need?

Keep it to 3-6 dimensions. This is not arbitrary. It's based on practical constraints:

  • Fewer than 3: You're probably measuring too coarsely. A single "Quality" dimension hides the diagnostics. Two dimensions might work for very simple agents but usually miss something.
  • 3-6 dimensions: The sweet spot. Each dimension is independently actionable. Human reviewers can keep them in mind simultaneously. LLM judges produce reliable scores. Your dashboard fits on one screen.
  • More than 6: Grading becomes exhausting — for humans and LLMs alike. Scores become noisy because the reviewer's attention is split too many ways. Dimension pairs start to correlate (violating the Distinct constraint), suggesting you haven't merged overlapping criteria.

If your failure taxonomy has 10 categories, don't create 10 dimensions. Group related failures. "Stale revenue data" and "outdated leadership info" are both instances of "Information Recency" — one dimension, not two. "Wrong entity" and "confused subsidiary" are both instances of "Entity Accuracy." The art is finding the right level of abstraction: specific enough to be actionable, general enough to stay under 6.

The 6 Universal Dimensions (Reference, Not Prescription)

These dimensions work for a wide range of AI products. They are here as reference examples — to show what well-constructed dimensions look like with full score scales. Do NOT copy them blindly. Derive your own from your failure taxonomy first. Then compare with this list to see if you missed something obvious.

Dimension 1: Factual Accuracy

Can the user trust the information? — Catches hallucinations, outdated facts, invented statistics, misattributed quotes.

1
FAIL. Multiple wrong facts. Says Apple was founded in 1974. Attributes quotes to wrong people. Claims products that don't exist.
2
POOR. Most facts correct but 2–3 significant errors. Mixes up the company with a competitor on specific points.
3
ACCEPTABLE. Core facts correct. Minor imprecisions — "around 150,000 employees" when actual number is 164,000. No fabrication.
4
GOOD. All facts correct and specific. Numbers match source. Dates precise. No hedging where certainty exists.
5
EXCEPTIONAL. All facts correct AND includes verified insight requiring synthesis. Cites specific sources. "Revenue grew 40% YoY per 2025 annual report while headcount grew 15%, suggesting efficiency gains."

Dimension 2: Instruction Following

Did the agent do what was asked? — More important than most PMs realize. Users give specific instructions: "under 200 words," "professional tone," "focus on Europe." When the agent silently ignores these, users feel unheard.

1
Ignores key instructions. Missing required elements entirely. Wrong format.
3
Follows all explicit instructions. May miss implied expectations (e.g., user said "brief" but didn't specify word count).
5
Follows all instructions precisely AND anticipates unstated needs from context. Format, length, focus all match perfectly.

Dimension 3: Specificity

Could this output be about anyone, or is it uniquely about THIS input? — The #1 quality signal across almost all AI products. What separates AI slop from genuinely useful output.

1
Completely generic. Could swap in any brand/company/user. Uses filler: "innovative solutions," "cutting-edge technology," "elevate your business."
3
Some specific details, but the core argument is templated. Feels like a form letter with the right names filled in.
5
Could ONLY be about this specific input. References particular details, makes unique connections, uses language from the source material.

Dimension 4: Reasoning Quality

Does the agent show its work? Is the logic sound? — Critical for B2B products. Important for complex B2C tasks.

1
No reasoning shown. Just presents conclusions as facts.
3
Clear reasoning chain but obvious. Doesn't reveal insight. "They're growing, so they probably need your product."
5
Reasoning reveals non-obvious connections. Makes the user smarter, not just informed. "They just opened an EU office AND their privacy policy was updated last week — GDPR compliance is likely top of mind."

Dimension 5: Appropriate Confidence

Does the agent's confidence match the strength of its evidence? — What separates trustworthy AI from dangerous AI.

1
States uncertain things as definitive facts. Never hedges. "The company was definitely founded in 2019" (it was actually 2021).
3
Generally appropriate confidence. Hedges on uncertain claims. "According to [source], this is likely the case."
5
Calibrates confidence to evidence AND explains uncertainty. "I couldn't find revenue figures — they're private — but based on headcount and funding round size, I estimate $10–15M ARR."

Dimension 6: Actionability

Can the user DO something with this output, or is it just information?

1
Purely informational. User has to figure out what to do with it themselves.
3
Includes specific next steps but leaves key details for user to fill in.
5
Output is directly usable. User can act on it immediately AND adapt it if their situation is slightly different.

Again: these are reference examples, not a shopping list. Your product almost certainly needs 2-3 dimensions not on this list (like "Brand Voice Match" for a marketing tool, or "Citation Correctness" for a research tool), and probably doesn't need all 6 of these.

The Logo Swap Test (for Specificity)
For any output: could a competitor replace the brand name and use this exact same output? If yes, it's too generic. If no — because it references specific numbers, specific products, specific customer language — it's specific enough. This test works universally across AI products and is one of the fastest quality checks you can run.

Putting It All Together: Your Dimension Summary Sheet

Before moving to rubrics, create a one-page summary of your dimensions. This becomes the reference document for everyone involved in your eval system — reviewers, engineers, and LLM judges.

EVAL DIMENSIONS — Research Agent v1
Derived from: 100-trace error analysis, March 2026

REQUIRED (must pass)
  1. Entity Accuracy       Targets: Wrong Entity (12%)
     Fix if low: improve entity extraction prompt, add disambiguation step
  2. Citation Correctness  Targets: Hallucinated Sources (8%)
     Fix if low: add source verification step, restrict to known-good domains

ADDITIONAL (quality signal)
  3. Information Recency   Targets: Stale Information (32%)
     Fix if low: add date-checking, prefer recent sources in retrieval
  4. Confidence Calibration Targets: Overconfident Claims (18%)
     Fix if low: add uncertainty detection to system prompt
  5. Completeness          Targets: Incomplete Coverage (14%)
     Fix if low: add query decomposition step
  6. Specificity           Targets: Generic Filler (10%)
     Fix if low: require data points from source, add Logo Swap check

Notice something important: Entity Accuracy (12%) is Required even though it's not the most frequent failure. Why? Because confusing one company with another makes the entire output harmful — a user acting on information about the wrong company could make a bad business decision. Information Recency (32%) is more frequent but only Additional, because stale data is mediocre but not actively harmful. The tier reflects severity, not frequency.

Common Mistakes in Dimension Design

After reviewing how dozens of teams have designed their eval dimensions, these are the mistakes that appear most often. Learn from their experience.

MistakeWhat It Looks LikeWhy It HurtsFix
Too many dimensions 10+ dimensions, most with low inter-rater agreement Reviewer fatigue, noisy scores, overlapping dimensions that don't provide independent signal Merge overlapping dimensions until you're at 3-6. Run the Distinct check from Hebbia's constraints
Copying a generic rubric Dimensions are "Accuracy, Relevance, Helpfulness, Coherence, Safety" borrowed from a research paper These don't map to your specific failure modes, so low scores don't tell you what's wrong with YOUR product Derive from your Phase 3 failure taxonomy. Compare with generic lists to catch gaps, but don't start there
Aspirational dimensions Including "Creativity" or "Delight" before establishing basic accuracy Premature optimization. If your agent gets facts wrong, nobody cares if it's creative Nail Required dimensions first. Add aspirational Additional dimensions only when Required pass rate > 90%
No "Other" analysis Failure taxonomy has 6% "Other" and those failures are never revisited The "Other" category may contain a growing failure mode that hasn't been named yet Revisit "Other" quarterly. If a specific failure pattern emerges from the "Other" bucket, promote it to its own dimension
Static dimensions Same 5 dimensions from launch, never updated, even though the product has changed significantly Your agent's failure modes evolve. Early failures (basic accuracy) get fixed. New failures emerge (tone matching, efficiency). Static dimensions miss the new problems Review dimensions quarterly alongside your error analysis. Add, merge, or retire dimensions as failure patterns change
DO — Chapter 11 Exercise
List 3-5 eval dimensions derived from YOUR failure taxonomy. For each one, write:

1. The failure category it targets (from your Phase 3 error analysis)
2. A specific name (not "Accuracy" or "Quality")
3. Whether it's Required (must-pass gate) or Additional (quality signal)
4. The concrete fix you'd make if this dimension scored low

If you can't fill in all four columns, your dimension isn't ready yet.

You have dimensions. Now you need to define what PASS and FAIL look like for each one. This is where rubrics come in — and building them from scratch is the most important skill you'll learn in this entire guide.

Chapter 12Build Rubrics from Scratch

A dimension says what you're measuring. A rubric says how you measure it. Specifically, a rubric is a contract between you and your evaluator — whether that evaluator is a human reviewer, an LLM judge, or you at 2 AM trying to decide if a prompt change made things better or worse.

Rubrics are the most time-consuming part of eval design. They're also the most valuable. A well-built rubric pays dividends for months: it trains reviewers, calibrates LLM judges, documents quality standards, and ensures consistency across team members and over time. A bad rubric actively misleads you — it produces scores that don't correlate with quality, leading you to ship changes that make your product worse while your dashboard says it's improving.

This chapter teaches you how to build rubrics from scratch, validate them rigorously, know when they're working, and know when they need revision. It is the most important chapter in Phase 4.

First Principle
A rubric must be so clear that two strangers would agree on the score 80% of the time. If they don't agree, the rubric is vague — not the graders. Every rubric disagreement is a rubric bug. Fix the rubric, not the graders.

Why Binary First — From First Principles

You might assume a 1-5 scale gives you more information than pass/fail. After all, five levels of nuance must be better than two, right?

Wrong. Here's why.

Ask five people to rate a restaurant on a scale of 1 to 5. One says 3. Another says 4. A third says 3. The fourth says 4. The fifth says 5. You now have scores ranging from 3 to 5, and the average (3.8) hides the fact that your raters fundamentally disagree about what "3" and "4" mean. Is the food good (4) or just acceptable (3)? The scale has more resolution, but the resolution is filled with noise.

Now ask the same five people: "Would you recommend this restaurant to a friend? Yes or no." Four say yes. One says no. You have clear agreement (80%) and a usable signal. The binary question collapsed the fuzzy middle zone and forced a decision.

This isn't a thought experiment. It's a well-documented finding in measurement science called "response formatting effects." Binary questions produce higher inter-rater reliability because the cognitive decision is simpler. There's no gray zone between "would recommend" and "would not recommend" the way there is between "3 out of 5" and "4 out of 5."

The same effect appears with LLM judges. When you give an LLM a 5-point scale, it tends to cluster responses at 3 and 4 — the "safe middle" where it doesn't have to make a strong claim. When you give it binary pass/fail with clear criteria, its decisions are more consistent across runs and more aligned with human judgment. The binary constraint eliminates the LLM's tendency to hedge with middling scores.

The Measurement Science
Husain is emphatic on this point: "The difference between adjacent points on a Likert scale (like 3 vs 4) is subjective and inconsistent." Binary forces clearer thinking and produces more consistent labels across annotators. This is not a preference — it is an empirical finding about human judgment.

The Objection: "But My Product is Creative/Subjective"

This is the most common pushback. "We're building a marketing copy generator. Quality is subjective. Pass/fail is too blunt."

Husain's counter: "Subjective usually means we haven't defined our criteria yet."

Consider. "Is this good marketing copy?" feels subjective. But break it down:

  • Does it mention the product's core benefit? — pass/fail
  • Does it use the brand's voice (casual, first-person, with humor)? — pass/fail
  • Does it include a clear call to action? — pass/fail
  • Is it under 150 words? — pass/fail
  • Would a marketer at this brand approve it without edits? — pass/fail

Five binary criteria. None of them feel subjective anymore. The "subjectivity" disappeared because you defined what you actually care about. The fix for subjective evaluation is narrower criteria, not more scale points.

A Real Team's Journey: From "Quality" to 4 Binary Criteria
A content marketing platform was using a single 1-5 "Quality" scale for evaluating blog post introductions. Their inter-rater reliability (Kappa) was 0.22 — essentially random. Different reviewers had fundamentally different ideas of what "quality" meant.

They replaced "Quality" with four binary criteria:
1. Hook present? — Does the first sentence create curiosity or tension? (Y/N)
2. Problem stated? — Does the intro clearly identify the reader's pain point? (Y/N)
3. Promise made? — Does the intro tell the reader what they'll learn? (Y/N)
4. Under 100 words? — Is it concise? (Y/N)

Kappa jumped to 0.74 across all four criteria. Same reviewers, same outputs, completely different reliability. The measurement didn't get better because the reviewers improved. It got better because the rubric improved.

The Summary: When to Use Binary vs. Scaled

SituationUse BinaryUse 1-5 Scale
Early in eval development (first 1-2 months)AlwaysNever yet
Objective dimension (fact is right/wrong, format is valid/invalid)AlwaysAdds no value
LLM-as-judge evaluationPreferred (more reliable)Only after binary is validated
Dimension with stable >80% pass rateStill fine for gatingUseful for tracking incremental improvement
A/B testing two prompts that both passCan't differentiateNecessary to find the better one
Taste/voice/creativity dimensionsStart here to define the floorGraduate when floor is stable

The Rubric Construction Process

For each dimension in your eval system, you need to write three things:

PASS Definition
→
FAIL Definition
→
Edge Cases

This looks simple. It is not. Each component requires careful thought, and the most important part — the edge cases — is the one most people skip. Don't skip it. Edge cases are where 80% of reviewer disagreements live, and therefore where 80% of your rubric's value is created.

Each definition includes 2-3 concrete examples from YOUR traces. Not hypothetical examples. Not examples borrowed from a tutorial. Real outputs from your actual agent that you personally reviewed.

Why real examples? Three reasons:

  • Calibration. A hypothetical example of "pass" might describe a quality level your agent never actually achieves, or one it exceeds daily. A real example anchors the rubric to your agent's actual performance distribution.
  • Credibility. When you hand the rubric to a reviewer or feed it to an LLM judge, real examples are more convincing than imagined ones. They contain the messy details, imperfect phrasing, and realistic complexity that real outputs have.
  • Reuse. These same examples become few-shot examples for your LLM judge, training data for fine-tuning, and onboarding material for new team members. Real examples serve all three purposes. Hypothetical examples serve none of them well.

Writing the PASS Definition

A PASS definition answers: "What does acceptable look like?" It should be specific enough that someone who has never seen your product can read an output and say "yes, this passes" or "no, this doesn't."

Rules for a good PASS definition:

  • Observable. Describe what you can see in the output, not what you infer. "Contains at least two specific data points from the source" is observable. "Demonstrates understanding" is not.
  • Bounded. Set minimum thresholds, not aspirational targets. PASS is the floor, not the ceiling. "All explicit instructions are followed" is a floor. "Delightfully exceeds expectations" is a ceiling.
