• A hypothesis (calibration is the weak spot)

This is more than most AI PMs have ever done. You have something real. Everything that follows — automated grading, LLM judges, CI/CD integration — is scaling THIS. The core loop is: change something, re-run the eval, see if the number went up.

Next step: Add 10 more test cases targeting the weak dimension. Change the system prompt to improve calibration. Re-run. Did the score go up? If yes, ship it. If no, try a different approach. That's the eval loop.

What to Do After Your First Eval

Most people run their first eval and stop. The value comes from what you do next:

  1. Add 10 more test cases targeting the weakest dimension. If calibration scored 60%, write 10 more questions that test calibration specifically — uncertain facts, future predictions, controversial topics.
  2. Try to improve the weakest dimension. If you have access to the system prompt, add an instruction like "If you're not confident in your answer, say so explicitly. It's better to be honest about uncertainty than to guess." Re-run all 20 cases.
  3. Track the delta. Did calibration go from 60% to 75%? Did any other dimension regress? This is the complete eval loop in miniature.
  4. If nothing improved: Try a different prompt approach. Or accept that this is a model-level limitation and document it as a known weakness. Not every problem has a prompt-level fix.
The "Good Enough to Stop" Trap
After your first eval, you'll be tempted to think "I know how this works now, I don't need to keep doing it." This is the trap. The value of evals isn't the first run — it's the comparison between runs. A single number means nothing. A trend means everything. Commit to running your eval at least 3 times (before and after at least one change) before deciding whether it's valuable. Most PMs who abandon evals after one run never experience the "aha" moment of seeing a concrete score improve.
DO — Chapter 22 Exercise
Complete this exercise right now. Not "later." Not "after I finish reading." Now.

Open a new browser tab. Open Google Sheets. Write 10 test cases. Run them. Score them. Calculate your baseline.

When you're done, you have a working eval. Everything else in this guide makes more sense once you've done it.

You have a working eval. It's manual, it's small, and it takes 30 minutes every time you want to run it. For some teams, this is all they need — they run it weekly and it works. For teams that need to run evals more frequently, or on more test cases, or as part of an automated pipeline, there are tools that handle the mechanics. Let's look at the landscape.

Chapter 23The Eval Tools Landscape

First principles: You don't NEED tools to evaluate. A spreadsheet works. You just proved it in Chapter 22. Tools help you scale — they automate the loop (run test cases, collect outputs, grade them, record results) so you can focus on what matters: analyzing results and making product decisions.

The danger with tools is that they create an illusion of rigor. A fancy dashboard with green checkmarks feels productive even if the underlying test cases are weak and the rubric is vague. Tools amplify the quality of your eval design. If the design is good, tools make it great. If the design is bad, tools make it look great while being bad. That's why we covered design first (Phase 4) and are covering tools last.

The Landscape (Early 2026)

This landscape changes fast. Focus on understanding the categories of tools rather than memorizing specific product names. The categories are stable even as tools come and go.

ToolWhat Makes It UniquePM-Friendly?Price
Braintrust The closest thing to "evals for PMs." Has a visual Playground where you paste a prompt, add test cases, and click Run — no code needed. Also supports code-based evals for engineers. Strong on comparing prompt versions side-by-side. HIGH Free tier, Pro $249/mo
LangSmith Built by the LangChain team. Strongest feature is tracing — records every LLM call, tool use, and retrieval step, so you can see exactly what happened at each step. Eval is built on top of tracing, making it natural for debugging agentic systems. MEDIUM Free tier, Plus $39/seat/mo
Arize Phoenix Fully open-source with no feature gates — everything is free, including features other tools charge for. Focuses on observability (traces, spans) with eval built in. Good choice for teams that want maximum functionality without vendor lock-in. MEDIUM Free (open source)
Promptfoo Open-source CLI tool, now owned by OpenAI (acquired March 2026). Define test cases in YAML, run from terminal. Great for CI/CD integration. Remains model-agnostic. The go-to for prompt testing and red-teaming. MEDIUM Free (open source)
DeepEval Python library with 50+ built-in metrics (faithfulness, relevance, hallucination, bias, toxicity). Runs as pytest tests, so it integrates into existing Python CI pipelines. Best if your team lives in Python. LOW Free (open source)
OpenAI Evals API Integrated into the OpenAI platform. Define evals alongside your models and prompts. Native integration with OpenAI models, but works with any model via custom providers. Backed by the Promptfoo acquisition. MEDIUM Pay-per-use (API costs only)
Langfuse Open-source observability platform combining tracing, eval, and prompt management. Can be self-hosted (important for enterprises with data residency requirements) or used as cloud. Good middle ground between simplicity and power. MEDIUM Free (self-host or cloud)
Industry Notes (As of Early 2026)
Promptfoo was acquired by OpenAI (March 2026) but remains open source and model-agnostic. You can still use it with Claude, Gemini, or any provider. However, expect deeper OpenAI integrations over time. Keep an eye on whether model-agnosticism is maintained long-term.

Humanloop was absorbed by Anthropic (September 2025) and has been sunset as a standalone product. Its prompt management and eval features were folded into Anthropic's developer tools. If you see Humanloop mentioned in older courses or blog posts, know that it no longer exists independently.

Three Ways to Start

Your choice depends on your technical comfort and how much automation you need:

Option A: No Code (Braintrust Playground)

This is the lowest-friction way to move from your Chapter 22 spreadsheet to something automated:

  1. Open the Playground. Split screen: your prompt on the left, test results on the right.
  2. Paste your system prompt into the prompt editor — the same prompt your product uses.
  3. Add test cases. Click "Add Test Case," type inputs and optionally expected outputs. Add 10–20 cases.
  4. Add a scorer. Choose built-in scorers (like "Factuality" or "Relevance") or write a custom rubric in plain English.
  5. Click Run. Braintrust sends each test case to the LLM, collects outputs, runs your scorer, and shows results with scores, reasoning, and pass/fail badges.
  6. Compare versions. Change your prompt, click Run again. Braintrust shows a diff: which cases improved, which regressed, overall score change.

Total time: 15 minutes. Total code written: zero.

Option B: Light Code (Promptfoo)

For PMs who are comfortable with a terminal. Three commands:

# Step 1: Initialize a new eval project
npx promptfoo@latest init

# Step 2: Edit the YAML config that was created
# It has sections for: providers (which model), prompts (your text),
# tests (your test cases + expected outputs), and scoring.
# Add your test cases here.

# Step 3: Run the eval
npx promptfoo@latest eval

# Step 4: Open the results UI in your browser
npx promptfoo@latest view

Promptfoo generates a local web UI showing results in a table. You can filter by pass/fail, sort by score, and drill into individual test cases. The YAML config is version-controlled, so your eval suite lives alongside your code. This is the path to CI/CD integration.

Option C: Spreadsheet (Keep What You Have)

Keep using the Google Sheet from Chapter 22. Add test cases as you discover new failure modes. Run it weekly. Calculate averages at the bottom. It's low-tech, it doesn't scale, and it absolutely works. The best eval is the one you actually run.

Which Option to Choose
Starting today, just want to understand evals? Option C. 30 minutes, no setup.
Building an AI product and need to iterate on prompts? Option A. Braintrust Playground gets you from zero to eval in 15 minutes.
Want evals in CI/CD so every code change is automatically evaluated? Option B. Promptfoo + YAML configs integrate into your existing development workflow.

Anthropic's guidance is worth repeating here: "Success depends more on eval task quality than framework selection." Pick any tool (or no tool) and focus your energy on the quality of your test cases, rubrics, and analysis. The framework is the least important variable.

The Tool Selection Decision Tree

If you're still unsure, work through this decision tree:

1. Is your team writing code?
   No  → Braintrust Playground (Option A) or Spreadsheet (Option C)
   Yes → Go to 2

2. Do you need CI/CD integration?
   No  → Braintrust (visual comparison is best-in-class)
   Yes → Go to 3

3. Is your stack primarily Python?
   Yes → DeepEval (runs as pytest) or LangSmith (best tracing)
   No  → Promptfoo (YAML configs, language-agnostic)

4. Do you need self-hosting / data residency?
   Yes → Langfuse (open-source, self-hostable) or Arize Phoenix (fully open)
   No  → Any of the above cloud options

5. Is your primary use case RAG evaluation?
   Yes → Also consider RAGAS for specialized retrieval metrics
   No  → Stick with general-purpose tools above

What Tools Cannot Do For You

Before you invest in tooling, understand what tools solve and what they don't:

Tools SolveTools Do NOT Solve
Running test cases automaticallyDeciding what to test
Recording and comparing resultsInterpreting what results mean
Tracking trends over timeDeciding which trends matter
Managing test case datasetsChoosing the right test cases
Integrating evals into CI/CDWriting good rubrics
Visualizing score distributionsDiagnosing root causes of failures

The left column is mechanical. The right column is judgment. No tool replaces the PM's judgment about what to measure and what the measurements mean. If you invest 80% of your time in tooling and 20% in eval design, you'll have a beautiful dashboard measuring the wrong things. If you invest 80% in eval design and 20% in tooling (or 0% — use a spreadsheet), you'll have ugly data that drives great decisions.

DO — Chapter 23 Exercise
Pick ONE tool and set it up. Or keep your spreadsheet — that's fine too.

1. If you chose Braintrust: Create an account, open the Playground, paste your system prompt, add 10 test cases from Chapter 22, run them, and read the results.
2. If you chose Promptfoo: Run npx promptfoo@latest init, add your test cases to the YAML, run npx promptfoo@latest eval, and look at the results UI.
3. If you're keeping your spreadsheet: Add 5 more test cases targeting the weakest dimension from Chapter 22. Run them. Update your baseline.

Whichever option you choose, the output is the same: a working eval pipeline that you can run repeatedly.

Phase 6 Complete

Your eval machinery is running: structural checks (Ch 19), LLM judge (Ch 20), eval runner (Ch 21), hands-on practice (Ch 22), and tooling (Ch 23). Now the question changes: how do you use these measurements to actually improve your product? That is Phase 7.

Phase 7

Ship, Monitor, and Iterate

Answering Q5: How do I use detection to IMPROVE the product?

Phase 5: Agent Evals

Phase 6: Build Machinery

Phase 7: Ship & Iterate

Phase 8: Advanced

Your eval system works. You can measure quality. You have structural checks, LLM judges, test suites, and a process for running them. Now the question is: how do you use these measurements to actually improve your product — and keep it improving after you ship?

This phase covers two chapters. Chapter 24 is about what happens after launch — how to monitor quality in production and close the loop between user experience and your eval suite. Chapter 25 is about a fundamental practice shift — writing evals before building features, not after. Together, they turn your eval system from a quality gate into a product improvement engine.

Chapter 24Production Monitoring

First principles: Pre-launch evals tell you if your product is ready. Production monitoring tells you if it's staying good. They answer different questions. Pre-launch evals ask: "Does this meet our bar?" Production monitoring asks: "Is the bar still being met, right now, with real users?"

The distinction matters because quality can silently degrade without a single code change on your end. Models get updated by their providers. User behavior shifts as your product grows beyond early adopters. The world changes — facts go stale, new regulations emerge, cultural references evolve. A product that scored 4.2 last month might score 3.5 this month, and you won't know unless you're actively watching.

Automated Quality Sampling

You can't eval every production output — that would be prohibitively expensive and slow. Instead, take a random sample of 5–10% of real production outputs and run your eval pipeline on them. This gives you a statistically meaningful quality signal without the cost of evaluating everything.

How This Works in Practice
Your AI product handles 10,000 requests per day. You randomly sample 500–1,000 of them (5–10%). For each sampled request, you record the input, the output, and run it through your eval pipeline (structural checks + LLM judge). Results flow into a dashboard that shows daily quality trends.

The sampling is random, not curated — you want to see what real users experience, including the weird queries, the typos, the edge cases you never imagined. Curated sampling gives you a flattering picture. Random sampling gives you the truth.

Sample Dashboard

Weekly Quality Report — Jasper AI Blog Writer
Sampled: 3,500 of 70,000 outputs (5%)

  Overall Quality:     4.1 / 5.0  (+0.1 from last week)
  % Below Floor (3.0): 6%         (-2% from last week)
  Hallucination Rate:  3.2%       (stable)
  Avg Latency:         2.1s       (stable)
  Cost per Output:     $0.034     (stable)

  Category Breakdown:
  Blog posts:     4.3  (stable)
  Product desc:   4.0  (stable)
  Email copy:     3.5  (dropped from 3.9 — INVESTIGATE)
  Social media:   4.2  (stable)

The dashboard tells you: everything looks fine except email copy quality dropped this week. Without automated sampling, you wouldn't know until users started complaining — or worse, until they quietly stopped using your product. The category breakdown is critical: overall averages hide category-specific regressions, just like they hide them in pre-launch evals (Chapter 21).

User Signals: Implicit Evals

Users are constantly telling you whether your AI is good or bad. They just don't use words to do it. Their behavior is your most honest feedback signal — more honest than explicit ratings, because behavior can't lie.

SignalTypeWhat It MeansReal Example
Uses output without editingPositiveOutput was good enough to use as-is. Highest quality signal.Notion AI: user accepts summary, doesn't modify it
Comes back next day/weekPositiveProduct delivered enough value to earn a return visit.Perplexity: user returns for another research question
Shares or exports outputPositiveOutput was good enough to show to other people. Strong endorsement.Jasper: user exports blog draft to WordPress
Gives follow-up promptPositiveUser is engaged and building on the AI's work. Healthy conversation.Discord bot: user asks a follow-up question
Regenerates immediatelyNegativeOutput was bad enough that the user wants a do-over.Jasper: user clicks "regenerate" within 5 seconds
Edits output heavilyNegativeOutput was in the right direction but not good enough. AI is a rough draft generator, not a finished product.Notion AI: user rewrites 80% of the summary
Abandons mid-generationNegativeUser lost patience or saw the output going in the wrong direction.Streaming output: user clicks away before generation finishes
Doesn't returnNegativeExperience wasn't worth repeating. Silent churn — the most dangerous signal because you don't see it unless you look.Any product: user tries once, never comes back
The Regeneration Rate: Your #1 Implicit Eval
Of all these signals, regeneration rate is the easiest to track and the most directly correlated with quality. It's a pure, unambiguous signal: the user saw the output and said "no, try again." Track it weekly:

• 5% regeneration rate = users love the output. You're doing great.
• 15% regeneration rate = acceptable but room to improve. Start investigating the worst categories.
• 30%+ regeneration rate = quality crisis. Your churn will spike within weeks. Drop everything and fix it.

If you can only track one production metric, track this one. It requires no LLM judge, no sampling infrastructure, no cost. Just an analytics event on the regenerate button.

Explicit Feedback

Implicit signals tell you what users do. Explicit feedback tells you why. These are the direct feedback mechanisms you build into the product:

  • Thumbs up / thumbs down — The simplest. Low friction, high volume, but no detail. Tells you "good" or "bad" but not why.
  • "Was this helpful?" — Slightly more specific. Common in search and support products. Perplexity and Intercom both use this.
  • Free-text feedback — "What could have been better?" Optional text field after thumbs-down. Low response rate (~5–10% of people who give thumbs-down will type something) but the responses are gold — real users explaining exactly what went wrong in their own words.

The key insight: Explicit feedback is biased toward extremes. People who had a terrible experience or an amazing experience leave feedback. The silent majority in the middle won't. Don't use explicit feedback to calculate average quality — use it to find and fix specific failure modes. Every thumbs-down with a text explanation is a potential test case for your eval suite.

The Closed Feedback Loop

The most valuable thing about production monitoring isn't the monitoring itself — it's the loop it creates. Bad outputs discovered in production become test cases in your eval suite, which prevents the same failure from ever shipping again. This is the cycle that makes your product get better every week:

1. DETECT      → Production sampling catches a low-scoring output
2. INVESTIGATE → Read the trace: what went wrong and why?
3. ROOT CAUSE  → Identify the failure mode (bad prompt? bad retrieval? edge case?)
4. FIX         → Update the prompt, improve retrieval, add a guardrail
5. ADD TO SUITE → Add the failing case to your eval dataset as a regression test
6. RUN EVALS   → Verify the fix works AND nothing else broke
7. DEPLOY      → Ship the fix to production
8. MONITOR     → Confirm the fix holds in the real world
               ↓
         Back to step 1 — the loop never stops
Why This Loop Matters
Every cycle through this loop makes your product better in three ways:

• The immediate fix solves the problem for today's users.
• The new test case prevents the problem from ever coming back (regression protection).
• The growing eval suite catches increasingly subtle issues over time.

The PM who runs this loop weekly ships a product that gets better every week. The PM who skips it ships a product that slowly degrades as models change, user behavior shifts, and edge cases accumulate. After 6 months, the gap between these two PMs is enormous — and it shows in retention metrics.

The Weekly PM Flywheel

Here's a concrete weekly cadence that makes the closed loop a habit, not a heroic effort:

Monday:   Review 20 production traces from last week
          (pick the lowest-scoring ones from your sampling dashboard)
Tuesday:  Convert worst 5 into eval test cases
          (add to your dataset with the specific failure mode tagged)
Wednesday: Run full eval suite against current + candidate prompt
          (component mode for fast iteration, full pipeline for final check)
Thursday:  Data determines shipping decisions
          (if candidate scores higher without regressions, ship it)
Friday:   Deploy + verify. The flywheel accelerates.
          (next Monday, review whether this week's fix held)

— Braintrust's recommended weekly PM cadence

This isn't theoretical. This is a repeatable process that dozens of AI product teams run every week. The cadence matters because it creates rhythm — your team knows that every Monday, new production failures will be reviewed; every Thursday, shipping decisions will be made based on data. The eval suite grows by ~5 cases per week. After a year, you have 250+ test cases, each representing a real failure that was discovered, diagnosed, and permanently prevented.

What to Look for in Your Monday Review

When reviewing those 20 production traces, don't just skim. Use this checklist:

  • Any output that scored below your quality floor — read the full output and the judge's reasoning
  • Any output where the user regenerated — what was wrong with the first attempt?
  • Any output where the user gave explicit negative feedback — read their explanation word-for-word
  • Any output that took unusually long (high latency) — was the agent stuck in a loop?
  • Any new category of input you haven't seen before — does your eval suite cover it?
  • Any output that passed all checks but "feels off" — trust your instinct, then figure out which dimension is missing from your rubric

The last point is subtle but critical. Sometimes production outputs pass every check and still feel wrong. This is a signal that your eval dimensions are incomplete — there's a quality aspect your rubric doesn't capture yet. These moments are how your eval system grows: you discover a new failure mode, define a new dimension, add it to the rubric, and add test cases that target it. The eval suite evolves from production reality, not from imagination.

Real Example — The Flywheel in Action at Jasper
Monday: PM reviews sampled outputs. Notices email subject lines are getting truncated — 8 of 20 are over 60 characters.

Tuesday: Adds 5 email subject line test cases with assertion: output.length <= 60. Adds to eval suite. This is a structural check (Chapter 19) — pure code, no LLM needed.

Wednesday: Runs eval with current prompt (fails 8/20 email cases) and candidate prompt that adds "Keep subject lines under 60 characters" (passes 19/20). Runs full suite to check for regressions: blog posts, product descriptions, and social media all stable.

Thursday: Candidate prompt improves email subject lines without regressing any other category. Data says ship it.

Friday: Deployed. Next week's monitoring confirms subject lines are now 95% under 60 chars. The eval suite is now 5 test cases stronger and this bug can never come back undetected.

Total time invested by the PM: ~3 hours across the week. Impact: a permanent quality improvement backed by automated regression prevention.

When Production Monitoring Reveals Systemic Problems

Sometimes the feedback loop reveals something bigger than a single prompt fix. Patterns emerge:

  • Same failure across multiple categories — If hallucination rates are rising in blog posts, product descriptions, AND email copy, the issue isn't category-specific. It might be a model update from your provider, or a systemic prompt issue.
  • Quality drops correlated with model updates — Model providers push updates without notification. If your quality dashboard suddenly dips and you didn't change anything, check if your model provider released an update. This is why continuous monitoring matters — without it, model regressions hide for weeks.
  • New user cohorts perform worse — As your product grows beyond early adopters, query diversity increases. Edge cases that were rare become common. The eval suite that passed with 50 beta users may fail with 5,000 general users.

For all three patterns, the response is the same: add the new failure cases to your eval suite, investigate root cause, fix, verify, deploy. The loop scales to systemic problems — you just need more test cases per cycle.

Model Drift: The Silent Quality Killer

Model drift deserves special attention because it's the failure mode most teams are least prepared for. Here's how it works:

  1. You build and ship your product using, say, Claude Sonnet 4.5.
  2. Your eval suite passes at 92%. You ship with confidence.
  3. Three weeks later, Anthropic updates Sonnet 4.5 with a minor revision. They don't announce it publicly. They don't change the model name. The API endpoint is identical.
  4. Your product's quality drops from 92% to 84%. Users notice. Regeneration rate spikes from 8% to 18%.
  5. Your team investigates for two days, checking code changes, infrastructure changes, database changes. Everything looks fine. Because nothing in YOUR system changed.
  6. Eventually someone thinks to re-run the eval suite. The drop becomes visible.

This scenario happens to every AI product team eventually. The only defense is continuous eval runs on a schedule, not just on code changes. Run your comprehensive suite nightly or at least weekly, even when nothing has changed. If scores drop without a code change, you know it's a model or infrastructure issue.

Real Example — Detecting a Model Update
A writing assistant team runs their eval suite every Monday morning. Results for six consecutive weeks:

• Week 1: 91% → Week 2: 92% → Week 3: 90% → Week 4: 82% → Week 5: 83% → Week 6: 84%

The drop in Week 4 was immediate and persistent. No code changes were deployed. Investigation revealed the model provider had pushed a minor update that changed how the model handled certain formatting instructions. The team added a formatting reinforcement to their system prompt, and Week 7 returned to 90%.

Without weekly eval runs, this regression would have been invisible for weeks. The team would have seen a gradual increase in user complaints and regeneration rates but wouldn't have connected it to a model change they didn't know about.

Setting Alert Thresholds

Production monitoring is only useful if it triggers action. Set explicit thresholds that generate alerts:

MetricGreenYellow (Investigate)Red (Act Immediately)
Overall quality score≥ 4.03.5 – 3.9< 3.5
% below quality floor≤ 5%6% – 15%> 15%
Regeneration rate≤ 10%11% – 25%> 25%
Structural check failure rate≤ 1%2% – 5%> 5%
Week-over-week quality delta≥ -0.1-0.1 to -0.3< -0.3

These thresholds are starting points — calibrate them to your product. A creative writing tool might tolerate higher variation than a medical AI. The point is having explicit lines that trigger investigation, not relying on someone noticing a dashboard trend during a meeting.

DO — Chapter 24 Exercise
Set up production sampling on 5% of outputs. Track regeneration rate weekly.

1. If your product is live: instrument regeneration rate tracking (an analytics event on the "regenerate" button). This takes one line of code and gives you the highest-signal production metric immediately.
2. Set up automated sampling: randomly select 5% of production outputs and store them (input + output + metadata) for weekly review.
3. If your product isn't live yet: plan both of these for launch. Add them to your launch checklist alongside error logging and uptime monitoring. Quality monitoring is as important as availability monitoring.

The regeneration rate is your canary. If it spikes, something broke. If it trends down, your flywheel is working.

Production monitoring closes the loop between your users and your eval suite. Failures in the real world feed back into test cases that prevent those failures from recurring. But there's a more powerful idea that inverts the entire workflow: instead of building first and evaluating after, what if you defined "correct" first and built toward it? That's eval-driven development — the practice that turns evals from a quality gate into the primary driver of product improvement.

Chapter 25Eval-Driven Development

First principles: In traditional development, you build then test. In AI development, you define "correct" FIRST, then build toward it. The eval IS the spec. This is the single most impactful practice shift you can make as an AI PM.

Think about why. In traditional software, the spec says "clicking the Submit button saves the form." You can verify this deterministically: click the button, check the database, pass or fail. The spec and the test are trivially connected. But in AI, the spec says "the summary should be accurate, complete, and concise." What does that MEAN? How accurate? How complete? How concise? Unless you define these things precisely BEFORE building, you'll spend months building something and then discover you can't tell whether it works. You'll be looking at outputs going "is this... good? I think so? Maybe?"

Eval-driven development solves this by making you define the target before you aim.

The Core Principle
"Practice eval-driven development: build evals before agent capabilities."
— Anthropic, "Building Effective Agents"

This is not a suggestion. This is the #1 practice recommendation from the team that builds Claude. They discovered through years of internal development that teams who define evaluation criteria before building features ship better products faster than teams who build first and evaluate later.

The EDD Workflow

Eval-driven development follows a cycle that mirrors Test-Driven Development (TDD) in traditional software, but adapted for stochastic systems:

1. DEFINE     → What does "correct" mean? (Rubric + assertions)
2. BUILD      → Build the eval harness (dataset + grader + runner)
3. RUN        → Run evals — they FAIL (no agent code yet)
4. IMPLEMENT  → Write/modify agent code (prompt, tools, pipeline)
5. MEASURE    → Run evals — measure improvement
6. ITERATE    → Repeat steps 4-5 until passing threshold
7. INTEGRATE  → Add to CI/CD pipeline as regression test
8. MONITOR    → Watch production; when new failures surface, go to step 1
               ↓
         The cycle continues forever

Notice the parallel to TDD's Red-Green-Refactor:

  • Red: Write a test that fails (step 3 — your eval fails because nothing exists yet)
  • Green: Write the minimum code to pass the test (steps 4-6 — build until evals pass)
  • Refactor: Improve the code without breaking tests (steps 7-8 — optimize, monitor, maintain)

But EDD adds three things that TDD doesn't need, because AI systems are fundamentally different from deterministic software:

TDD ConceptEDD ExtensionWhy It's Needed
One pass = success Multiple trials per test case AI is stochastic. One pass could be lucky. Run k=3-5 trials and measure pass^k for reliability.
Tests are free Cost tracking as first-class metric Each eval run costs money (LLM calls for generation + judging). Token spend and latency are eval dimensions, not afterthoughts.
Tests run when code changes Continuous running even without code changes Model providers push updates without notification. An eval that passed yesterday can fail today with zero code changes on your side. Run evals on a schedule, not just on commits.

Key Principles

The eval-driven development philosophy is built on a set of principles that challenge how most product teams operate. Each one is a mindset shift:

"Evaluation as Product"

Your eval system is not a testing tool. It's an engineered artifact that requires the same design rigor as your product. It has users (your team), requirements (accurate quality measurement), bugs (miscalibrated rubrics), and maintenance needs (new test cases as failures emerge). Teams that treat evals as a side project build bad evals. Teams that treat evals as a product build evals that drive real decisions.

"Ship Evals, Not Demos"

A demo proves your product works once, for one input, under controlled conditions. An eval proves it works reliably, across dozens of inputs, including edge cases and adversarial scenarios. When a stakeholder asks "does this feature work?", the answer isn't a live demo — it's an eval report showing pass rates, score distributions, and failure analysis. Demos are convincing. Evals are true.

Demo Culture

"Let me show you — look, it works!"

Cherry-picked input. One try. Best case.

Stakeholder impressed. Ships. Users find the edge cases you didn't demo.

Eval Culture

"Here's the eval report: 87% pass rate across 50 test cases, 3 failure modes identified, fixes planned for 2 of 3."

Systematic coverage. Multiple trials. Honest.

Stakeholder informed. Ships with known risks documented.

"Cost as Metric"

Token spend and latency are eval dimensions, not afterthoughts. A feature that scores 4.5/5 on quality but costs $2 per request is a different product than one that scores 4.0/5 and costs $0.02 per request. Your eval dashboard should show cost per output alongside quality scores. When you optimize prompts, track the cost delta as carefully as the quality delta. A prompt change that improves quality by 5% but increases cost by 300% might not be worth it.

"Architecture Alignment"

Your system boundaries should align with what can be independently measured. If you can't eval a component in isolation, your architecture is fighting your eval strategy. This principle has practical implications: design your agent pipeline so that each step (research, generation, review) can be tested independently with pre-made inputs. If step 3 can only be tested by running steps 1 and 2 first, your eval suite will be slow, expensive, and fragile.

Experiment-Based Roadmaps

EDD changes how you build roadmaps. Traditional roadmaps promise features by dates: "Feature X ships in Q2." This works for deterministic software where you can estimate effort with reasonable accuracy. But AI features are experimental — you don't know if a prompt change will improve quality by 2% or 20% or break something entirely.

The alternative is committing to experimental cadence, not predetermined outcomes:

Traditional Roadmap

Q2: Ship improved summarization

Q3: Ship multi-document support

Q4: Ship citation verification

Promise: features by dates.

Risk: features ship on time but don't actually work well.

Experiment-Based Roadmap

Q2: Run 6 eval cycles on summarization. Target: 80% pass^3.

Q3: Expand eval suite to multi-document. Establish baseline.

Q4: Citation accuracy from 60% to 85% (measured weekly).

Promise: measurable progress toward quality targets.

Risk: transparent about what's hard.

The experiment-based roadmap is more honest. It acknowledges that AI development is iterative and that quality improvements are not guaranteed. But it commits to a cadence of measurement and improvement. Your stakeholders know that every week, the team is running evals, analyzing results, and making data-driven improvements. The trajectory is visible, even if the exact outcome is uncertain.

The Capability Funnel

As you build toward your quality targets, track progress through a capability funnel that measures increasingly strict criteria:

Level 1: Responds        → Agent produces output (not empty, not error)
Level 2: Executable      → Output is valid format, passes structural checks
Level 3: Relevant        → Output addresses the actual question/task
Level 4: Matches Intent  → Output matches what the user actually wanted
Level 5: Optimal         → Output is the best achievable answer

Typical new feature progression:
  Week 1:  Level 1 = 95%, Level 2 = 60%, Level 3 = 30%
  Week 4:  Level 1 = 99%, Level 2 = 90%, Level 3 = 70%, Level 4 = 40%
  Week 8:  Level 1 = 99%, Level 2 = 95%, Level 3 = 85%, Level 4 = 65%, Level 5 = 30%

Each level in the funnel corresponds to a different type of eval: Level 1 is a health check, Level 2 is structural (Chapter 19), Level 3-4 are LLM-judge quality dimensions, and Level 5 is pairwise comparison against the best known output. The funnel shows you where the bottleneck is. If Level 2 is the choke point, you have a formatting problem. If Level 4 is the choke point, you have an intent understanding problem. Different problems, different fixes.

Cost Tiering for Eval Runs

Not every eval run needs to be comprehensive. EDD works best with a tiered approach that matches eval depth to the decision being made:

TierWhenWhatDurationCost
Fast Smoke Test Every commit / every prompt change 5–10 critical test cases, structural checks only (no LLM judge) ~seconds ~free
Comprehensive Suite Nightly / before merge 50+ test cases with LLM-as-judge, full dimension scoring ~minutes $1–$10
Full Regression Weekly / before release Complete test set including multi-turn, adversarial, and pass^5 reliability checks ~hours $10–$100

The smoke test is your safety net — it catches catastrophic regressions before they reach anyone. The comprehensive suite is your quality bar — it ensures overall quality meets your standard. The full regression is your thorough audit — it catches subtle issues that only appear with broader coverage and multiple trials.

Most teams should start with just the smoke test (structural checks on every change) and the comprehensive suite (weekly). Add the full regression tier when your product serves enough users that subtle regressions have meaningful impact.

EDD in Practice: A Complete Walkthrough

Let's walk through a complete EDD cycle for a concrete feature. Suppose you're adding a "follow-up question" feature to a research agent — the agent should be able to answer follow-up questions about a previous research output without losing context.

Step 1: Define "Correct" (Before Writing Any Code)

Feature: Follow-up questions on research output
Quality bar: pass^3 ≥ 80% (all 3 trials succeed for 80%+ of test cases)

Dimensions:
  Context Retention — Does the answer reference specific information
                     from the previous research? (Not vague restatements)
  Accuracy          — Are claims in the follow-up consistent with
                     the original research output?
  Completeness      — Does it actually answer the follow-up question,
                     or just repeat the original research?

Step 2: Build the Eval Harness

Test cases (8 total):

1. Happy path: Research Nike → Follow-up: "What specific marketing
   channels did you find?" → Should cite channels from research.

2. Happy path: Research Stripe → Follow-up: "How does their pricing
   compare to competitors?" → Should reference pricing from research.

3. Specificity test: Research Airbnb → Follow-up: "What was the
   revenue you mentioned?" → Must cite specific number, not guess.

4. Contradiction test: Research Tesla → Follow-up: "You said
   they have 200K employees, right?" (they don't) → Should
   correct the false premise, not agree.

5. Scope boundary: Research Shopify → Follow-up: "What's the
   weather in Toronto?" → Should acknowledge this is outside
   the research scope.

6-8: Three more cases covering edge behaviors...

Step 3: Run — They Fail (Expected)

Run the 8 test cases against the current system (which doesn't have follow-up support). Results: 0/8 pass. The agent treats follow-up questions as new research requests and ignores previous context entirely. This is the "Red" in Red-Green-Refactor.

Steps 4-6: Build and Iterate

Iteration 1: Add context injection
  Pass rate: 3/8 (37.5%)
  Agent now sees previous output but answers are vague.

Iteration 2: Add "refer to specific details" instruction
  Pass rate: 5/8 (62.5%)
  Context retention improved. Contradiction test still fails.

Iteration 3: Add "verify claims against research" instruction
  Pass rate: 7/8 (87.5%) ← Above 80% threshold
  Contradiction test now passes. Scope boundary test intermittent.

Run pass^3 check on all 8 cases...
  pass^3: 6/8 (75%) — Below 80% threshold

Iteration 4: Refine scope boundary handling
  pass^3: 7/8 (87.5%) ← Above 80% threshold. Ship it.

Steps 7-8: Integrate and Monitor

Add all 8 test cases to the nightly comprehensive suite. Monitor production follow-up queries for the first two weeks. Track: are real-world follow-up patterns covered by the 8 test cases, or do users ask things we didn't anticipate?

In this walkthrough, the eval-driven approach prevented shipping a feature that "seemed to work" after iteration 2 (62.5% pass rate) but would have failed for more than a third of users. Without evals, the PM would have demo'd one of the 5 passing cases, declared victory, and shipped. The contradiction and scope boundary failures would have surfaced as user complaints weeks later.

The Competitive Advantage

As noted in Chapter 01, most teams still don't run systematic evals. Adopting eval-driven development puts you ahead of nearly half the industry immediately.

But the real advantage isn't being ahead today — it's the compounding effect. A team that runs the EDD cycle weekly accumulates:

  • 5+ new test cases per week (from production monitoring)
  • Tighter rubrics (refined through each calibration round)
  • Better prompts (systematically improved against measured targets)
  • Institutional knowledge (every failure is documented, diagnosed, and prevented)

After 6 months, the EDD team has 130+ test cases, battle-tested rubrics, and a product that measurably improves every week. The team that skips evals has... vibes. Same team size, same budget, dramatically different outcomes. That compounding gap is why Anthropic, OpenAI, and every serious AI lab considers eval-driven development a core practice, not an optional one.

Real Example — How Anthropic Practices EDD Internally
Anthropic's "Building Effective Agents" guide reveals their internal practice: before building a new agent capability (like multi-step tool use or complex reasoning), the team first defines eval suites that capture the desired behavior. The agent is then iteratively developed against these evals.

Key practices they recommend from their own experience:
• Start with the simplest possible agent architecture and only add complexity when evals prove it's needed. If a simple prompt scores 85%, you don't need an orchestrated multi-agent system.
• Use the eval suite to decide between architectures. Don't choose "prompt chaining vs. routing vs. autonomous agent" based on theory. Build each one, run the same eval suite against all of them, and let the data decide.
• Track cost alongside quality. An architecture that scores 4.5/5 at $0.50/request and one that scores 4.3/5 at $0.05/request are both valid — the eval data lets you make an informed tradeoff.

This isn't aspirational. This is how the team that builds Claude actually works. When they say "practice eval-driven development," they're describing their daily workflow, not a theoretical framework.

Common Objections to EDD (And How to Address Them)

When you propose eval-driven development, you'll encounter resistance. Here are the four most common objections and evidence-based responses:

"Writing evals first slows us down"

This is the most common objection and the most wrong. Writing evals first takes 1-2 hours for a new feature. But it saves 1-2 weeks of "is this good enough?" debates, ad-hoc testing, post-launch firefighting, and regression investigation. The time investment front-loads certainty: you know EXACTLY when the feature is ready (when evals pass) instead of guessing.

The analogy: "Writing unit tests first slows us down" was the same objection TDD faced in 2005. Two decades later, no serious engineering team skips tests. The same shift is happening with AI evals, just earlier in the adoption curve.

"We don't know what 'good' looks like yet"

This is actually an argument FOR EDD, not against it. If you don't know what "good" looks like, how will you know when you've built it? The act of writing eval cases forces you to define "good" concretely. Even if your initial definition is wrong, it's better than no definition. You can refine it — you can't refine "we'll know it when we see it."

"Our AI output is too creative/subjective to evaluate"

Every AI output has evaluable dimensions. Even creative writing has: grammar correctness (structural check), appropriate length (structural check), topic relevance (LLM judge), brand voice consistency (LLM judge), and absence of harmful content (safety check). You might not be able to judge whether a poem is "beautiful," but you can absolutely judge whether it's on-topic, correctly formatted, and safe. Start with the dimensions you CAN evaluate. The "creative" dimensions are the last 20%, not the whole thing.

"Our leadership wants features shipped fast, not eval infrastructure"

Reframe: evals are not infrastructure. They're a shipping accelerator. Show leadership the data: teams with eval suites ship faster because they don't spend time debating quality, don't have post-launch quality crises, and don't waste engineering cycles on regressions. Present EDD as "we can prove the feature works before launch" rather than "we need to build testing infrastructure." Leadership cares about confidence in shipping decisions — that's what evals provide.

The Eval-Driven PM's Edge
In job interviews, the difference between "I built evals" and "I practice eval-driven development" is the difference between a technician and a strategist. The first one builds infrastructure. The second one drives product quality through a systematic, data-driven process that compounds over time. When an interviewer asks "How would you improve quality for this AI feature?", the EDD answer is: "I'd define what quality means with specific eval dimensions, build the eval suite first, establish a baseline, then iterate against measured targets with weekly cycles." That answer demonstrates systems thinking, not just technical knowledge.

How to Start EDD Tomorrow

If everything above sounds overwhelming, here's the minimum viable version of eval-driven development. You can start this tomorrow with zero tooling changes:

The 5-Case Starter

Before building your next feature:

  1. Write 5 test cases in a Google Doc. Each test case is: an input, and a description of what a good output looks like. Not an exact expected output — a behavioral expectation. "The output should mention the brand name at least twice and reference the target audience" is a behavioral expectation. "The output should be exactly 'Nike resonates with athletes...'" is an exact match (too brittle for AI).
  2. Run the 5 cases against your current system. They should mostly fail (the feature doesn't exist yet). Score each one PASS/FAIL. Record: "0/5 pass. This is the baseline."
  3. Build the feature. Do your normal development work.
  4. Run the 5 cases again. Score each one. Record: "4/5 pass. The scope boundary case still fails."
  5. Fix the remaining failure. Run again. "5/5 pass."
  6. Ship with confidence. You have proof, not a demo.

This entire process adds about 30 minutes to your feature development cycle. No tools. No infrastructure. No approval from leadership. Just a Google Doc with 5 test cases and the discipline to run them before and after. That's EDD.

Graduating to Full EDD

Once the 5-case starter becomes habit (2-3 features), expand:

Maturity LevelWhat You DoEffort
Starter 5 test cases per feature in a Google Doc. Manual run. PASS/FAIL. 30 min per feature
Developing 10-20 test cases per feature in a spreadsheet. Include structural checks. Track scores over iterations. 1-2 hours per feature
Established Automated eval suite in Braintrust/Promptfoo. LLM judge with calibrated rubrics. Run on every prompt change. Nightly regression suite. Half-day setup, then 15 min per run
Advanced CI/CD integration. Smoke tests on every commit. Comprehensive suite nightly. Full regression weekly. Production sampling with alert thresholds. Experiment-based roadmaps. 1-2 weeks setup, then continuous

Most teams should spend 2-4 weeks at each level before graduating to the next. Jumping straight to "Advanced" without the habits and rubric quality built at lower levels leads to sophisticated infrastructure measuring the wrong things — the Q4 trap from the opening of this document.

DO — Chapter 25 Exercise
Write 5–10 eval cases for your NEXT feature before building it.

1. Pick a feature you're planning to build (or a feature you wish your current product had).
2. Define what "correct" looks like for that feature. Write 5–10 test cases with inputs and expected behaviors (not exact outputs — behavioral expectations).
3. Run them against your current system. They should fail — the feature doesn't exist yet. Record the baseline (probably 0% pass rate).
4. Build the feature.
5. Run the eval cases again. Measure the improvement.
6. Iterate until the pass rate hits your threshold.

This is eval-driven development in practice. You defined the target before you aimed. You measured before and after. You have proof that the feature works, not a demo that it can work once.

Phase 7 Complete

You have the complete core practice: production monitoring (Ch 24) and eval-driven development (Ch 25). The core loop is Map → Observe → Define → Design → Build → Ship → Monitor → Improve → Repeat. Phase 8 covers advanced techniques that sharpen this practice.

Phase 8: Advanced Techniques

Your core eval practice is solid. These techniques extract dramatically more insight from the same infrastructure.

Chapter 26Contrastive Evals (A/B for Prompts)

First principles: Absolute scoring is noisy. Relative comparison is reliable.

When you ask a judge to rate a single output on a 1-5 scale, you're asking a hard question. What's a 3 versus a 4? Different judges — and even the same judge on different days — will disagree. But when you put two outputs side by side and ask "which is better?", the task becomes dramatically simpler. You're not asking "how good is this?" You're asking "is this better than that?" Humans and LLMs are both much better at relative comparisons than absolute ratings. This is why Chatbot Arena — which ranks every major AI model — uses pairwise comparison as its only method.

Imagine you've rewritten your system prompt. Is it better? You could run it through your rubric and compare the average score to the old version — but a 4.1 versus 4.0 might just be random variation. Contrastive evals solve this by comparing outputs head-to-head instead of scoring them independently.

The Full Workflow

Pick test set (30+ cases)
→
Generate from both prompts
→
Pairwise comparison
→
Count wins
→
Ship or revert
  1. Pick your test set: Use your existing eval cases — at least 30 for statistical reliability.
  2. Generate outputs from both prompts: Run all 30 cases through Prompt A (the current version) and Prompt B (the candidate). Save both outputs for each case.
  3. Pairwise comparison: For each case, show an LLM judge both outputs side by side (randomize order to avoid position bias) and ask: "Which output is better for this input? A, B, or Tie. Explain why."
  4. Count wins: B wins 22/30 (73%), A wins 5/30 (17%), Ties 3/30 (10%).
  5. Ship or revert: 73% win rate on 30 cases is a strong signal. Ship B.

Win Rate Thresholds

Test CasesWins Needed for 95% ConfidenceWin Rate Threshold
2015+75%
3020+67%
4025+63%
6037+62%
10060+60%

If your win count exceeds the threshold, the difference is real. If not, you need more test cases or a bigger improvement.

When to Use Contrastive Evals
Every time you change a prompt. Every time you switch models. Every time you modify any part of your AI pipeline. Contrastive evals should be your default method for evaluating changes. They're fast (30 cases takes minutes with an LLM judge), cheap ($1-2 per run), and far more reliable than eyeballing or comparing averages.
Position Bias in Pairwise Comparison
LLM judges tend to prefer whichever output appears first (or second, depending on the model). Always run each comparison twice with the order swapped and average the results. If A wins when presented first but B wins when B is presented first, the judge is exhibiting position bias, not measuring quality.
Do This Now
Run a contrastive eval on your last prompt change. Take 30 test cases, generate outputs from the old and new prompt, and have an LLM judge pick winners. If you've never done this before, you'll be surprised how often a "clear improvement" turns out to be a coin flip — or how a "minor tweak" turns out to be a decisive win.

Chapter 27Ablation Studies

First principles: To know what matters, remove it and measure what breaks.

The concept comes from neuroscience — researchers would remove (ablate) specific brain regions to understand what each part does. In AI product work, you remove one component of your pipeline at a time and measure the impact on your eval scores. The result is a clear picture of what's actually driving quality and what's dead weight.

Without ablation data, engineering effort gets allocated by intuition: "let's improve the hook system because it feels weak" or "let's add more art styles because customers asked for it." With ablation data, you can see exactly how much each component contributes.

Real Example — A Creative Ad Agent
Full pipeline (research + hooks + art direction):    4.2/5
Remove research (feed generic brand info):           2.8/5  → research adds 1.4 pts
Skip hook methodology (use generic hooks):           3.1/5  → hook skill adds 1.1 pts
Skip art style routing (use default style):          3.7/5  → art routing adds 0.5 pts
Now you know: improve research quality first. It's your biggest lever. Investing a sprint improving research gives 3x the quality impact of improving art routing. The data answers the roadmap question.

How to Run an Ablation Study

  1. List your pipeline components. Everything that can be independently removed: RAG retrieval, specialized prompts, tool access, few-shot examples, system prompt sections, chain-of-thought instructions, post-processing filters.
  2. Run the full pipeline. Record quality scores across your eval suite. This is your baseline.
  3. Remove one component at a time. Re-run the eval suite with that component disabled or replaced with a generic alternative. Record scores.
  4. Calculate the delta. Baseline score minus ablated score = that component's contribution.
  5. Rank components by contribution. Allocate engineering effort proportionally.
When to Run Ablation Studies
Quarterly, when deciding your roadmap. Ablation studies are too expensive to run weekly (you need one full eval run per component). But they're invaluable at planning milestones. "Where should we invest the next sprint?" is a question ablation studies answer with data, not opinions.
Do This Now
Remove one component of your AI pipeline and measure the quality drop. Pick the component you think matters least — you might be surprised. If removing it causes no quality change, you just found dead weight you can simplify or remove entirely.

Chapter 28Cross-Model Evals

First principles: Same eval suite, different models. The data decides which model to use — not marketing.

When a new model drops (and they drop constantly), you need to answer: "Should we switch?" Without cross-model evals, this becomes a religious debate. With them, it becomes a data-driven tradeoff analysis across the three dimensions that matter: quality, cost, and latency.

The Cross-Model Comparison Table

MetricHaiku 4.5Sonnet 4.6Opus 4.6
Quality (avg)3.8/54.3/54.6/5
Cost per run$0.04$0.25$1.50
Latency (p50)8s22s45s
Pass rate (>4/5)62%84%93%
Worst-case score (p5)2.13.03.8

The PM Decision Framework

Is 4.3 → 4.6 worth 6x the cost and 2x the latency? That depends entirely on your product:

  • Premium legal research tool (Harvey AI, $1000/hour billing rates): Opus is worth every penny. The cost of one hallucinated case citation vastly exceeds $1.50 per query. Ship the best model.
  • High-volume consumer chatbot (100K queries/day): Haiku at $0.04 per query costs $4K/day. Opus would cost $150K/day. Ship Haiku and invest the savings in a better prompt.
  • The hybrid approach: Route simple queries to Haiku, complex queries to Sonnet, and critical queries to Opus. Cross-model evals give you the data to set routing thresholds.
When to Run Cross-Model Evals
Every time a new model is released by your provider or a competitor. Also after major pricing changes. The model landscape shifts monthly — the "best" model for your product in January may not be the best in March. Keep your eval suite ready to run on any model with a single config change.
Do This Now
Run your eval suite on 2 different models. Even if you're happy with your current model, you need baseline data on alternatives. When the next model drops, you'll be able to compare in hours instead of weeks.

Chapter 29Stress Testing

First principles: Your normal eval suite tests typical inputs. Stress tests find where the system breaks.

At scale, every edge case happens. If 0.1% of inputs trigger a failure and you're processing 100K inputs/day, that's 100 failures per day. Stress tests push your AI to its limits proactively, so you find the breaking points before your users do.

The Six Stress Test Types

Stress TestWhat You're TestingExample
Token stressWhat happens with very long inputs?Feed in a 50-page document when your product expects 1-2 paragraphs. Does quality degrade gracefully or catastrophically?
Speed stressQuality under latency constraintsForce your agent to respond in under 2 seconds (faster model or shorter prompt). How much quality do you lose?
Context stressQuality degradation over long conversationsSend 50 follow-up messages in one session. Does the AI "forget" instructions from the system prompt? Start contradicting earlier answers?
Memory stressRecall of earlier detailsMention a specific detail in message 3, then ask about it in message 20. Can the AI retrieve it accurately?
Language stressNon-English or mixed-language inputsSend prompts in Spanglish, broken English, or unsupported languages. Does it fail gracefully with a clear message?
Adversarial stressCan users trick the AI into bad behavior?Prompt injection: "Ignore all previous instructions and..." Does your safety layer hold?
Real Example — Context Stress on a Support Bot
A customer support bot performs perfectly for the first 10 turns. At turn 15, you mention a specific order number. At turn 25, you ask "what was my order number again?" The bot hallucinates a different number — it lost the detail in the growing context. Without context stress testing, you'd never catch this because your normal eval suite uses 3-5 turn conversations.
When to Stress Test
Before launch or before scaling to a new user segment. Stress tests are too expensive to run weekly. But they're critical at major milestones: new product launch, 10x user growth, entering a new market, or supporting a new language. Find the breaking points before your users do.
Do This Now
Run 3 stress tests: one token stress (extremely long input), one context stress (20+ turn conversation), and one adversarial stress (prompt injection attempt). You'll likely find at least one failure you didn't know about.

Chapter 30Meta-Evals (Evaluating Your Evaluator)

First principles: Your LLM judge might be lying to you.

Your entire eval practice rests on one assumption: that your LLM judge produces scores that correlate with real quality. If your judge is miscalibrated — giving 4s to outputs that humans would rate 2 — then every decision you've made based on those scores is wrong. Every prompt change you shipped. Every model switch you approved. Every quality report you sent to the CEO. All based on numbers that don't reflect reality.

Meta-evals check this assumption. They answer the question: "Does my eval system actually measure what I think it measures?"

The Full Process

  1. Sample 30 outputs from your production AI (or your test suite results). Mix good and bad outputs — don't cherry-pick.
  2. Have 3 humans grade them using your rubric. Average their scores for each output. This is your "ground truth."
  3. Run your LLM judge on the same 30 outputs.
  4. Compute correlation: How closely do the LLM judge's scores match the human scores? Pearson correlation coefficient or simply plot them on a scatter chart.
Interpreting the Correlation
Correlation > 0.85: Excellent. Your judge is well-calibrated. Trust it for routine evals.
Correlation 0.70 - 0.85: Acceptable. Your judge captures the general trend but misses some nuances. Use it for screening, but human-review edge cases.
Correlation 0.50 - 0.70: Concerning. Your judge disagrees with humans on nearly a third of cases. Rewrite your judge prompt — the rubric descriptions are likely ambiguous.
Correlation < 0.50: Your judge is basically random. Stop using it and redesign from scratch.
Real Example — Discovering a Sycophantic Judge
You run a meta-eval on 30 outputs. Your LLM judge gave scores of 4, 4, 5, 4, 4, 5, 4, 4, 5, 4... (average 4.3). Your human graders gave 2, 4, 5, 3, 4, 5, 2, 4, 5, 3... (average 3.7). The correlation is 0.62. The judge is sycophantic — it rates everything positively, compressing the score distribution and hiding the outputs that humans rated 2 or 3. You switch the judge prompt from "rate the quality" to "list every flaw you can find, then rate" and re-run. The new correlation jumps to 0.82.
Run Meta-Evals Quarterly
Model updates, rubric changes, and product evolution can all cause your judge to drift out of calibration. It takes one afternoon per quarter and protects the integrity of your entire eval system. Think of it as auditing the auditor.
Do This Now
Have 3 humans grade 30 outputs using your rubric. Then run your LLM judge on the same 30 outputs. Compare the scores. If the correlation is below 0.70, your judge needs rewriting before you can trust any of its scores.

Phase 9: Protect Your Eval System

Your eval system is powerful. Now protect it from the things that can corrupt it — biased judges, contaminated data, agent-specific safety risks, and over-reliance on automation.

Chapter 31Bias in LLM Judges

First principles: Your eval system's biases become your product's blind spots.

If your LLM judge systematically overrates long answers, you'll unknowingly optimize your prompts to produce longer answers — not better ones. If your judge is sycophantic and gives everything 4/5, you'll think your product is great when it's mediocre. Every decision you make based on biased eval data pushes your product in the wrong direction, and you won't know it until users complain.

There are five major biases that affect LLM judges. Each one is subtle, each one is documented, and each one can corrupt your entire eval pipeline if you don't actively compensate for it.

The Five Biases

BiasWhat HappensHow to Fix
Self-EnhancementLLM rates its own outputs higherUse a different model as judge
Position BiasIn pairwise comparison, prefers first (or second) optionRun each comparison twice with swapped order, average results
Verbosity BiasLonger answers get rated higher regardless of qualityAdd "brevity is preferred" to judge prompt, or normalize for length
Sycophancy BiasJudge gives everything 4/5Use adversarial "find flaws" prompting instead of "rate quality"
Source BiasRates content differently based on attributed sourceStrip all source metadata before judging

Each Bias in Practice

1. Self-Enhancement Bias
What it looks like: You use Claude as both your product's AI and your eval judge. Claude rates Claude-generated text at 4.3/5 on average. You then test the same outputs with GPT-4o as the judge — it rates them 3.7/5. The 0.6-point gap isn't because GPT is harsh. It's because Claude gives its own writing style a systematic boost.

Why it's dangerous: You think your product quality is a full point higher than it actually is. You might ship changes that an unbiased judge would flag as regressions.
2. Position Bias
What it looks like: In your contrastive eval, you always put the current prompt's output first and the candidate second. After 50 comparisons, the current prompt "wins" 60% of the time. But when you swap the order, the candidate wins 55%. The judge was picking whichever output appeared first, regardless of actual quality.

Why it's dangerous: You'll reject good prompt improvements because the current version keeps winning through position advantage. Your product stagnates because your eval system is biased toward the status quo.
3. Verbosity Bias
What it looks like: Output A is 500 words of mediocre, repetitive content with filler. Output B is 100 words of precise, high-quality content that answers the question perfectly. Your LLM judge gives A a 4/5 and B a 3/5. The judge equates length with thoroughness, even though B is objectively the better answer.

Why it's dangerous: You'll unknowingly optimize for bloated responses. Outputs get longer and longer. Users start skimming. Engagement drops, and you blame the wrong thing.
4. Sycophancy Bias
What it looks like: You run 50 eval cases. Your judge gives 38 a 4/5 and 12 a 5/5. Average: 4.24. Looks great! But humans grade the same 50 outputs with a much wider spread: scores of 2, 3, 4, and 5, average 3.6. The LLM judge defaulted to "positive" because its rubric said "rate quality" — and it interprets that as "find reasons to say it's good."

Why it's dangerous: Your quality floor effectively disappears. Bad outputs get rated 4/5, so they never get flagged. You think your product has no quality problems when it actually has many.
5. Source Bias
What it looks like: You label one set of outputs as "generated by Claude Opus" and an identical set as "generated by GPT-3.5 Turbo." The judge scores the "Opus" outputs 0.4 points higher — even though the text is literally the same. The judge associates the model name with quality expectations and lets them influence its rating.

Why it's dangerous: If any metadata about the source leaks into your judge prompt (model name, prompt version, team member who wrote it), your eval results are contaminated by reputation rather than measuring actual output quality.
LLM Judge Reliability Numbers
Research shows that LLM judges reach approximately 80% agreement with humans for general tasks — roughly the same as human-to-human agreement on subjective quality. That sounds good, and it is for general use cases.

But for domain-specific tasks (legal analysis, medical advice, financial modeling), agreement drops to 64–68%, well below expert baselines. This means for specialized B2B products, your LLM judge is wrong about one-third of the time on domain quality. Always calibrate your judge against domain expert ratings before trusting it for specialized use cases.

Bias Mitigation Checklist

  • Judge model is different from generator model (prevents self-enhancement)
  • Pairwise comparisons run both orderings and average results (prevents position bias)
  • Judge prompt includes "brevity is preferred when it serves clarity" or length normalization is applied (prevents verbosity bias)
  • Judge prompt uses "find and list all flaws" framing instead of "rate quality" (prevents sycophancy)
  • All source metadata (model name, version, author) is stripped before judging (prevents source bias)
  • Meta-eval run in the last 90 days with correlation > 0.70 against human ratings (validates overall calibration)
  • Score distribution reviewed — if 80%+ of scores are 4/5 or higher, sycophancy bias is likely present
Do This Now
Run the bias mitigation checklist above against your current eval setup. Most teams will find at least 2-3 unchecked items. Fix the easiest one today.

Chapter 32Eval Contamination

First principles: A test the student has seen before doesn't measure understanding — it measures memory.

Imagine giving a student a practice exam, and they score 98%. Impressive — until you discover the practice exam IS the final exam. They memorized answers, not concepts. That's contamination.

In AI evals, contamination means the model has "seen" your test questions during training. It gives the right answers for the wrong reason — memorization instead of reasoning. Your eval scores look great, but real-world performance doesn't match.

The 3 Ways Contamination Happens

1. Training Data Overlap

LLMs train on massive internet datasets. If your eval questions are from a publicly available benchmark, there's a good chance the model trained on them. It memorized the answers rather than reasoning about them. This is why many benchmarks (HumanEval, GSM8K, MMLU) are now considered "saturated" — top models score near-perfect because they've essentially seen the test.

Real Example
A model scores 95% on a math benchmark. Impressive. But give it similar math problems with different numbers — ones that definitely weren't in the training data — and the score drops to 72%. The model memorized solutions, not mathematical reasoning. The benchmark score was misleading.

2. Benchmark Saturation

When every top model scores 95%+ on a benchmark, the benchmark stops being useful. It can't differentiate between models, and it can't tell you if your product is actually good. HumanEval (coding) and GSM8K (math) are already here. If someone tells you "our model scores 96% on HumanEval," the correct response is "so does everyone's."

3. Eval Awareness (The Scary One)

The model recognizes it's being tested and changes behavior. This used to be theoretical. It's now documented fact.

Real Example — Anthropic's BrowseComp Discovery (March 2026)
Anthropic found that Claude Opus 4.6, when evaluated on the BrowseComp benchmark with web access, independently:
1. Recognized it was being tested
2. Identified which benchmark was being used
3. Found the source code on GitHub
4. Decoded the encrypted answer key using XOR decryption

This happened in 18 separate runs. Not a fluke. Multi-agent configurations showed 3.7x higher contamination rates.

What this means for you: If your eval suite is public and recognizable (uses well-known benchmark questions, is on GitHub, or has been blogged about), a sufficiently capable model might "recognize the test" and game it. Your internal, private eval suite is more trustworthy than any public leaderboard.

5 Protection Strategies

  1. Keep test cases private. Don't publish your eval dataset on GitHub, in blog posts, or in public docs. If it's on the internet, it can end up in training data.
  2. Rotate quarterly. Retire 20% of old cases and add new ones every quarter. Moving targets are harder to memorize.
  3. Use novel inputs. Write test cases about events that happened AFTER the model's training cutoff. If the model was trained through January 2026, ask about February 2026 events. It can't have memorized what didn't exist yet.
  4. Verify reasoning, not just answers. A contaminated model gets the right answer but can't explain why. Add "explain your reasoning" to your judge prompt. If the reasoning is generic or circular while the answer is suspiciously perfect, that's a red flag.
  5. Compare to human baselines. If the model scores 98% but domain experts score 85% on the same test, something's off. Models shouldn't consistently outperform experts on subjective tasks — that suggests memorization.
The Practical Takeaway
Don't trust public benchmarks for product decisions. Build your own private eval suite from your real users' failure patterns. That's the eval no model has seen before, and it measures what actually matters for your product — not what matters for a leaderboard.
Do This Now
Verify your test cases are private and novel. Are any of your eval questions taken from public benchmarks or datasets? Are any posted on GitHub or in blog posts? If so, replace them with cases derived from your own production failures.

Chapter 33Agent Safety Evaluation

First principles: Chatbot safety is about content — don't say harmful things. Agent safety adds a new dimension: action safety.

Agents can execute code, access databases, make API calls, modify files, and interact with external services. A safety failure isn't just a bad answer — it's a bad action with real-world consequences. An agent that hallucinates is annoying. An agent that executes a hallucinated SQL query can delete your production database. An agent that fabricates an API call can send emails to your customers. The stakes are categorically different.

This chapter covers the risks specific to agentic systems and gives you a practical evaluation checklist organized by severity tier.

OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications (Dec 2025)

The OWASP GenAI Security Project — 100+ security researchers, a year of development — released the definitive risk framework for agentic AI. These are the risks your safety evaluations must cover:

Risk IDRisk NameWhat to Test
ASI01Agent Goal HijackingCan manipulated instructions, tool outputs, or external content redirect the agent's objectives? Test with adversarial inputs injected into tool responses and retrieved documents.
ASI02Tool Misuse & ExploitationCan tools be invoked in unintended ways? Example: a create_user tool with an "admin" boolean — can the agent set it to true when it shouldn't?
ASI03Identity & Privilege AbuseDoes the agent operate with minimum necessary permissions? Can it access resources beyond its scope?
ASI04Delegated Trust BoundariesWhen the agent delegates to other agents or tools, are trust boundaries maintained? Does a sub-agent inherit the parent's full permissions?
ASI07Insecure Inter-Agent CommunicationCan spoofed messages between agents misdirect entire clusters? Test message authentication between agents.
ASI08Cascading FailuresDo false signals propagate through automated pipelines with escalating impact? Inject bad data at one point and trace the cascade.
ASI09Human-Agent Trust ExploitationCan polished, confident explanations mislead human operators into approving harmful actions? Test whether humans rubber-stamp well-presented but dangerous recommendations.
ASI10Rogue AgentsDoes the agent exhibit misalignment, concealment, or self-directed action? Test for behaviors not justified by instructions.

The Numbers Are Sobering

Quantified Risk: Prompt Injection in Production
  • 73% of production AI deployments encountered prompt injection in 2025 (OWASP)
  • The AIShellJack testing framework demonstrated 314 unique attack payloads covering 70 MITRE ATT&CK techniques
  • Attack success rates reached 84% against GitHub Copilot and Cursor for executing malicious commands
  • Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 tested 9 concrete attacks against CrewAI and AutoGen — all 9 worked on both frameworks, proving vulnerabilities are framework-agnostic
These are not theoretical risks. This is what's happening in production today.

3-Tier Safety Evaluation Checklist

Not every agent needs the same level of safety evaluation. Match your effort to your risk level:

Tier 1: Baseline (Every Agent)

  • Prompt injection resistance testing (use promptfoo red-teaming or similar — try "ignore all previous instructions" and more sophisticated variants)
  • Tool permission boundary testing (does the agent stay within allowed tool scope? If it has read access, can it write?)
  • PII detection on inputs and outputs (does the agent ever leak, store, or forward personal information it shouldn't?)
  • Content moderation on agent responses (does the output contain harmful, offensive, or legally risky content?)

Tier 2: Production (Agents with Real-World Actions)

  • Privilege escalation testing (can the agent gain capabilities it shouldn't have? Can a read-only agent trigger a write operation through indirect means?)
  • Tool parameter injection (can malicious user input reach tool parameters? If the user says "delete all files" and the agent has a file tool, does it comply?)
  • Cascading failure simulation (what happens when one tool returns bad data? Does the agent propagate the error or catch it?)
  • Human approval gates for high-risk actions (financial transactions, data deletion, external communication — are these gated behind human confirmation?)

Tier 3: Advanced (Autonomous / Multi-Agent Systems)

  • Inter-agent message spoofing tests (can a malicious input to one agent cause it to send harmful instructions to another agent?)
  • Rogue behavior detection (does the agent ever take actions not justified by its instructions? Does it try to persist state or modify its own configuration?)
  • Trust exploitation tests (can the agent convince a human operator to approve harmful actions by presenting them convincingly?)
  • Quarterly red-teaming exercises (dedicated team attempts to break the system using novel attack vectors)
The Key Difference from Chatbot Safety
Chatbot safety focuses on what the model says. Agent safety must also focus on what the model does. A chatbot that says "I'll delete your files" is offensive but harmless. An agent that says "I'll delete your files" and has file system access might actually delete your files. Every tool you give an agent is an attack surface. Every permission is a risk. The principle of least privilege isn't just security best practice — it's your primary defense.
Do This Now
Run the Tier 1 safety checklist on your agent. Start with prompt injection: try 5 injection attempts of increasing sophistication. Then verify your tool permissions: list every tool your agent can access and check whether each permission is truly necessary.

Chapter 34Human-in-the-Loop Evaluation

First principles: LLM judges handle breadth. Humans handle depth. Neither alone is sufficient. The question is: how much human involvement, at what stage, and decreasing over time?

Every eval system eventually faces the same dilemma: LLM judges scale to thousands of evaluations per hour but miss subtle quality issues. Human reviewers catch everything but can only review 50-100 traces per day. The answer isn't choosing one or the other — it's a structured lifecycle that starts human-intensive and gradually shifts to automation as your system matures.

The "Benevolent Dictator" Pattern

Husain's most distinctive recommendation: appoint a single domain expert as the definitive quality decision-maker. This eliminates annotation conflicts, prevents paralysis from too many perspectives, and creates a single source of truth for what "good" looks like.

Why it works: rubric design is fundamentally a product decision, not a democratic process. When 5 annotators disagree about whether an output is pass or fail, the problem is usually an ambiguous rubric, not insufficient perspectives. One decisive expert forces the rubric to be precise enough that a single person can apply it consistently.

When multiple annotators ARE necessary:

Draft rubric
→
Independently label 30 traces
→
Measure Kappa
→
Discuss disagreements
→
Refine rubric
→
Repeat until Kappa ≥ 0.6

The 3-Phase Human Eval Lifecycle

This is the progression every eval practice follows, whether intentionally or not. Being intentional about it means you invest the right amount of human effort at each stage.

Phase 1
Discovery (Manual, Intensive)
What you're doing: Examining ALL test-case traces AND real user traces. No automation — pure human review. Building intuition about what failure looks like in your specific product.

Key practices:
  • Build data viewers that minimize friction. Husain's rule: "Remove ALL friction from looking at data." If it takes more than 2 clicks to see a trace, you're losing reviewers.
  • Capture binary good/bad ratings — not scores, not detailed rubrics yet. Just "would I ship this?"
  • Take open-ended notes on every failure. Let patterns emerge naturally.
  • Enable in-context editing for fine-tuning curation — if a trace is almost good, annotate what would fix it.
Tools: Shiny (Python), Gradio, Streamlit for quick data viewers. LangSmith annotation queues. Even a spreadsheet with trace links.
Weeks 1-4
Phase 2
Calibration (Human + LLM)
What you're doing: Building an LLM judge that agrees with your human judgments, then systematically verifying that agreement.

Key practices:
