  • Use powerful models to generate binary correctness assessments alongside your human labels
  • Track model-human agreement iteratively (spreadsheets work fine initially)
  • Measure precision and recall separately — raw agreement metrics mislead with imbalanced datasets
  • Refine critique prompts until alignment exceeds 90%
The 90% target: Husain's benchmark is clear: automated evaluators must achieve >90% agreement with human judgments on held-out sets. Below that, you don't have an evaluator — you have a coin flip with extra steps.
Month 2-3
Phase 3
Scaled Monitoring (LLM + Spot-Checks)
What you're doing: LLM-as-judge handles breadth. Humans handle depth and edge cases. You review a sample, not everything.

Key practices:
  • LLM judge evaluates 100% of production samples automatically
  • Human reviews 10-20% of traces weekly, prioritized by: low LLM scores, new user segments, and randomly sampled "high-scoring" traces (to catch sycophancy)
  • Group similar sessions for reviewer efficiency
  • Prioritize user journeys that align with core product value
  • Watch for criteria drift quarterly — your rubric may need updating as the product evolves
What humans still catch that LLMs miss: Anthropic's multi-agent eval found that human oversight caught hallucinations and subtle source selection biases (agents preferring SEO-optimized content over authoritative academic PDFs) that automated testing missed entirely.
Ongoing

Anthropic's 5 Complementary Methods

From "Demystifying Evals": automated evals alone are not sufficient. Combine them with these five human-in-the-loop methods:

  1. Production monitoring — observe real-world behavior at scale
  2. A/B testing — validate user outcome differences between versions
  3. User feedback — catch unanticipated issues through thumbs up/down, regeneration signals
  4. Manual transcript review — read full conversations to catch subtle quality issues invisible to automated scoring
  5. Systematic human studies — gold-standard grading for subjective tasks where LLM judges are insufficient

The key insight: "You won't know if your graders are working well unless you read the transcripts and grades from many trials." There is no substitute for actually reading your agent's outputs.

Tools for Human-in-the-Loop Eval

ToolWhat It DoesBest For
LangSmithAnnotation queues with custom rubrics, inline scoring on full sessions, CI/CD integrationTeams already using LangChain
ArgillaOpen-source annotation platform with team workflows, annotation guidelines, and agreement metricsTeams needing multi-annotator workflows
Streamlit / GradioBuild custom data viewers in hours. Show trace, output, context side by side with a Pass/Fail buttonQuick Phase 1 data viewers
Google SheetsTrace links + output + human score columns. Zero setup.Getting started today
Husain's Core Principle
"Remove ALL friction from looking at data." If your trace viewer requires logging into a platform, navigating three menus, and waiting for a page to load — nobody will use it. The best trace viewer is the one that's so easy to use that you actually look at traces daily. Build for habit, not comprehensiveness.
Do This Now
Set up a simple trace viewer. Even a Google Sheet with columns for: trace link, AI output, and your Pass/Fail rating. Review 20 traces this week. Write notes on what fails and why. This is Phase 1 Discovery — and it's the most important phase, because everything else builds on what you learn here.

Phase 10: Scale and Mature

Your eval practice works. Now scale it across your organization.

Chapter 35Eval Governance

As your eval practice matures, you need systems to keep it organized. This matters when team members change, when you need an audit trail, or when someone asks "why did we change the rubric?"

Version Your Rubrics

rubrics/
  v1_2026-01-15.md  — Initial rubric (3 dimensions, pass/fail)
  v2_2026-02-20.md  — Added "Calibration" dimension after error analysis
  v3_2026-03-10.md  — Switched Accuracy from pass/fail to 1-5 Likert
  CHANGELOG.md     — Why each change was made

Why it matters: When scores change, you need to know if it's because the agent changed or the rubric changed. Without version history, you can't tell.

Version Your Test Datasets

datasets/
  v1_20_cases.json   — Initial 20 cases from error analysis
  v2_40_cases.json   — Added adversarial + regression cases
  v3_55_cases.json   — Added production failures from March
  CHANGELOG.md      — What was added/removed and why

Document Decisions

For each rubric change, record:

  • What changed: "Added source grounding as a dimension"
  • Why: "Error analysis showed 22% of failures were unsupported citations"
  • Who decided: "PM (you) after reviewing 50 production outputs"
  • Impact: "Baseline dropped from 4.1 to 3.8 because new dimension is harder"

The Eval Registry

If your product has multiple AI features, maintain a simple registry:

FeatureRubric VersionDataset SizeLast RunScoreOwner
Search answersv355 cases2026-03-254.1/5You
Doc summarizationv230 cases2026-03-204.3/5Alice
Email draftingv120 cases2026-03-153.6/5Bob
Ad campaign agentv240 cases2026-03-284.0/5You

This table becomes your quality dashboard for the entire product. Any stakeholder can look at it and understand the state of AI quality across all features in 30 seconds.

Do This Now
Create v1 of your rubric file and dataset file with changelogs. Even if your rubric is in a Google Doc and your dataset is in a spreadsheet — add a "Version History" section with dates and reasons for changes. Start the governance habit now, before the complexity makes it painful.

Chapter 36Cost Tracking

Evals cost money. Your AI product costs money. Tracking both is part of the eval discipline — because quality without cost context is an incomplete picture.

What Evals Themselves Cost

Running 50 test cases through your agent (Haiku 4.5):
  50 cases × ~2K tokens each = 100K tokens
  At $1/M in + $5/M out ≈ $0.50 per eval run

Running 50 cases through LLM-as-judge (Sonnet 4.6):
  50 cases × ~1K tokens each = 50K tokens
  At $3/M in + $15/M out ≈ $0.75 per judge run

Full pipeline (agent + judge on 50 cases):
  ~$1.25 per eval run

Weekly cadence (50 cases, once a week):
  ~$5/month — less than a coffee

Full pipeline with Opus 4.6 (expensive model):
  50 cases × ~5K tokens = 250K tokens
  At $15/M in + $75/M out ≈ $20 per run
Key Insight
Evals are cheap. Even at the expensive end ($20/run), running evals weekly costs $80/month. Compare that to the cost of shipping a bad prompt that causes 30% regeneration rate for a week. Not running evals is the expensive choice.

Cost Tracking Tools for Your AI Product

ToolWhat It DoesSetup TimePrice
HeliconeChange one URL to start logging all LLM costs. 300+ model pricing database.5 minutesFree tier
LiteLLMUnified API proxy. Budget limits per user/team. Works with 100+ providers.30 minutesOpen source
LangfuseToken/cost tracking built into traces. Breakdowns by feature.1 hourFree (self-host)

The Weekly Cost Dashboard

Track these numbers weekly:
  Cost per request:    $0.04 (trending down 10% from last month)
  Cost per session:    $0.18 (avg 4.5 requests per session)
  Token efficiency:    Output quality 4.1/5 at 1,200 avg tokens
                       (can we get 4.1 at 800 tokens? That's a 33% cost cut)
  Model mix:           85% Haiku, 10% Sonnet, 5% Opus
                       (could we route more to Haiku without quality drop?)
Real Example — Galileo's Luna
Galileo built fine-tuned eval models (Luna) that cost 97% less than using GPT-3.5 as a judge ($175/month for 1M evaluations vs. $6,248). This trend — specialized, cheap eval models — means cost tracking for evals themselves is becoming less important, but cost tracking for your product's AI calls remains critical.
Do This Now
Track cost per request for one week. If you don't have cost tracking set up, start with Helicone (5-minute setup, free tier). Knowing your cost per request is the foundation for every cost-quality tradeoff decision you'll make.

Chapter 37B2C vs B2B Evaluation

B2C and B2B AI products look similar on the surface — both use LLMs, both generate text, both need evals. But the way you evaluate them is fundamentally different because the relationship between user and AI is fundamentally different.

B2C: Delight + Safety

The defining characteristic of B2C AI: your users are not experts in the domain you're serving. A Duolingo user doesn't speak Spanish fluently. A Snapchat My AI user isn't a trained therapist. This creates a dangerous asymmetry: the AI can be confidently wrong and the user has no way to know.

B2C Priority Stack (Duolingo, Spotify DJ, Snapchat AI):
  #1 Safety           (users can't verify, trust is fragile)
  #2 Calibration      (say "I'm not sure" when uncertain)
  #3 Taste/Tone       (emotional experience IS the product)
  #4 Completeness     (users expect "it just works")
  #5 Accuracy         (important but users are more forgiving)

The "show your friend" test: Take the AI's output and imagine the user screenshots it and texts it to a friend. Would the response make them look smart? Would they be proud of it? Or would they be embarrassed? In consumer products, your AI's output becomes the user's output. If they'd hesitate before sharing it, your product has failed.

B2B: Precision + Explainability

The defining characteristic of B2B AI: your users are domain experts who know more about their field than the AI does. A lawyer using Harvey AI has passed the bar exam. A data analyst using Notion AI can spot a wrong formula instantly. These users aren't looking for magic — they're looking for leverage.

B2B Priority Stack (Notion AI, Salesforce Einstein, Harvey AI):
  #1 Accuracy         (experts WILL verify — one error kills trust)
  #2 Reasoning        (need to know WHY, not just WHAT)
  #3 Completeness     (partial work = more work for them)
  #4 Calibration      (flagging uncertainty shows sophistication)
  #5 Taste/Tone       (professional, not flashy)

The "email your boss" test: Take the AI's output and imagine a knowledge worker copying it directly into an email to their boss or client. No editing, no disclaimers. Would the boss read it and think "good work"? If they'd need to spend 20 minutes editing, fact-checking, or reformatting before hitting send, your product is creating work, not saving it.

Same Scenario, Different Evaluation

Eval DimensionB2C (Duolingo AI Tutor)B2B (Harvey AI Legal Research)
Primary failure modeTeaching wrong grammar that student can't detectCiting case law that doesn't exist
Accuracy thresholdMust be correct for core instruction; minor cultural notes can be approximateMust be 100% verifiable; zero tolerance for fabricated citations
Tone eval weightWeighted heavily — must feel like an encouraging, patient teacherWeighted lightly — must be professional, nothing more
Calibration style"I'm not 100% sure about this — let me check!" (friendly)"This interpretation rests on limited case law. Independent verification recommended." (precise)
"Fail" thresholdBelow 3/5 on safety OR below 3/5 on toneBelow 4/5 on accuracy OR any unsupported claim
User signal to monitorLesson abandonment rate, "report error" tapsEdit distance between AI output and final submitted work
The Core Insight
The same output quality might be a "pass" in B2C and a "fail" in B2B, or vice versa. A charming, slightly imprecise answer scores well in B2C (great tone, good enough accuracy) but fails in B2B (unacceptable imprecision). A dry, exhaustively-sourced answer scores well in B2B (bulletproof accuracy) but fails in B2C (boring, feels like reading a textbook). Your eval rubric must be designed for your user, not for "AI quality" in the abstract.
Do This Now
Identify whether your product is B2C or B2B and adjust your quality floor accordingly. If B2C, make sure safety and tone evaluations are weighted above accuracy. If B2B, make sure accuracy and reasoning evaluations are weighted above everything else.

Chapter 38The Eval Maturity Model

We introduced the eval maturity model in Chapter 03 — five levels from Flying Blind (Level 0) to Self-Improving (Level 4). Refer back to that chapter for the full breakdown of each level. Here is how to use it as a self-assessment after completing the guide:

Do This Now
Honestly assess your current level. Not where you aspire to be — where you actually are today. Then identify the one thing you need to do to reach the next level. Level 0 to 1 takes a day. Level 1 to 2 takes a week. Start now.

Chapter 39Communicating Evals to Stakeholders

Here's the hard truth: eval data is useless if your stakeholders don't understand it. You can build the most sophisticated eval pipeline in the world, but if the CEO's eyes glaze over when you present results, if engineers don't know what to fix, or if customers don't trust your quality claims — all that work is wasted. Communication is the last mile of the eval practice, and it's where most PMs fail.

Different audiences need fundamentally different information. Not just different levels of detail — different framing, different metrics, and different actions.

For Executives

What they care about: "Is the AI getting better or worse, and what's the business impact?"

What to include: One quality number with a trend direction, one risk metric, one cost metric. All tied to business outcomes they already care about. Keep it to 3 bullets maximum.

What to omit: Rubric details, per-dimension breakdowns, statistical methodology, specific test cases.

Executive Template
"Our AI quality is 4.1/5 this month, up from 3.8. The improvement came from [specific prompt change]. Our biggest risk is hallucination rate at 8% — we have a plan to reach 3% by [date]. Cost per query is $0.04, down 20% from switching to [model]."
Don't Do This

"Our ACCT framework scores improved across 3 of 4 dimensions. Accuracy went from 3.9 to 4.2, Completeness from 3.7 to 3.8, but Calibration dropped from 4.1 to 3.9. Our Cohen's Kappa with human raters is 0.78..."

You've lost the CEO after the first sentence.

Do This Instead

"AI quality up 8% this month. Hallucination risk dropping. Cost down 20%. Details in the appendix if you want them."

Three numbers. One trend. One risk. Done.

For Engineers

What they care about: "What exactly broke, where in the code did it break, and what should I do about it?"

What to include: The specific dimension that regressed, the specific commit or change that caused it, the specific test cases that failed, and a hypothesis about why. Engineers want actionable specificity — enough to open a PR, not just a vague direction.

Engineer Template
"Specificity dropped from 3.9 to 3.4 after commit abc123. Cases 3, 7, 12, 15, 19 regressed — all finance brands. The new system prompt seems to generalize hook language for this category. Reverting the finance-specific section should fix it."
Don't Do This

"Quality is trending down this week. Can you look into it?"

This gives the engineer nothing to work with. Which dimension? Which cases? Which change?

Do This Instead

Specific dimension, specific commit, specific test cases, specific hypothesis. They can open a PR in 10 minutes.

For Enterprise Customers

What they care about: "Can we trust this AI in our workflows? What's your quality process?"

What to include: The rigor of your evaluation process, the dimensions you measure, the frequency of testing, and hard numbers on accuracy and hallucination rate. Enterprise customers want process discipline — it signals that you take quality as seriously as they do.

Enterprise Customer Template
"We evaluate every output across 5 quality dimensions. Our benchmark suite covers 75 scenarios across 8 industry categories. Accuracy scores 4.3/5 with monthly human calibration audits. Hallucination rate is below 3%."
Don't Do This

"Our AI is really good — users love it!"

No numbers, no process, no rigor. Enterprise buyers hear "we don't measure quality."

Do This Instead

Numbers, process, frequency, methodology. This is what enterprise procurement needs to build a business case.

For Board / Investors

What they care about: "Is AI quality a competitive advantage or a liability? How does it affect unit economics?"

Board Deck Template
AI Quality Metrics — Q1 2026
• Output quality: 4.1/5 (up from 3.6 at launch, target 4.5 by EOY)
• Hallucination rate: 3.2% (industry average ~8-12%)
• Cost per AI query: $0.04 (down 40% YoY from model optimization)
• Regeneration rate: 9% (proxy for user satisfaction; was 28% at launch)
• Eval coverage: 75 test cases across 8 segments, automated weekly runs

The Weekly Product Review Ritual

The most effective way to communicate eval results is through a standing weekly review:

  1. Quality dashboard (2 min): Show the trend chart. Quality score, hallucination rate, regeneration rate — all trending over the last 8 weeks. Is the line going up or down?
  2. This week's failures (3 min): Show the 3 worst outputs from the week. Not to shame anyone — to build shared intuition about what "bad" looks like.
  3. What we shipped (2 min): Any prompt changes that went live. What did contrastive evals show? What was the win rate?
  4. What's next (1 min): Top 3 failure categories from error analysis. What are we fixing next week?

Total: 8 minutes. This single ritual creates a shared quality culture that no amount of documentation can replace.

Do This Now
Prepare your first weekly quality report. Pick the format that matches your primary audience (executive, engineering, or customer-facing) and populate it with real data from your eval runs. Then schedule a 15-minute slot on your team's calendar for next week.

Phase 11: Your Career

Everything you've learned is a competitive advantage in the job market. Here's how to leverage it.

Chapter 40Land the AI PM Job

The Opportunity
As noted earlier, the majority of AI products shipping today have no systematic quality measurement. The companies that hire AI PMs are specifically looking for people who can close this gap. If you can demonstrate eval expertise, you're competing against a very thin talent pool.

What Hiring Managers Look For

AI-native companies (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google) rarely use the word "evals" in postings. The eval work is embedded in phrases like "scalable methodologies for evaluating," "define success criteria," and "model performance benchmarks." If you can't read between these lines, you're already filtered out.

Signal phrases to look for in job postings:

  • "Define and track quality metrics for AI features" = they need someone to build an eval practice from scratch
  • "Partner with ML engineers on model evaluation" = they have engineers running evals but no PM owning the rubric
  • "Drive continuous improvement in model performance" = they want the eval flywheel
  • "Evaluate third-party AI vendors" = they need cross-model eval expertise
  • "Ensure AI safety and reliability" = they need someone who understands safety evals and red-teaming

Interview Questions You'll Face

  1. "Design an evaluation framework for [product]."
    • What they're testing: Can you go from vague product context to a specific, implementable eval plan? Do you start with error analysis or jump to metrics?
    • Great answer: Start by asking what failure looks like. Propose 3-4 specific eval dimensions (not generic "accuracy and helpfulness"). Describe test cases. Suggest a grading method for each dimension and justify why.
    • Red flag: "I'd measure accuracy, helpfulness, and user satisfaction." Too generic. Shows no understanding of domain-specific quality.
  2. "How would you know if your AI feature is getting worse?"
    • What they're testing: Do you understand the difference between pre-ship eval and post-ship monitoring? Do you know about drift?
    • Great answer: Production quality sampling on a percentage of outputs, implicit signals (regeneration rate, session abandonment), drift detection against baseline scores, automatic alerting when scores drop below thresholds.
  3. "You notice a 5% CSAT drop. Walk through your debugging process."
    • What they're testing: Do you jump to prompt changes, or trace failures systematically?
    • Great answer: Segment the data (which cohorts? which features?). Sample 30 low-CSAT outputs and run error analysis. Categorize failures. Check if recent changes correlate with timing. Only then propose a fix — and test it with a contrastive eval before shipping.
  4. "When would you use human eval vs. LLM-as-judge vs. code checks?"
    • What they're testing: Do you understand the cost-reliability tradeoff?
    • Great answer: Code checks for clear right answers (format, length, required fields). LLM judge for subjective quality at scale (tone, completeness, relevance). Human eval for high-stakes calibration and things LLM judges get wrong (domain expertise, nuance, safety edge cases).
  5. "Your team wants to ship a new prompt. How do you decide if it's ready?"
    • What they're testing: Do you have a quality gate process?
    • Great answer: Run a contrastive eval against the current prompt on at least 30 cases. Win rate must exceed 60%. No regression on any individual dimension below the quality floor. Check edge cases specifically.

The Knowledge-Doing Gap

Your Biggest Advantage
Most candidates who interview for AI PM roles have read about evals. They can talk about LLM-as-judge, rubrics, and error analysis in the abstract. Very few have actually done them. They haven't built a rubric, scored 50 outputs, set up a judge, computed agreement metrics, or iterated on a prompt based on eval data.

Having a documented eval project — even a small one, even on a personal project — puts you in a completely different league. It's the difference between "I would build an eval suite" and "I built an eval suite. Here's the rubric, here are the scores, here's how I improved quality from 3.2 to 4.1 over 4 weeks."

Resume Language That Signals Expertise

"Designed 75-case eval suite across 8 industry segments"
"Built LLM-as-judge pipeline achieving 0.85 Cohen's Kappa with human ratings"
"Reduced hallucination rate from 12% to 3% via eval-driven prompt iteration"
"Implemented production quality sampling on 10% of outputs"
"Led weekly eval review cadence across PM and engineering"

Portfolio Projects

  1. Eval an existing product. Pick any AI product (Perplexity, Copy.ai, Claude). Use it 50+ times across diverse inputs. Build an eval suite with a rubric (3-5 dimensions), 20+ test cases, and a scoring system. Run the evals, compute summary statistics, identify the top 3 failure categories. Publish your methodology and findings.
  2. Build a simple AI app + eval it. Build a small RAG app or chatbot with the Claude API. Write evals before you build (Eval-Driven Development). Show the iteration story: prompt v1 scored 3.2, v2 scored 3.8, v3 scored 4.3. The improvement arc is what impresses — it proves you can use eval data to make real product decisions.
  3. Contribute to an open-source eval framework. Add test cases or custom scorers to promptfoo or DeepEval. Publishing a contribution shows practical capability, not just theory.

The Case Study Format for Interviews

1. CONTEXT:    What product, what AI feature, what problem
2. ERROR ANALYSIS: I reviewed 50 outputs. 35% failed on X, 20% on Y.
3. EVAL DESIGN:   3 dimensions, 25 test cases, pass/fail + LLM judge
4. EXECUTION:    Ran weekly for 4 weeks, iterated prompts
5. RESULTS:     Quality: 3.2 → 4.1. Hallucination: 12% → 3%. Cost: -20%.
6. IMPACT:      Regeneration rate dropped from 28% to 9%.
Do This Now
Build one portfolio project using the skills from this document. It doesn't need to be perfect. It doesn't need to be comprehensive. It needs to exist. A documented project — rubric, test cases, scores, iteration story — is more powerful in an interview than any amount of theoretical knowledge.

Chapter 41The 10 Commandments

These are the principles that underpin everything in this document. When you're unsure what to do next, return here.

1
Look at your data first. Build evals second.
The majority of eval development time should be error analysis, not infrastructure. — Husain
If you violate this: You'll build an eval suite that tests for the wrong things. Your rubric will measure what you imagine goes wrong instead of what actually goes wrong.
2
You own the rubric. Engineers own the infrastructure.
Don't delegate "what good looks like." They'll optimize for what's measurable, not what matters.
If you violate this: Engineers will define quality in terms of what's easy to code — string matching, format checks, response time. Your product will score 95% on structural evals while producing mediocre content that users hate.
3
Start with 20 test cases, not 200.
Well-chosen cases from real failures beat large collections of artificial examples. — Anthropic
If you violate this: You'll spend weeks creating synthetic test cases that don't represent real user behavior, giving you a false sense of coverage while missing the failures that actually matter.
4
Prefer code checks over LLM judges.
Anthropic's hierarchy: code-based (cheapest, most reliable) > LLM-based > human (gold standard). Use the simplest that works.
If you violate this: You'll use an expensive, non-deterministic LLM judge to check things a 3-line regex can verify perfectly every time.
5
Run each test case multiple times.
AI is non-deterministic. A single run tells you what CAN happen. Multiple runs tell you what WILL happen.
If you violate this: You'll ship a prompt that passed on a lucky run. In production, it fails 30% of the time. Your users experience inconsistency your single-run eval never caught.
6
Never use the same model as generator and judge.
Self-enhancement bias: LLMs rate their own outputs higher. Use a different (ideally stronger) model for judging.
If you violate this: Your scores will be inflated by 0.3-0.6 points. You'll miss regressions because the judge gives its own model's mistakes a pass.
7
Report distributions, not just averages.
Average 4.0 with two catastrophic 1s is worse than consistent 3.5. Your users feel the variance, not the mean.
If you violate this: Catastrophic failures hide in your averages. A 4.0 average with 10% score-1 outputs means 1 in 10 users has a terrible experience.
8
Check statistical significance before celebrating.
A 0.3-point improvement on 15 test cases might be noise. Use confidence intervals. Run more cases if uncertain.
If you violate this: You'll ship "improvements" that were just random variance. Next week, the scores swing back. You'll waste engineering cycles chasing phantom gains.
9
Add every production failure to your test suite.
Regression cases are your "never again" insurance. They compound into a quality moat over time.
If you violate this: The same failure will recur. Each prompt fix will re-introduce a bug you fixed three months ago. Without regression cases, you're playing whack-a-mole.
10
Calibrate your judge quarterly.
Grade 30 outputs yourself. Have your judge grade them. If Cohen's Kappa drops below 0.6, rewrite your judge prompt.
If you violate this: Your judge will drift out of calibration as your product evolves, silently corrupting every eval score. You'll make decisions based on numbers that no longer reflect reality.

Appendices

Reference material you'll come back to. Math, domain patterns, specialized eval approaches, and glossary.

Appendix AThe Math You Need

Every concept is a story first, then the formula. No symbol will appear without you understanding why it's there.

1. Mean, Median, and Why Averages Lie

The story: 10 restaurants on a street. 9 rated 4.5 stars. One rated 1 star (food poisoning). The average rating: 4.15 stars. A tourist sees "4.15 — great street!" and eats at the food poisoning place. The average lied.

Real Example — Notion AI Summarization
You run 20 summarization evals. Scores:
[5, 5, 4, 5, 4, 5, 5, 4, 5, 1, 5, 4, 5, 5, 4, 5, 5, 4, 5, 1]

Mean: 4.3 — "Looks great!"
Median: 5 — "Even better!"
But: 2 out of 20 (10%) scored 1 — catastrophic failures hiding in the average.

Always report: mean, min, max, and % below your quality floor.

2. Standard Deviation: "How Spread Out Are My Scores?"

The story: Two pizza delivery services both deliver in "30 minutes on average." Service A always takes 28–32 minutes. Service B sometimes takes 15 minutes, sometimes 45. Same average, very different reliability. Standard deviation measures this spread.

Step by Step
Scores: 3, 4, 5, 4, 4

Step 1: Average = (3+4+5+4+4) ÷ 5 = 4.0
Step 2: Distance from average: −1, 0, +1, 0, 0
Step 3: Square each (to make all positive): 1, 0, 1, 0, 0
Step 4: Average of squares: 2 ÷ 5 = 0.4 (this is the variance)
Step 5: Square root (to undo the squaring): √0.4 = 0.63

Standard deviation = 0.63. "On average, each score is about 0.63 points from the mean."
What the Numbers Mean
SD < 0.5: Very consistent. Scores barely vary.
SD 0.5 – 1.0: Normal variation. Typical for most eval suites.
SD > 1.5: Wildly inconsistent. Fix this before anything else.

3. Confidence Intervals: "Is This Change Real or Just Luck?"

The intuition: You want to know the average height of people in your city. You measure 30 random people and get 5'8". But a different 30 people might give you 5'7.5" or 5'8.5". The confidence interval says: "The real average is probably between 5'7" and 5'9"."

Standard Error — Why It Exists
The Standard Error answers: "If I ran this eval again with different test cases, how much would the average change?"

SE = Standard Deviation ÷ √(number of test cases)

Why divide by the square root? Think of asking people about a restaurant. You ask 1 person — unreliable. 4 people — better. 16 people — pretty confident. 64 people — only a little more confident than 16. That's the square root pattern: to double your confidence, you need 4x the cases.
The Confidence Interval Formula
95% CI = Average ± (1.96 × SE)

Where 1.96 is a constant for 95% confidence. For evals, always use 95%, so always use 1.96.

The rule: If the confidence intervals of two versions don't overlap, the difference is real. If they overlap, it might be noise. When in doubt, run more test cases.
Real Example — Copy.ai Prompt Change
Old prompt (30 cases): avg 3.8, SD 0.9, SE 0.16, CI [3.48 to 4.12]
New prompt (30 cases): avg 4.1, SD 0.8, SE 0.15, CI [3.81 to 4.39]

The ranges overlap between 3.81 and 4.12. The improvement might be noise. Run 60 cases or make a bigger improvement.

4. Cohen's Kappa: "Does My LLM Judge Agree With Me?"

The intuition: A coin that's PASS on both sides will produce 100% "agreement" between two people flipping it — but that's meaningless. Cohen's Kappa subtracts out the agreement you'd expect from random chance.

Worked Example
You and your LLM judge grade 20 outputs:

Judge: PASSJudge: FAILTotal
You: PASS14216
You: FAIL134
Total15520
Actual agreement: (14+3)/20 = 85%
Expected by chance: (0.80 × 0.75) + (0.20 × 0.25) = 65%
κ = (0.85 − 0.65) ÷ (1.00 − 0.65) = 0.57 (moderate agreement)
Interpreting Kappa
κ > 0.8: Strong. Your judge is reliable. Trust it.
κ 0.6–0.8: Moderate. Refine the judge prompt based on the specific disagreements.
κ 0.4–0.6: Weak. Rewrite the rubric — it's probably ambiguous.
κ < 0.4: Basically random. Start from scratch.

5. pass@k and pass^k

What Does the "k" Mean?
k = the number of attempts. Not Cohen's kappa, not a constant — just how many times you run the same test.

pass@k = did it succeed at least once out of k tries? (the @ means "at least one")
pass^k = did it succeed every single time out of k tries? (the ^ means "all")

Quick example (k=5, 3 out of 5 correct):
• pass@5 = 100% — yes, it got it right at least once
• pass^5 = 0% — no, it did not get all 5 right

When to use which:

  • pass@k → "At least once" → Code generation, creative brainstorming, any task where you pick the best output
  • pass^k → "Every time" → Customer support, medical info, education, anything where users see each output directly

6. Sample Size

Practical Guidelines
Looking for big differences (0.5+ points): 15–20 test cases is enough.
Looking for small differences (0.2–0.3 points): Need 40–50 test cases.
High-variance scores (SD > 1.5): Need 60+ cases.

Rule of thumb: Start with 20–30. If your confidence intervals overlap, add more.

7. Win Rate

The easiest comparison method. Show two outputs side by side. Ask "which is better?" Count the wins.

Test CasesWins Needed (95% CI)Win Rate Threshold
2015+75%
3020+67%
4025+63%
6037+62%
10060+60%

If your wins exceed the threshold, the difference is real. If not, you need more test cases.

Appendix CRAG Evaluation

70% of enterprise AI products use RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation). If you're evaluating any product that searches documents then generates answers, this appendix is your reference.

The Two-Layer Problem

A bad answer can come from two places: bad retrieval (didn't find the right documents) or bad generation (found the right docs but misinterpreted them). You must eval both layers separately.

User question
→
Retrieval (find docs)
→
Generation (write answer)
→
Answer

Retrieval Metrics

MetricPlain EnglishExample
Context PrecisionOf the docs we retrieved, how many were actually relevant?Retrieved 10 docs, 7 were relevant = 70% precision
Context RecallOf all the relevant docs that exist, how many did we find?15 relevant docs exist, we found 7 = 47% recall

Generation Metrics

MetricPlain EnglishExample
FaithfulnessDoes the answer ONLY use info from retrieved docs? (No hallucination)Answer says "founded in 2018" but no retrieved doc mentions this = unfaithful
Answer RelevancyDoes the answer actually address the question?Question about pricing, answer talks about features = irrelevant
Real Example — Zendesk AI Support
Customer asks: "How do I cancel my subscription?"

Bad retrieval: System retrieves docs about "subscription plans" and "billing FAQ" but misses "cancellation policy." AI hallucinates a process. → Fix retrieval.

Bad generation: System retrieves the right doc but AI says "cancel anytime" when the doc says "30-day notice required." → Fix generation prompt.

Without two-layer eval, you'd just see "wrong answer" and not know which layer to fix.

Appendix DHallucination Detection

If you prepare for one eval topic before an AI PM interview, make it this one. Hallucination detection comes up in nearly every interview because it sits at the intersection of user trust, product quality, and technical understanding.

The Five Detection Techniques

1. Fact Decomposition

Break the output into individual, atomic claims. Verify each claim separately. Calculate the ratio of accurate claims to total claims. This is the most reliable technique because it leaves no hiding places for hallucinations buried in fluent-sounding paragraphs.

2. Self-Consistency

Ask the same question 3–5 times (temperature > 0). Compare answers. If the AI gives different facts each time, it's uncertain and may be hallucinating. Catches inconsistent hallucinations but misses consistent ones.

3. Citation Verification

When the AI cites a source, follow the citation. Three failure modes: (a) the citation doesn't exist, (b) it exists but says something different, (c) it exists but doesn't actually support the specific claim. Mode (c) is the sneakiest.

4. Cross-Reference

Compare claims against a verified knowledge base or ground truth document. The gold standard when ground truth exists. Automate by extracting claims and running them against your docs using semantic search.

5. Entailment Scoring

Given a "premise" (source document) and "hypothesis" (AI output), does the premise logically support the hypothesis? An NLI model assigns: entailment, contradiction, or neutral. For summarization and doc Q&A, anything that's not "entailment" is potentially a hallucination.

TechniqueBest ForCatchesMisses
Fact DecompositionAny factual outputAll verifiable errorsSubjective claims
Self-ConsistencyQuick uncertainty detectionInconsistent hallucinationsConsistent hallucinations
Citation VerificationRAG, search productsFabricated/misused sourcesUncited claims
Cross-ReferenceWhen ground truth existsContradictions with known factsNovel/open-domain topics
Entailment ScoringSummarization, doc Q&AUnsupported additionsSubtle misinterpretations
The Interview Angle
Interviewers test three things: (1) Do you know the techniques exist? (2) Can you pick the right one for a given product? (3) Do you understand the tradeoffs? The strongest answer combines techniques: self-consistency as a fast first pass, then fact decomposition on anything flagged as uncertain. Layered detection is always better than any single technique.

Appendix EMulti-Modal Evaluation

Not all AI outputs are text. If your product generates images, audio, or video, you need different eval approaches.

Image Generation (Midjourney, DALL-E, fal.ai)

MetricWhat It MeasuresMethod
Prompt adherenceDoes the image match what was asked for?LLM vision judge: "Does this image show [prompt]?"
Aesthetic qualityDoes it look good?Human rating or CLIP aesthetic predictor
Brand consistencyColors, style, tone match the brand?LLM vision judge with brand guidelines as context
Text renderingIs any text in the image spelled correctly?OCR + string comparison
SafetyNo NSFW, violent, or biased content?Safety classifier (NSFW detector)
DistinctivenessDo multiple outputs look sufficiently different?CLIP embedding distance between pairs
Real Example — Ad Campaign Agent
An agent generates 6 ad images per campaign. Multi-modal evals needed:
• Prompt adherence: Does image 3 actually show a "clay diorama style product shot"?
• Brand colors: Are the hex colors from the research brief visible in the image?
• Entity ID diversity: Do the 6 images differ on 3+ visual signal axes?
• Mobile safe zone: Is important visual content in the bottom 35%?

For prompt adherence and brand checks, use Claude's vision capabilities as the judge — send the image + prompt and ask "does this match?"

Audio/Voice (ElevenLabs, Spotify DJ voice, voice assistants)

  • Intelligibility: Can you understand what was said? (speech-to-text comparison)
  • Naturalness: Does it sound human? (MOS score — Mean Opinion Score from human raters)
  • Consistency: Same voice across outputs? (speaker embedding similarity)
  • Prosody: Correct emphasis, pausing, intonation? (human eval mostly)

Video (Runway, Sora, Kling)

  • Temporal coherence: Do objects stay consistent frame-to-frame?
  • Motion quality: Are movements natural, not glitchy?
  • Prompt alignment: Does the video show what was described?
  • FVD (Frechet Video Distance): Automated quality metric

Key point for PMs: Multi-modal eval is less mature than text eval. Human review is still necessary for most quality dimensions. Automated metrics (CLIP, FID, FVD) are useful for detecting regressions but don't replace human judgment on aesthetics and brand fit.

Appendix FGlossary & Quick Reference

Ablation Study
Remove one component, measure the quality impact. Tells you what's actually driving performance.
Axial Coding
Grouping open-code labels into higher-level categories. Phase 2 of error analysis.
Benchmark
A standardized eval suite used to compare models. HumanEval (code), MMLU (knowledge), Chatbot Arena (overall).
CLIP Score
Measures how well an image matches a text description. Used for image generation eval.
Cohen's Kappa (κ)
Agreement between two raters beyond chance. >0.8 = strong, 0.6–0.8 = moderate, <0.6 = weak.
COMET
Neural machine translation quality metric. Correlates better with human judgment than older metrics like BLEU.
Confidence Interval
Range around a score where the "true" score likely falls. 95% CI means you're 95% confident the real score is within that range.
Contamination
When the model has seen test questions during training, inflating scores. Like a student having the answer key.
Context Window
Maximum tokens an LLM can process at once. System prompt + conversation + response must all fit.
Contrastive Eval
Compare two outputs head-to-head instead of scoring each independently. More reliable than absolute scoring.
Elo Rating
Ranking system from head-to-head comparisons (like chess). Chatbot Arena uses this to rank models.
Entailment
Logical relationship: does statement A follow from statement B? Used in hallucination detection.
Eval-Driven Development (EDD)
Write eval test cases first, then build the feature. TDD for AI.
Faithfulness
RAG metric: does the answer only use info from retrieved docs? No hallucinated additions.
FID (Frechet Inception Distance)
Image quality metric: compares distribution of generated images to real images. Lower = better.
Ground Truth
The known-correct answer for a test case. The answer key your eval checks against.
Hallucination
When the AI confidently states something that isn't true. The #1 concern in AI product quality.
Inter-Rater Agreement
How much different judges (human or LLM) agree. Measured by Cohen's Kappa or correlation.
LLM-as-Judge
Using an LLM to grade another LLM's output. Scalable but needs calibration against human judgment.
Meta-Eval
Evaluating your eval system itself. "Does my LLM judge agree with human experts?"
MOS (Mean Opinion Score)
Audio quality metric from human raters. Scale 1–5. Used for voice AI evaluation.
N-shot
How many examples shown to the model. Zero-shot = none. Few-shot = 2–5 examples.
nDCG
Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain. Search metric: are the best results ranked highest?
NLI (Natural Language Inference)
Does statement A entail, contradict, or is neutral to statement B? Used in hallucination detection.
Open Coding
First pass of error analysis: label each failure with a description. No predefined categories.
OWASP Agentic Top 10
The 10 most critical security risks for AI agents. Published Dec 2025 by 100+ security researchers.
pass@k
Probability of at least 1 success in k attempts. Use when one success is enough (code gen).
pass^k
Probability of ALL k attempts succeeding. Use when consistency matters (customer support).
Precision@K
Of the top K results shown, how many are relevant? Search/recommendation metric.
Prompt Injection
Adversarial input that overrides the system prompt. The #1 security risk for agents with tool access.
RAG
Retrieval-Augmented Generation: search for docs first, then generate answer using those docs.
Recall@K
Of all relevant items that exist, how many appear in the top K? Search metric.
Red-Teaming
Deliberately trying to break your AI with adversarial inputs. Essential for safety evaluation.
Regression
Quality got worse after a change. The eval equivalent of "you broke it."
ROUGE
Text overlap metric for summarization. Measures word overlap between generated and reference summary.
Rubric
Criteria for judging AI output. Defines dimensions, score levels, and examples for each level.
SEM (Standard Error of Mean)
How much your average score would vary if you re-ran the eval. Smaller = more reliable.
System Prompt
Hidden instructions to the AI that the user never sees. Defines behavior, rules, persona.
Temperature
Controls randomness (0–1). Low = deterministic. High = creative/variable. Affects eval reproducibility.
Token
The unit LLMs process text in. ~4 characters or ~3/4 of a word. Pricing and limits are in tokens.
Trajectory Eval
Evaluating the sequence of steps an agent took, not just the final output. Were the right tools called in a reasonable order?
Win Rate
In A/B comparison, how often does version A beat version B? >60% on 40+ cases is usually significant.

You started with 5 questions: Is my AI good? Is it getting better? Where does it break? Can I trust these scores? Am I ready to ship?

You now have the tools, frameworks, and thinking patterns to answer them for any agentic product. You know how to observe failures, define quality, design rubrics, automate judges, run evals in CI/CD, monitor production, protect your eval system from bias and contamination, evaluate agent safety, scale with human-in-the-loop workflows, and communicate results to every stakeholder.

The eval practice never ends — but you're no longer starting from zero.
